Multiplicative equivariant K K -theory and the Barratt-Priddy-Quillen theorem Thanks: B. J. Guillou was partially supported by Simons Collaboration Grant No. 282316 and NSF grants DMS-1710379 and DMS-2003204. M. Merling was partially supported by NSF grant DMS-1709461/1850644, a Simons AMS travel grant, and NSF CAREER grant DMS-1943925. A. M. Osorno was partially supported by the Simons Collaboration Grant No. 359449, the Woodrow Wilson Career Enhancement Fellowship, and NSF grant DMS-1709302.
Bertrand J. Guillou, J. Peter May, Mona Merling, Angélica M. Osorno

Abstract
. We prove a multiplicative version of the equivariant Barratt-Priddy-Quillen theorem, starting from the additive version proven in [ 13 ] . The proof uses a multiplicative elaboration of an additive equivariant infinite loop space machine that manufactures orthogonal G G -spectra from symmetric monoidal G G -categories. The new machine produces highly structured associative ring and module G G -spectra from appropriate multiplicative input. It relies on new operadic multicategories that are of considerable independent interest and are defined in a general, not necessarily equivariant or topological, context. Most of our work is focused on constructing and comparing them. We construct a multifunctor from the multicategory of symmetric monoidal G G -categories to the multicategory of orthogonal G G -spectra. With this machinery in place, we prove that the equivariant BPQ theorem can be lifted to a multiplicative equivalence. That is the heart of what is needed for the presheaf reconstruction of the category of G G -spectra in [ 12 ] .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 19D23, 19L47, 55P48; Secondary 18D20, 18D40, 18M65, 55P91, 55U40

1.
Introduction

We can view algebraic
K
-theory as a machine that takes as input a category with a structured additive operation and produces a spectrum by
group-completing

the operation in a homotopy coherent way. The homotopy groups of this spectrum—the higher
K
-groups—are rich invariants which connect homotopy theory with number theory, algebraic geometry, and geometric topology. For example, the homotopy groups of the
K
-theory spectrum of the category of finitely generated projective
R
-modules for a ring
R
are Quillen’s higher
K
-groups of
R
, which are related to important problems and conjectures in number theory, especially when
R
is a number ring.

Classically, there were two approaches for building the
K
-theory spectrum associated to a symmetric monoidal category: Segal’s approach based on
\Gamma
-spaces
[
47
]
, and the operadic approach of
[
2
,
29
,
30
]
. These two infinite loop space machines were shown to be equivalent in
[
37
,
31
]
. One fundamental problem in infinite loop space theory is to determine what structure on the input category ensures that its
K
-theory spectrum is a highly structured ring spectrum. If the input has a second, related, structured multiplicative operation, making it into a “ring category”, then a suitably multiplicative
K
-theory machine should yield a ring spectrum. The study of multiplicative infinite loop space theory saw much development early on
[
32
,
33
,
38
,
50
,
51
]
. A space level modernized survey is given in
[
35
]
and a modernized categorical treatment is given in
[
34
]
. A treatment of multiplicative infinite loop space theory that is structured around the use of multicategories and multifunctors is given in
[
8
]
, and that has served for inspiration in this paper.

For a finite group
G
, the Segal infinite loop space machine has been generalized equivariantly by Shimakawa in
[
48
]
, and the operadic infinite loop space machine has been generalized equivariantly by two of us in
[
13
]
to build (genuine) orthogonal
G
-spectra from categories with additive operations that are suitably equivariant.
1
1

1

A much earlier operadic machine with target Lewis-May
G
-spectra
[
25
]
was developed by Hauschild, May, and Waner. It was never published, but is outlined by Costenoble and Waner
[
6
]
.
These equivariant infinite loop space machines have been shown to be equivalent by three of us in
[
39
]
.

It is a natural question to ask what kind of structure on a
G
-category makes its
K
-theory into an equivariant ring spectrum, and this is not addressed in any of the papers just mentioned. Nonequivariantly, the question can be answered without serious use of
2
-category theory, but we have not found such an answer equivariantly.
The multiplicative structure at the categorical level is encoded via multilinear maps that are distributive up to coherent natural isomorphisms and is thus intrinsically
2
-categorical. The very different but essentially combinatorial ways around this found nonequivariantly in
[
8
,
34
]
do not appear to generalize equivariantly, or at least not easily. Our work involves conceptual categorical processing of
2
-categorical input so that it feeds into an equivariant version of the
1
-categorical Segal machine, whose multiplicative properties we have established in
[
15
]
.

An equivariant version of the Barratt-Priddy-Quillen theorem, which expresses the suspension
G
-spectrum of a
G
-space as the equivariant algebraic
K
-theory of a
G
-category, was proven in
[
13
]
using the equivariant operadic machine. However, this equivalence does not a priori preserve the multiplicative structure coming from the smash product of based
G
-spaces.
The main result of
[
12
]
relies on having a multiplicative equivariant
K
-theory machine starting at the level of
G
-categories that is compatible with the Barratt-Priddy-Quillen theorem, and we provide that in this paper. An easier multiplicative version of the equivariant Barratt-Priddy-Quillen theorem is proven in
[
15
, Theorem 6.7]
), but that starts from categorical input that is quite different from the input needed in
[
12
]
.

We start with an equivariant
K
-theory machine
\mathbb{K}_{G}
producing orthogonal
G
-spectra from structured
G
-categories, which we take to be algebras over a suitable operad
{\mathcal{O}}
.
In the nonequivariant case, the input would be permutative categories, which are algebras over the Barratt-Eccles operad.
Conceptually, we would like to extend
\mathbb{K}_{G}
to a monoidal functor from structured
G
-categories to orthogonal
G
-spectra.
However, the ring
G
-categories that arise in nature are not the monoids for a monoidal structure on structured
G
-categories.
Rather, as in
[
8
,
24
]
and elsewhere, we have a multicategory structure on structured
G
-categories.
A multicategory structure on a category
{\eus{C}}
allows one to make sense of the notion of monoid in
{\eus{C}}
as well as module over a monoid.
We will thus extend
\mathbb{K}_{G}
to a multifunctor, meaning that it is compatible with the multicategory structure.

We give some intuition for finding the structure on an operad that ensures that its category of algebras is a multicategory. We think of the operad
{\mathcal{O}}
as parametrizing addition. Now suppose that we want to define a multiplication that distributes over addition. Just as the product of integers
mn
is the
n
-fold addition of the integer
m
, we can define a pairing
{\mathcal{O}}(m)\times{\mathcal{O}}(n)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(mn)
by repeating
n
times the variable in
{\mathcal{O}}(m)
and then “adding” using the operad structure map. The diagram that we obtain when we compare this with the map
{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{O}}(m)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(nm)
that we get by twisting in the source and using a reordering permutation in the target does not strictly commute in general. We define a
pseudo-commutative
operad to be one for which this comparison diagram commutes up to natural isomorphism (see
Definition 3.10
), and we show that this condition allows us to define a multicategory structure on the category of
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras. A key example is the permutativity operad
\mathcal{P}_{G}
of
[
16
, Definition 3.4]
; its algebras are the permutative
G
-categories and its pseudoalgebras are the symmetric monoidal
G
-categories
[
16
]
. The operad
\mathcal{P}_{G}
is a categorical
E_{\infty}

G
-operad as defined in
[
13
, Definition 2.1]
, and when
G=e
it is just the categorical
E_{\infty}
Barratt-Eccles operad.

We write
G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}
for the category of
G
-spaces and
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}})
for the 2-category of categories internal to
G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}
, as described in
Section 2.1
.
Fix a chaotic (
Definition 2.5
)

E_{\infty}

G
-operad
{\mathcal{O}}
in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}})
. We construct a multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})
whose underlying category is the category
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras and pseudomorphisms.
Writing
\mathbf{Sp}_{G}
for the category of orthogonal
G
-spectra, we construct a functor

\mathbb{K}_{G}\colon{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Sp}_{G}

that group completes the additive structure,
and most of the paper is devoted to establishing the following result, which appears as
Theorem 9.13
.

Theorem A
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a chaotic
E_{\infty}

G
-operad in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}})
.
Then the functor
\mathbb{K}_{G}\colon{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Sp}_{G}
extends to a multifunctor.

We have the following direct corollary of
Theorem A
.

Corollary
.

If
{\mathcal{A}}
is a monoid
in
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}
, then
\mathbb{K}_{G}({\mathcal{A}})
is a ring
G
-spectrum. If
{\mathcal{B}}
is an
{\mathcal{A}}
-module in
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}
, then
\mathbb{K}_{G}({\mathcal{B}})
is a
\mathbb{K}_{G}({\mathcal{A}})
-module
G
-spectrum.

We warn the reader, however, that
Theorem A
does not assert that
\mathbb{K}_{G}
is
symmetric
. In particular, we do not claim that our version of
\mathbb{K}_{G}
produces commutative ring
G
-spectra as output. Constructing a symmetric equivariant
\mathbb{K}
-theory multifunctor is an ongoing challenge. Our multifunctor
\mathbb{K}_{G}
is a composite of multifunctors all but one of which are symmetric, and we shall keep track of symmetry as we go along. However, associative and unital multiplicative properties are all that are needed for the following result, which is the heart of what is needed in
[
12
]
.
We prove the following theorem in
Section 10
. Here we use that
G\mathcal{U}
embeds in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}})
, as recalled from
[
16
, Remark 1.8]
in
Remark 9.20
.

Theorem B

(Multiplicative equivariant Barratt-Priddy-Quillen)
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a topologically discrete chaotic
E_{\infty}

G
-operad in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}})
and
\mathbb{O}_{+}
the associated monad.
There is a lax monoidal natural transformation

\alpha\colon\Sigma^{\infty}_{G+}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{K}_{G}\mathbb{O}_{+}

of functors
G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Sp}_{G}
such that
\alpha_{X}
is a stable equivalence of orthogonal
G
-spectra for all input

G
-CW complexes
X
.

The main result of
[
12
]
gives a Quillen equivalence between the category of orthogonal
G
-spectra and the category of “spectral Mackey functors,” i.e., spectrally enriched functors
G\mathscr{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Sp}
, where
G\mathscr{A}
is a spectral version of the Burnside category. The proof of that result rests on having a multiplicative machine
\mathbb{K}_{G}
which satisfies
Theorem B
. Its construction was deferred to this paper. An alternative
\infty
-categorical perspective on spectral Mackey functors as a model for
G
-spectra is given in
[
1
,
4
,
41
]
.
Moreover, a version of the multiplicative equivariant Barratt-Priddy-Quillen theorem appears as
[
1
, Theorem 10.6]
. As the input for their machine differs from that of ours, a direct comparison of their result with ours would be nontrivial but worthwhile.

Remark 1.1
.

An illuminating
\infty
-category treatment of multiplicative infinite loop space theory is given in
[
11
]
. We briefly compare that approach to the theory here. The input with that approach is symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories, which are
\infty
-categorical generalizations of Segal’s special
\Gamma
-spaces. In this paper, as classically, the input is symmetric monoidal
1
-categories, but we need to work with the 2-category of such, in order to keep track of the multiplicative structure.
The focus of this paper is the passage from there to special
\Gamma
-categories, while the machine
\mathbb{S}_{G}\circ B
from special
\Gamma
-categories to
\Omega
-
G
-spectra is taken as a black box. We view the machine
\mathbb{S}_{G}\circ B
as essentially formal. Like the
\infty
-category machine, it is symmetric monoidal, at least in the variant form given in
[
15
]
. Philosophically, from the
\infty
-category point of view, we are showing that, even equivariantly, the passage from symmetric monoidal 1-categories to symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories preserves multiplicative structure, albeit with a loss of symmetry.

1.1.
A road map

The organization of this paper focuses on the multiplicative elaboration of the following diagram, which displays
\mathbb{K}_{G}
as the composite of a sequence of functors.

(1.2)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{K}_{G}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sp}_{G}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}\text{-}\bf{Alg_{ps}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf Top}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{S}_{G}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}_{ps}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{G}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf PsAlg}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{St}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B}

The notation
{\bf Alg_{ps}}
denotes
2
-categories of
strict
algebras and
pseudomorphisms
between them. Modulo just a bit of additional notational complexity, we can just as well replace the
2
-categories (-)-
{\bf Alg_{ps}}
in the left hand column by more general
2
-categories (-)-
\bf{PsAlg}
of pseudoalgebras and pseudomorphisms, with no significant change in constructions or proofs. We shall often write
\mathbb{R}_{G}
for the composite
\mathbb{P}\mathbb{R}
in the left column.

After a few preliminaries setting up our categorical framework of operads and multicategories in
Section 2
, multicategories with underlying categories of the form
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}
are defined in
Section 3
. Here
{\mathcal{O}}
is a chaotic operad or, a bit more generally, a pseudo-commutative operad. We develop a general categorical framework that will specialize to an understanding of categories of operators over both finite sets and finite
G
-sets, together with their algebras and pseudoalgebras, in
Section 4
. We do this in a general framework that will later clarify some key distinctions. Multicategories with underlying categories of the form
{\mathscr{D}}\text{-}\bf{Alg_{ps}}
are defined in
Section 5
, where
{\mathscr{D}}
is a category of operators over the category
\mathscr{F}
of finite sets. Categories of operators were first introduced in
[
37
]
, where they mediated between the operadic and Segalic infinite loop space machines. They play the same role here.

Taking
{\mathscr{D}}
to be the category of operators associated to a chaotic operad
{\mathcal{O}}
, the functor
\mathbb{R}
is constructed as a multifunctor in
Section 5.5
, but with a key proof deferred to
Section 12
. All of this works in a general categorical context that a priori has nothing to do with either equivariance or topology. A crucial technical point is that the “pseudo-commutative pairing” on an operad that we have already mentioned gives rise to an analogous “pseudo-commutative pairing” on its category of operators. The term “pseudo-commutative” was first coined by Hyland and Power
[
18
]
in a monadic avatar of our categories of operators; it can be viewed as shorthand for “pseudo-symmetric strict monoidal”.

Working equivariantly, but in fact in a specialization of our general categorical framework, multicategories with underlying categories of the form
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}_{ps}
, where
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
is a category of operators over the category
\mathscr{F}_{G}
of finite
G
-sets, are defined in
Section 6
. Taking
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
to be the category of operators associated to a chaotic operad
{\mathcal{O}}
, the multifunctor
\mathbb{P}
is also defined in that section.

The categories of operators
{\mathscr{D}}
and
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
come with projections
\xi\colon{\mathscr{D}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}
and
\xi_{G}\colon{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}_{G}
.
Pulling back structure along these projections gives functors
\xi^{*}
and
\xi_{G}^{*}
that send
\mathscr{F}
-algebras to
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras and
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-algebras to
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras, and similarly for pseudoalgebras. Taking full advantage of the equivariant context, we construct a section
\zeta_{G}\colon\mathscr{F}_{G}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
to
\xi_{G}
in

Section 7
. Pulling back along
\zeta_{G}
gives the functor
\zeta_{G}^{*}
.

However, since
\zeta_{G}
does not preserve structure as strictly as one might hope,
\zeta_{G}^{*}
takes strict algebras to pseudoalgebras. As we explain in
Section 8
,
\mathrm{St}
is a specialization of a general strictification functor due to Power and Lack
[
42
,
20
]
that rectifies the loss of strictness and lands us in the multicategory associated to the symmetric monoidal
2
-category
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
of strict
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-algebras in categories internal to
G
-spaces and strict maps between them. Specializing general theory developed in
[
16
,
14
]
, we explain in
Section 8.2
how it extends to a multifunctor.

From here,
B
is the standard classifying space functor and
\mathbb{S}_{G}
is the space level multiplicative equivariant infinite loop space machine of
[
39
]
;
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf Top}
is the category of
\mathscr{F}
-
G
-spaces and
\mathbf{Sp}_{G}
is the category of orthogonal
G
-spectra. We use these functors to complete the proof of
Theorem A
in
Section 9
, and we combine our results here with results of
[
39
]
and
[
13
]
to prove
Theorem B
in
Section 10
.

All of the multifunctors in
(1.2)
are symmetric except
\mathrm{St}
and
\mathbb{S}_{G}
. We could equally well have used the slightly more elaborate but equivalent choice for
\mathbb{S}_{G}
constructed in
[
15
]
, which is symmetric. However, although
\zeta_{G}^{*}
is itself symmetric, loss of strict structure along it engenders the loss of symmetry of
\mathrm{St}
, as we shall explain in
Section 8.3
.

Remark 1.3
.

We alert the reader to an alternative route to Theorems
A
and
B
that was found at the same time as the one presented here. It will be presented in
[
28
]
. It is illuminating, but it is more categorically intensive since it focuses on
2
-monads, which we have avoided here despite this being a paper that is intrinsically all about them. We will see in
[
28
]
that the
k
-ary morphisms in our operadic multicategories are the pseudoalgebras over a
2
-monad
M_{k}
and that the
M_{k}
form a graded comonoid of
2
-monads. Such structure also appears in other multicategorical contexts.

The alternative route uses a
2
-monadic reinterpretation of the vertical arrows in
(1.2)
, but it replaces the
horizontal composite
\mathrm{St}\circ\zeta_{G}^{*}
by a multifunctor whose underlying map of
2
-categories is the composite of Power-Lack strictification
\mathrm{St}\colon{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}_{ps}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}
and a derived variant of the left adjoint

\xi^{G}_{*}\colon{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
to the forgetful functor
\xi_{G}^{*}\colon\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}
. The section

\zeta_{G}^{*}\colon{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf PsAlg}
is a categorical shortcut that avoids use of
\xi_{*}^{G}
, whose homotopical behavior is problematic. The alternative route avoids any use of pseudoalgebras over
\mathscr{F}
or
\mathscr{F}_{G}
, but we again lose symmetry, now due to the passage from
\xi^{G}_{*}
to a homotopically well-behaved derived variant. Conceivably, a more sophisticated derived variant might circumvent this.

Acknowledgements

2.
Preliminaries on operads and multicategories

We begin here by introducing our categorical framework. We also recall the notions of operads, their algebras, and pseudomorphisms between those. Finally, we recall the notion of a multicategory.

Notation 2.1
.

Throughout the document, we will denote pseudomorphisms of various types (for example, see
Definition 2.11
or
Definition 4.5
) by arrows
.

2.1.
\mathcal{V}
-categories

The categorical framework we begin with is the same as the one explained in more detail in
[
16
, Section 1]
, hence we shall be brief.

Assumption 2.2
.

We let
\mathcal{V}
be a cartesian closed, bicomplete category.

The examples of primary interest are
\mathcal{V}=\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}
or
\mathcal{V}=G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}
, where
\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}
is the
category of (compactly generated weak Hausdorff) spaces and
G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}
is the category of
G
-spaces and
G
-maps for a finite group
G
. The reader focused on topology is free to read
\mathcal{V}
as
\mathcal{U}
, but nothing before
Section 7
(or after

Section 10
) would change in any way. We defer further discussion of the equivariant context to
Section 6
.

As discussed in more detail in
[
16
, Section 1.1]
, we let
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
denote the 2-category of categories, functors, and natural transformations internal to
\mathcal{V}
. We will refer to these as
\mathcal{V}
-categories
,
\mathcal{V}
-functors
, and
\mathcal{V}
-transformations
.
Thus any
\mathcal{V}
-category
{\eus{C}}
consists of objects
\mathbf{Ob}{\eus{C}}
and
\textbf{Mor}{\eus{C}}
in
\mathcal{V}
, and source, target, identity, and composition structure maps, which are all required to be morphisms in
\mathcal{V}
.
For
{\eus{C}}
and
{\eus{D}}
in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
, a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow{\eus{D}}
is given by morphisms
\mathbf{Ob}{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Ob}{\eus{D}}
and
\textbf{Mor}{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow\textbf{Mor}{\eus{D}}
in
\mathcal{V}
that are suitably compatible with the internal category structure. A
\mathcal{V}
-transformation
\alpha
between
\mathcal{V}
-functors
F_{1},F_{2}\colon{\eus{C}}\rightrightarrows{\eus{D}}
is given by a morphism
\alpha\colon\mathbf{Ob}{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow\textbf{Mor}{\eus{D}}
in
\mathcal{V}
that makes the naturality diagrams commute.

Since
\mathcal{V}
is complete, so is
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
.

Assumption 2.3
.

We assume that
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
is moreover
cocomplete.

This assumption holds if either
\mathcal{V}
is locally presentable or if
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{U}

[
49
, (3.24) and (3.25)]
. For similar reasons, it is also true for
\mathcal{V}=G\mathcal{U}
.

Remark 2.4
.

We note that
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
is cartesian closed since
\mathcal{V}
is assumed to be cartesian closed
[
19
, Lemma 2.3.15]
.

Definition 2.5
.

We say that a
\mathcal{V}
-category
{\eus{C}}
is
chaotic
,
or indiscrete, if the source and target maps yield an isomorphism

{\textbf{Mor}}{\eus{C}}\xrightarrow{(S,T)}{\mathbf{Ob}}{\eus{C}}\times{\mathbf{Ob}}{\eus{C}}.

Chaotic
\mathcal{V}
-categories and their properties are discussed in detail in
[
16
, §1.2]
.

2.2.
Based
\mathcal{V}
-categories

Let
\ast
denote the terminal object of
\mathcal{V}
. A basepoint of an object
V
of
\mathcal{V}
is a map
\ast\longrightarrow V
in
\mathcal{V}
. Write
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
for the category of based objects of
\mathcal{V}
and based maps. Let
\ast
also denote the
\mathcal{V}
-category whose object and morphism objects are both given by the object
\ast
of
\mathcal{V}
.

Definition 2.6
.

A
based
\mathcal{V}
-category
, or
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-category
, is a category internal to
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
. Equivalently, it is a category
{\eus{C}}
internal to
\mathcal{V}
equipped with a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
\ast\longrightarrow{\eus{C}}
. Its structure maps source, target, identity, and composition must be in
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
.
There are corresponding notions of based functors, called
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functors
, namely
\mathcal{V}
-functors compatible with basepoints, and based
\mathcal{V}
-transformations, called

\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations
, whose component morphisms
\mathbf{Ob}{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow\textbf{Mor}{\eus{D}}
are based. As noted in
[
16
, Remark 1.6]
, the resulting
2
-category, here denoted
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
, can be identified with
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})_{\ast}
.

Remark 2.7
.

For a
\mathcal{V}
-category or
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-category
{\eus{C}}
,
an
object
of
{\eus{C}}
will mean a functor
\ast\longrightarrow{\eus{C}}
or, equivalently, a morphism
\ast\longrightarrow\mathbf{Ob}{\eus{C}}
in
\mathcal{V}
. We warn the reader that we are using the term “object” in a technical sense. For example, when
\mathcal{V}
is the category of
G
-spaces, an object is a
G
-fixed point of the
G
-space
\mathbf{Ob}{\eus{C}}
, hence
{\eus{C}}
may have no objects.

We can form the wedge and smash product of based
\mathcal{V}
-categories
{\eus{A}}
and
{\eus{B}}
via the pushout diagrams

\textstyle{\ast\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\eus{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\eus{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\eus{A}}\vee{\eus{B}}}

and

\textstyle{{\eus{A}}\vee{\eus{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\eus{A}}\times{\eus{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ast\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\eus{A}}\wedge{\eus{B}}}

just as for spaces.
Since the objects functor
\mathbf{Ob}\colon\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}
has both a left and a right adjoint and therefore preserves limits and colimits, it follows that

(2.8)

\mathbf{Ob}({\eus{A}}\wedge{\eus{B}})\cong\mathbf{Ob}({\eus{A}})\wedge\mathbf{Ob}({\eus{B}})

for
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-categories
{\eus{A}}
and
{\eus{B}}
.

By the universal property of the smash product, a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functor

{\eus{A}}\wedge{\eus{B}}\longrightarrow{\eus{C}}
corresponds to a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
{\eus{A}}\times{\eus{B}}\longrightarrow{\eus{C}}
whose restriction to
\ast\times{\eus{B}}
and
{\eus{A}}\times\ast
is the constant functor at the basepoint of
{\eus{C}}
.
This will allow us to define maps from smash products by specifying basepoint conditions on
\mathcal{V}
-functors defined on products.

Similarly, a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformation

\textstyle{{\eus{A}}\wedge{\eus{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle F}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle G}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\varphi}
\textstyle{\eus{C}}

corresponds to a
\mathcal{V}
-transformation of functors defined on
{\eus{A}}\times{\eus{B}}
whose restriction to
{\eus{A}}\times\ast
and
\ast\times{\eus{B}}
is the identity.

Remark 2.9
.

Our standing assumptions on
\mathcal{V}
imply that
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
is closed symmetric monoidal with internal hom adjoint to
\wedge
(see
[
9
, Lemma 4.20]
,
[
43
, Construction 3.3.14]
).
2
2

2

The associativity of
\wedge
is not formal from the universal property of the pushout and requires
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
to be closed.

We will use the symmetric monoidal structure to enrich categories over
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
starting in
Section 4
.

In our applications, categories often have disjoint base objects, and we write
{\eus{A}}_{+}
for the coproduct (disjoint union in the relevant examples)
of
\ast
with an unbased
\mathcal{V}
-category
{\eus{A}}
. Then

{\eus{A}}_{+}\wedge{\eus{B}}_{+}\cong({\eus{A}}\times{\eus{B}})_{+}

(see
[
43
, Lemma 3.3.16]
).

2.3.
Operads in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})

We will work throughout with a reduced operad
{\mathcal{O}}
in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
,
reduced
meaning that
{\mathcal{O}}(0)
is the trivial category
\ast
. We will often assume that
{\mathcal{O}}
is chaotic, meaning that each
\mathcal{V}
-category
{\mathcal{O}}(n)
is chaotic. We will use the notation

\gamma\colon{\mathcal{O}}(k)\times{\mathcal{O}}(j_{1})\times\dots\times{\mathcal{O}}(j_{k})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(j_{1}+\dots+j_{k})

for the operad structure
\mathcal{V}
-functors and
\mathds{1}\colon\ast\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(1)
for the unit object in
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
.

Definition 2.10
.

An
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra is an object
{\mathcal{A}}
in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
equipped with action
\mathcal{V}
-functors

\theta(n)\colon{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}^{n}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}

that are appropriately
\Sigma_{n}
-equivariant, unital, and associative, as in
[
29
]
. Since
{\mathcal{O}}
is assumed to be reduced, the functor
\theta(0)\colon\ast\longrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
specifies a basepoint
0=0_{{\mathcal{A}}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
.

We will be using non-strict maps between
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras, called
{\mathcal{O}}
-pseudomorphisms. The full definitions of these and of
{\mathcal{O}}
-transformations between them are given in
[
5
]
and, with some minor emendations, in
[
16
, Definitions 2.23 and 2.24]
. We shall not repeat details, but we remind the reader of the key features.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
be
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras. An
{\mathcal{O}}
-pseudomorphism

\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
F\colon{\mathcal{A}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
such that
F(0_{\mathcal{A}})=0_{\mathcal{B}}
,
together with invertible
\mathcal{V}
-transformations
\partial_{n}

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta(n)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times F^{n}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\,\partial_{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{B}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta(n)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}}

for
n\geq 0
such that

\partial_{0}
and the restriction of
\partial_{1}
along
\mathds{1}\times\mathrm{id}\colon{\mathcal{A}}\cong\ast\times{\mathcal{A}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(1)\times{\mathcal{A}}
are identity
\mathcal{V}
-transformations and such that the appropriate equality of associativity pasting diagrams relating the
\partial_{n}

to the structure maps of the operad holds (see
[
16
, Definition 2.23]
).
It is a (strict)
{\mathcal{O}}
-map if the
\partial_{n}
are identity
\mathcal{V}
-transformations.

Definition 2.12
.

An
{\mathcal{O}}
-transformation
between
{\mathcal{O}}
-pseudomorphisms
E
and
F

is a
\mathcal{V}
-transformation
\omega\colon E\Longrightarrow F
such that the equality

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta(n)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times E^{n}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\,\partial_{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{B}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta(n)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle E}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle F}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\omega}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}}

=

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta(n)}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathrm{id}\times E^{n}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathrm{id}\times F^{n}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 16.38895pt\mathrm{id}\times\omega^{n}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\,\partial_{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{B}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta(n)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}}

holds for all
n
. We do
not
require the
\omega
to be invertible.

Notation 2.13
.

We will work throughout with the 2-category
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras,

{\mathcal{O}}
-pseudomorphisms, and
{\mathcal{O}}
-transformations.

There is a more general definition of
{\mathcal{O}}
-pseudoalgebras, as defined in
[
5
,
16
]
, but we choose not to introduce it since it is not needed for the purposes of this paper.

2.4.
Review of multicategories

We shall not repeat the complete definition of a multicategory given in such sources as
[
8
,
24
,
52
]
.
A multicategory
{{\eus{M}}}

has a class
\mathbf{Ob}({\eus{M}})

of objects and for each sequence

\underline{a}=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\}
of objects, where
k\geq 0
,
and each object
b
, it has a set of
k
-ary morphisms

{{\eus{M}}}_{k}(\underline{a};b)={{\eus{M}}}_{k}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k};b).

A quintessential example is that of
k
-linear maps in the category of vector spaces, which is why
k
-ary morphisms in arbitrary multicategories are sometimes
called
k
-linear maps, even when there is no linear structure in sight.

Throughout, we understand multicategories to be symmetric,
so that the symmetric group
\Sigma_{k}
acts from the right on the collection of
k
-ary morphisms via maps

\sigma\colon{{\eus{M}}}_{k}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k};b)\longrightarrow{{\eus{M}}}_{k}(a_{\sigma(1)},\dots,a_{\sigma(k)};b).

For each object
a
there is an identity
1
-ary morphism
a\longrightarrow a

and there are composition functions

(2.14)

\gamma\colon{{\eus{M}}}_{k}(\underline{b};c)\times{{\eus{M}}}_{j_{1}}(\underline{a}_{1};b_{1})\times\dots\times{{\eus{M}}}_{j_{k}}(\underline{a}_{k};b_{k})\longrightarrow{{\eus{M}}}_{j}(\{\underline{a}_{1},\dots,\underline{a}_{k}\};c),

where
\underline{b}
is a
k
-tuple,
\underline{a}_{q}
for
1\leq q\leq k
is a
j_{q}
-tuple, and, with
j=j_{1}+\dots+j_{k}
,

\{\underline{a}_{1},\dots,\underline{a}_{k}\}
is the
j
-tuple
\{a_{1,1},\dots,a_{1,j_{1}},\dots,a_{k,1},\dots,a_{k,j_{k}}\}
.

The
\gamma
are subject to direct generalizations of the associativity, identity, and equivariance properties required
of an operad in
[
29
]
. These properties are spelled out diagrammatically in
[
8
, 2.1]

and, with exceptional care, in
[
52
, Chapter 11]
.
3
3

3

The colored operads in
[
52
]
are symmetric multicategories
with a set of objects, called colors, but the generalization to a class of objects is evident.

All of our multicategories are enriched in
\mathbf{Cat}
, but since that is only used peripherally we will not go into detail.
4
4

4

In fact, they are enriched in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
when
\mathcal{V}
is closed.

A multicategory with one object is then the same thing as an operad in
\mathbf{Cat}
.
Multicategories are often called colored operads, with objects thought of as colors. The objects and
1
-ary morphisms of a multicategory
{{\eus{M}}}
specify its underlying category, which is often also denoted
{{\eus{M}}}
by abuse of notation.

Remark 2.15
.

There is a canonical
5
5

5

There is a slight subtlety here. It has been said that there is a choice of such multicategories depending on the chosen order of associating variables. With
an unbiased operadic definition of a symmetric monoidal category, the specification of
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{C}})
is unambiguous.
multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{C}})
associated to a symmetric monoidal category
({\mathcal{C}},\otimes)
.
Its objects are those of
{\mathcal{C}}
, and

\mathbf{Mult}_{k}({\mathcal{C}})(a_{1},\dots,a_{k};b)={\mathcal{C}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k},b).

It has the evident symmetric group actions and units. In schematic elementwise notation, using the notations of
(2.14)
, the composite of
a
k
-ary morphism
F\colon\underline{b}\longrightarrow c
with
(E_{1},\dots,E_{k})
, where
E_{r}\colon\underline{a}_{r}\longrightarrow b_{r}
is a
j_{r}
-ary morphism for
1\leq r\leq k
, is the
composite

(2.16)

\textstyle{\bigotimes\limits_{1\leq r\leq k}\bigotimes\limits_{1\leq s\leq j_{r}}a_{r,s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes_{r}E_{r}}
\textstyle{\bigotimes\limits_{1\leq r\leq k}b_{r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{c.}

This generalizes the example in vector spaces where
k
-linear maps correspond to maps out of the tensor product.

A morphism
\mathbb{F}\colon{\eus{M}}\longrightarrow{\eus{N}}
of multicategories, called a multifunctor, is a function

\mathbb{F}\colon\mathbf{Ob}({\eus{M}})\longrightarrow\mathbf{Ob}({\eus{N}})
together with functions

\mathbb{F}\colon{\eus{M}}_{k}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k};b)\longrightarrow{\eus{N}}_{k}(\mathbb{F}a_{1},\dots,\mathbb{F}a_{k};\mathbb{F}b)

for all objects
a_{i}
and
b
such that
\mathbb{F}(\mathrm{id}_{a})=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{F}(a)}
and
\mathbb{F}
preserves composition. If these functions are
\Sigma_{k}
-equivariant, we say that
\mathbb{F}
is a
symmetric multifunctor
. A lax monoidal (resp. lax symmetric monoidal) functor between symmetric monoidal categories gives rise to a multifunctor (resp. symmetric multifunctor) between the corresponsing multicategories.

Given a multicategory
{\eus{M}}
, one can define the notion of monoid in
{\eus{M}}
(see
[
24
, Example 2.1.11]
or
[
52
, §14.2]
). This can be done
using “parameter multicategories”, so that a monoid in
{\eus{M}}
is given by a multifunctor out of the appropriate parameter multicategory into
{\eus{M}}
. One can similarly define the notion of module over a monoid (see
[
8
, Definition 2.5]
).
These notions agree with the usual ones when dealing with the multicategory associated to a symmetric monoidal category as in
Remark 2.15
. A multifunctor preserves associative and unital algebraic structures, and a symmetric multifunctor moreover preserves commutative ones.

3.
The multicategory of
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras

The goal of this section is to establish a multiplicative structure on the category
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}
of algebras over an operad.
After some initial setup in
Section 3.1
, we introduce the key concept of a pseudo-commutative operad in
Section 3.2
, following Corner and Gurski
[
5
]
.
We then establish a multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})
for any pseudo-commutative operad
{\mathcal{O}}
in
Section 3.3
, following Hyland and Power
[
18
]
, and describe some variants in
Section 3.4
.
Finally, we show that the free
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra functor extends to a multifunctor in
Section 3.5
.

3.1.
The intrinsic pairing of an operad

Surprisingly, the following elementary structure implicit in the definition of an operad is central to our work. It is present in any reduced operad
{\mathcal{O}}
in any cartesian monoidal category
\mathcal{W}
.

Definition 3.1
.

The
intrinsic pairing

\circledast\colon({\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}
of an operad
{\mathcal{O}}
is given by the composites

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times{\mathcal{O}}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\Delta^{j}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times{\mathcal{O}}(k)^{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(jk),}

where
\gamma
is the structure map of the operad and
j\geq 0
and
k\geq 0
.

Thinking of
\gamma
as specifying additive structure, the “product”
\circledast
is taking seriously
that
jk=k+\cdots+k
. Thus the intrinsic pairing is an operadic manifestation of the grade school lesson that
multiplication is iterated addition.

Remark 3.2
.

The intrinsic pairing is
not
a pairing of operads in the sense originally defined in
[
32
, 1.4]
.
For many operads occurring naturally in topology, such as the little cubes or Steiner operads, the intrinsic pairing appears to be of no real
interest. However, as we shall see in
Section 3.3
, it appears naturally when trying to construct a multicategory of algebras over an operad.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an operad in a cartesian monoidal category
\mathcal{W}
. Then
\underline{{\mathcal{O}}}=\coprod_{j\geq 0}{\mathcal{O}}(j)
is a monoid in
\mathcal{W}
with product
\circledast
and unit the unit object
\mathds{1}\in{\mathcal{O}}(1)
. It has a zero object
\ast\in{\mathcal{O}}(0)
.

Proof.

The unit properties of an operad are

\gamma(\mathds{1};x)=x
and
\gamma(x;\mathds{1}^{j})=x

for
x\in{\mathcal{O}}(j)
. These say that
\mathds{1}
is a unit for
\underline{{\mathcal{O}}}
. The associativity of the pairing
is an easy diagram chase from the following special case of the associativity diagram for
\gamma
in the definition of an operad.

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times{\mathcal{O}}(k)^{j}\times{\mathcal{O}}(\ell)^{jk}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(jk)\times{\mathcal{O}}(\ell)^{jk}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(jk\ell)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times({\mathcal{O}}(k)\times{\mathcal{O}}(\ell)^{k})^{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\gamma^{j}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times{\mathcal{O}}(k\ell)^{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}

Since
{\mathcal{O}}
is reduced,
\ast\in{\mathcal{O}}(0)
is a zero element.
∎

Consider the category
\Sigma
of sets
\underline{n}=\{1,\dots,n\}
and isomorphisms. It is bipermutative under disjoint union and cartesian product. To be precise, the two monoidal structures,
\oplus
and
\otimes
, are given by sum and product at the level of objects. To apply
\oplus
to
permutations
\sigma\in\Sigma_{j}
and
\tau\in\Sigma_{k}
and regard the result as a permutation of the

j+k
letters
\underline{j+k}=\{1,\dots,j+k\}
, we are implicitly applying the evident isomorphism

\zeta_{j,k}\colon\underline{j+k}\longrightarrow\underline{j}\amalg\underline{k},

then taking the disjoint union of
\sigma
and
\tau
,
and then applying
\zeta_{j,k}^{-1}
. That is,
\sigma\oplus\tau
is defined by the commutative diagram

(3.4)

\textstyle{\underline{j+k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma\oplus\tau}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{j,k}}
\textstyle{\underline{j+k}}
\textstyle{\underline{j}\amalg\underline{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma\amalg\tau}
\textstyle{\underline{j}\amalg\underline{k}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{j,k}^{-1}}

Similarly, define

(3.5)

\lambda=\lambda_{j,k}\colon\underline{jk}\longrightarrow\underline{j}\times\underline{k}

to be the order-preserving bijection, where
\underline{j}\times\underline{k}
is ordered
lexicographically. Then,

\mu\otimes\nu
is defined by the commutative diagram

(3.6)

\textstyle{\underline{jk}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu\otimes\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{j,k}}
\textstyle{\underline{jk}}
\textstyle{\underline{j}\times\underline{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu\times\nu}
\textstyle{\underline{j}\times\underline{k}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{j,k}^{-1}}

Recall that the associative operad
\mathbf{Assoc}
is given by
\mathbf{Assoc}(n)=\Sigma_{n}
. Then
\otimes
gives the intrinsic pairing of
Definition 3.1
on
\mathbf{Assoc}
. Moreover,
if we think of the groups
\Sigma_{j}
as categories
with a single object and thus think of
\mathbf{Assoc}
as an operad in
\mathbf{Cat}
, then
\underline{\mathbf{Assoc}}=\Sigma
and the monoidal structure of
Proposition 3.3
is given by
\otimes
.

In particular,
e_{j}\otimes e_{k}=e_{jk}
. Since
\otimes
is a group homomorphism, it is equivariant
in the sense that
\mu\sigma\otimes\nu\tau=(\mu\otimes\nu)(\sigma\otimes\tau)
. Clearly
e_{1}\otimes\nu=\nu

and
\mu\otimes e_{1}=\mu
.

Remark 3.7
.

The equivariance formulas for an operad
{\mathcal{O}}
imply that the pairing on
\Sigma
and the pairing on
{\mathcal{O}}
are compatible in the sense that for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{j}
and
\tau\in\Sigma_{k}
the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times{\mathcal{O}}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\scriptstyle{\sigma\times\tau}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(jk)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma\otimes\tau}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times{\mathcal{O}}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(jk)}

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\tau_{j,k}\in\Sigma_{jk}
be the permutation specified by the composite

\textstyle{\underline{jk}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{j,k}}
\textstyle{\underline{j}\times\underline{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\underline{k}\times\underline{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j}^{-1}}
\textstyle{\underline{kj}=\underline{jk}}

It reorders the set
\underline{j}\times\underline{k}
from lexicographic ordering to reverse lexicographic ordering.
Clearly
\tau_{j,k}^{-1}=\tau_{k,j}
and
\tau_{1,n}=e_{n}=\tau_{n,1}
.

The
\tau_{i,j}
are the symmetry isomorphisms
for
\otimes
in
\Sigma
. More precisely, for
\mu\in\Sigma_{j}
and
\nu\in\Sigma_{k}
, we have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\underline{jk}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{j,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{j,k}}
\textstyle{\underline{j}\times\underline{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu\times\nu}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\underline{j}\times\underline{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{jk}^{-1}}
\textstyle{\underline{jk}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{j,k}}
\textstyle{\underline{kj}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j}}
\textstyle{\underline{k}\times\underline{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu\times\mu}
\textstyle{\underline{k}\times\underline{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j}^{-1}}
\textstyle{\underline{kj}.}

That is,

(3.9)

\tau_{j,k}(\mu\otimes\nu)=(\nu\otimes\mu)\tau_{j,k}\ \ \text{or equivalently}\ \ (\mu\otimes\nu)\tau_{k,j}=\tau_{k,j}(\nu\otimes\mu).

3.2.
Pseudo-commutative operads

Recall the permutativity operad
\mathcal{P}

from
[
13
, Definition 4.1]
. It is the chaotic categorification of
\mathbf{Assoc}
, and as an operad in
\mathbf{Cat}
, its algebras are in one-to-one correspondence with permutative categories. The intrinsic pairing
\circledast
on
\mathcal{P}
is inherited from that of
\mathbf{Assoc}
.
Note that equation
(3.9)
implies that the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(j)\times\mathcal{P}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(jk)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{k,j}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(k)\times\mathcal{P}(j)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(kj)}

does not in general commute. Rather, since
\mathcal{P}(kj)
is chaotic, there exists a natural isomorphism
\alpha_{j,k}\colon\tau_{k,j}\circ\circledast\Longrightarrow\circledast\circ t
. This is an example of a pseudo-commutative operad, as defined by Corner and Gurski
[
5
, §4]
.

We summarize their definition here. As usual, when defining categorical structures, coherence axioms are essential for completeness and rigor. However, they can be lengthy and may not make for enjoyable reading. To avoid disrupting the flow of exposition, we generally defer their precise formulation to
Section 11
. In particular, we give the axioms needed to complete the following definition in
Section 11.1
.
6
6

6

The original definition of
[
5
]
requires some minor corrections that are given there.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an operad in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
. A
pseudo-commutative structure
on an operad
{\mathcal{O}}
is a
collection of
invertible
\mathcal{V}
-transformations, one for each
(j,k)
, of the form

(3.11)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times{\mathcal{O}}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 8.19447pt\ \ \alpha_{j,k}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(jk)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{k,j}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(k)\times{\mathcal{O}}(j)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(kj).}

The
\alpha_{j,k}
must satisfy coherence axioms for identity, symmetry, equivariance, and operadic compatibility that are specified and discussed in
Section 11.1
.

If
{\mathcal{O}}
is chaotic, such transformations
\alpha
always exist and all conditions are automatically satisfied
[
16
, §1.2]
, thus giving the following result.

Lemma 3.12
.

(
[
5
, Corollary 4.9]
) A chaotic operad has a unique pseudo-commutative structure.

We will often write “pseudo-commutative operad” when we really mean “operad equipped with a pseudo-commutative structure”, but there is no ambiguity when
{\mathcal{O}}
is chaotic, and we later prefer to specialize to chaotic operads.

3.3.
The multicategory of
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras

Hyland and Power
[
18
]
show that there is a multicategory of algebras over a pseudo-commutative monad, and Corner and Gurski show in
[
5
]
that the monad corresponding to a pseudo-commutative operad is pseudo-commutative in the sense of
[
18
]
. We follow these sources to describe the multicategory of algebras over a pseudo-commutative operad.

For the pairings of
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras we want to consider maps
F\colon{\mathcal{A}}\times{\mathcal{B}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}
that preserve the algebra structure on each variable up to canonical isomorphism. For example, if
+
denotes a binary operation in
{\mathcal{O}}
, we need to make sense of a distributivity
law of the general form

F(a,b_{1}+b_{2})\cong F(a,b_{1})+F(a,b_{2}).

Diagonal maps enter since
a
appears
once on the left and twice
on the right. The definition contains a number of schematic
coherence diagrams to the effect that whenever two natural transformations have a chance to be equal
they are equal. We shall explain the diagrams after giving the definition.
The following maps
s_{i}

play a key role.
7
7

7

The elementary maps
s_{i}
correspond to the “strengths”
t_{i}
in Hyland and Power

[
18
, p. 156]
; in their categorical treatment, the existence of
t_{i}
with suitable properties is an axiom on a given 2-monad,
although they do make the strengths explicit in the case of permutative categories.

Notation 3.13
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq k
, be
\mathcal{V}
-categories and let
n\geq 0
. Define
s_{i}
to be the
composite
\mathcal{V}
-functor displayed in the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\dots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i-1}\times{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i}^{n}\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i+1}\times\dots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{i}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times({\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k})^{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\dots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i-1}\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i}^{n}\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i+1}\times\dots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\Delta}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{n}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

Here
t
is the evident transposition,
\Delta
is obtained by applying the diagonal maps

{\mathcal{A}}_{j}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{j}^{n}
for
j\neq i
, and the right hand isomorphism is obtained by transposing from a product
of
n
th powers to an
n
th power of a product.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a (reduced) pseudo-commutative operad in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
.
We define the (symmetric) multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})
of
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras and pseudomorphisms.
Its underlying 2-category is
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}
, so its objects, morphisms, and
2
-cells are the
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras, the

{\mathcal{O}}
-pseudomorphisms (
Definition 2.11
), and the
{\mathcal{O}}
-transformations (
Definition 2.12
).
Recall that since
{\mathcal{O}}
is reduced, all
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras are assigned basepoints. Its 0-ary morphisms are (unbased) maps
\ast\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
, that is, they correspond to a choice of object in
{\mathcal{B}}
.
For
k>1
, its
k
-ary morphisms
({\mathcal{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{A}}_{k})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}

are the tuples
(F,\delta_{i})
, where

(a)

F\colon{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
is a

\mathcal{V}_{*}
-functor, which we may equally well express as a
\mathcal{V}
-functor

F\colon{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}

such that
F(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})
is equal to
0_{\mathcal{B}}
if any object

a_{i}
is
0_{{\mathcal{A}}_{i}}
and
F(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
is
\mathrm{id}_{0_{\mathcal{B}}}
if any
f_{i}
is
\mathrm{id}_{0_{{\mathcal{A}}_{i}}}
, and

(b)

the
\delta_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq k
, are sequences of invertible
\mathcal{V}
-transformations
\delta_{i}(n)
as indicated in the following diagram.

(3.15)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times({\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k})^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times F^{n}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 8.19447pt\ \delta_{i}(n)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{B}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta(n)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i}^{n}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\quad\quad\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\theta(n)\times\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}}

The distributivity isomorphisms
\delta_{i}(n)
must satisfy coherence axioms that are specified and discussed in
Section 11.2
.

For
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
, the right action of
\Sigma_{k}
on the
k
-ary morphisms of
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})
sends

(F,\delta_{i})\colon({\mathcal{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{A}}_{k})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}

to
the composite

(3.16)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{\sigma(1)}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{\sigma(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}},}

where
\sigma
denotes the reordering of terms given by
\sigma(a_{\sigma(1)},\dots,a_{\sigma(k)})=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k}).

Permuting the indices, the
\delta_{i}
for
F\sigma
are inherited from the
\delta_{i}
for
F
.
Precisely,
\delta_{\sigma^{-1}(i)}(n)
for
F\sigma
is induced from
\delta_{i}(n)
for
F
by pasting the
defining diagram
(3.15)

to the right of the following diagram, which is easily checked to be commutative.

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times({\mathcal{A}}_{\sigma(1)}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{\sigma(k)})^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\sigma^{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times({\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k})^{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{\sigma(1)}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i}^{n}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{\sigma(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{\sigma^{-1}(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\theta(n)\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i}^{n}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\theta(n)\times\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{\sigma(1)}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{\sigma(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k}}

Since
i=\sigma\sigma^{-1}(i)
, the term
{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i}^{n}
appears in the
\sigma^{-1}(i)
th factor of
the middle left term. Note that
(F\sigma)\tau=F(\sigma\tau)
, both mapping
{\mathcal{A}}_{\sigma\tau(1)}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{\sigma\tau(k)}
to
{\mathcal{B}}
.

The identity functor of
{\mathcal{A}}
gives the unit element
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})({\mathcal{A}};{\mathcal{A}})
. With the notation for sequences from
Section 2.4
, the composition multiproduct

\textstyle{\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})(\underline{{\mathcal{B}}};{\mathcal{C}})\times\prod_{q=1}^{k}\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})(\underline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{q};{\mathcal{B}}_{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})(\{\underline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{1},\dots\underline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{k}\};{\mathcal{C}})}

is given by

\gamma(F;E_{1},\dots,E_{k})=F\circ(E_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge E_{k}).

We identify
\{(q,r)\}
,
1\leq q\leq k
and
1\leq r\leq j_{q}
, with
\{1\leq i\leq j_{1}+\cdots j_{k}\}
by letting
(q,r)
correspond to

i=j_{1}+\cdots+j_{q-1}+r
. Then
\delta_{q,r}(n)
for the multicomposition is given by pasting the diagrams for
\delta_{q}^{F}
and
\delta_{r}^{E_{q}}
. We show this explicitly in the case of
(q,r)=(1,1)
. The general case is shown similarly. We use the notation
{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}_{q}\!}}
for the product
{\mathcal{A}}_{q,1}\times\dots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{q,j_{q}}
.

(3.17)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times\left(\hskip 1.00006pt{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\!}}\times\cdots\times\overline{{\mathcal{A}}_{k}}\hskip 1.00006pt\right)^{\!n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times(E_{1}\times\dots\times E_{k})^{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times({\mathcal{B}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{B}}_{k})^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times F^{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{C}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta(n)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\!}}^{\,n}\times{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\!}}\times\cdots\times{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\!}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times E_{1}^{n}\times E_{2}\times\dots\times E_{k}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 16.38895pt\delta_{1}^{E_{1}}(n)\times\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{B}}_{1}^{n}\times{\mathcal{B}}_{2}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{B}}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta(n)\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{s_{1}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 16.38895pt\delta_{1}^{F}(n)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}_{1,1}^{n}\times{\mathcal{A}}_{1,2}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k,j_{k}}\quad\quad\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{1}\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\theta(n)\times\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\!}}\times\cdots\times{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\!}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E_{1}\times\dots\times E_{k}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\times\dots\times{\mathcal{B}}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}}

One can check that the
\delta
s satisfy the coherence axioms, and that this composition is associative, unital and respects equivariance.
Further verifications are needed to show that this all really does specify a multicategory.
For example, the symmetry axiom (ii) in
Section 11.1
is used in the verification that
F\sigma

satisfies the axioms when
F
does. However, we omit further details.
We have translated the axioms
of Hyland and Power to our operadic setting. Their
[
18
, Proposition 18]
applies to show
that
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})
is a multicategory enriched in the category
\mathbf{Cat}
of small categories.
We learned the central role played by pseudo-commutativity from them.

3.4.
Variants of
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})
and comparisons

We remark that our definition of
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})
applies almost verbatim to define a multicategory of
{\mathcal{O}}
-pseudoalgebras as defined in
[
5
,
16
]
. Pseudoalgebras over an operad are defined by relaxing the coherence diagrams for the operadic multiplication with the structure map of the algebra to only commute up to coherent natural isomorphisms. The only axioms in the definition of
{\mathcal{O}}
-pseudomorphisms (listed in
Section 11.2
) that would differ slightly
for
{\mathcal{O}}
-pseudoalgebras as opposed to
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras are (
iv
) and (
v
). In these conditions it wouldn’t make sense to ask for equality of 2-cells as written, since the maps on the boundary of the 2-cells would not be equal, and instead one would need to paste them with the coherence isomorphisms from the definition of
{\mathcal{O}}
-pseudoalgebras.

When
\mathcal{V}=\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}
is the permutativity operad
\mathcal{P}
,
the multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}(\mathcal{P})

is not quite the same as the multicategory of symmetric strict monoidal categories
defined by Hyland and Power
[
18
]
and the multicategory of permutative categories defined by Elmendorf and Mandell
[
8
]
.
The difference is that we have taken our distributivity 2-cells
\delta_{i}
to be invertible. Neither
[
18
]
nor
[
8
]
do so, and we have
drawn our arrows in the direction used in
[
18
]
, which is opposite to the choice in
[
8
]
.
This difference in the choice of direction of the 2-cells
\delta_{i}
would matter
if we relaxed the isomorphism requirement. For example, the strengths
s_{i}
of
Notation 3.13

would no longer be relevant with the opposite choice, so the definition in
[
8
]
would no
longer be a specialization of
[
18
]
and would not be compatible with the conventions of Corner
and Gurski
[
5
]
or with LaPlaza’s classical coherence theory for symmetric bimonoidal categories

[
21
,
22
]
. It would therefore lead to some erroneous conclusions, as explained in
[
34
, Scholium 12.3]
.

The work of
[
8
]
used the classical biased definition of permutative categories rather than
its unbiased operadic equivalent, and that simplifies details when comparing operadic algebraic
structures to their classical biased equivalents.
With our unbiased operadic reformulation, the equivariant generalization is immediate. For example, we can take

{\mathcal{O}}
to be the categorical equivariant Barratt-Eccles operad

\mathcal{P}_{G}
of
[
13
]

to obtain the multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}(\mathcal{P}_{G})
of genuine permutative
G
-categories; genuine permutative and symmetric monoidal
G
-categories are defined to be
\mathcal{P}_{G}
-algebras and
\mathcal{P}_{G}
-pseudoalgebras, respectively, in
[
13
,
16
]
. Our work also applies to the normed symmetric monoidal categories of Rubin
[
45
]
, which are defined as algebras over an operad, but which also admit a biased definition.

3.5.
The free
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra multifunctor
\mathbb{O}_{+}

As explained in
[
35
, Section 4]
, a (reduced) operad
{\mathcal{O}}
in a category
\mathcal{W}
has two associated monads,
\mathbb{O}
defined on the ground category
\mathcal{W}_{*}
and
\mathbb{O}_{+}
defined on the ground category
\mathcal{W}
. Their categories of algebras are isomorphic. The first takes the basepoint as given and requires the basepoint built in by the operad action to agree with the given one, and it is defined using basepoint identifications. The second just builds in the basepoint by the action. The first is the one central to topology and is in principle more general. For an unbased object
X\in\mathcal{W}
,

(3.18)

\mathbb{O}_{+}X=\mathbb{O}(X_{+})=\coprod_{j\geq 0}{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times_{\Sigma_{j}}X^{j}.

This is a based object with basepoint given by the inclusion of
\ast={\mathcal{O}}(0)\times X^{0}
.

Starting on the category level with
\mathcal{W}=\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
, we prefer to avoid basepoint identifications and we therefore focus on
\mathbb{O}_{+}
. When
\mathcal{V}
is
\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}
or
G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}
, applying the classifying space functor gives an operad
B{\mathcal{O}}
, and we denote the associated monad by
\mathbb{O}_{+}^{top}
. If
{\mathcal{O}}
is
\Sigma
-free, in the sense that
\Sigma_{j}
acts freely on
{\mathcal{O}}(j)
for each
j
, we then have the basic commutation relation

(3.19)

B(\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}})\cong\mathbb{O}_{+}^{top}(B{\eus{C}})

for
{\eus{C}}\in\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
, and that is essential to our applications.

Thus fix a chaotic operad
{\mathcal{O}}
in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
in this section.

Definition 3.20
.

For
\mathcal{V}
-categories
{\eus{C}}
and
{\eus{D}}
, define a
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-functor

\omega\colon\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}}\wedge\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{D}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{C}}\times{\eus{D}})

by passage to orbits from the maps

(3.21)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times{\eus{C}}^{j}\times{\mathcal{O}}(k)\times{\eus{D}}^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times{\mathcal{O}}(k)\times{\eus{C}}^{j}\times{\eus{D}}^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\times\ell}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(jk)\times({\eus{C}}\times{\eus{D}})^{jk}.}

The map
\ell
here is defined using the lexicographic ordering
\lambda
of
(3.5)
; explicitly, it is given by

(c_{1},\dots,c_{j}),(d_{1},\dots,d_{k})\mapsto(c_{1},d_{1}),\dots(c_{1},d_{k}),\dots\dots,(c_{j},d_{1}),\dots,(c_{j},d_{k}).

Since
{\mathcal{O}}
is reduced, the maps
(3.21)
factor through the smash product and, using the equivariance axiom for operad composition, they also pass to orbits with respect to symmetric group actions; therefore they induce a well-defined
map
\omega
.

Proposition 3.22
.

A pseudo-commutativity structure on
{\mathcal{O}}
induces an invertible
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformation

(3.23)

\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}}\wedge\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\ \alpha}
\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{C}}\times{\eus{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}(t)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{D}}\wedge\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{D}}\times{\eus{C}}).}

Proof.

By axiom (

) from

,
the transformations

descend to orbits.
It is straightforward to check that they define the claimed invertible

-transformation.
∎

We now extend

to a binary morphism in

.
We need to define the transformations

for

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times(\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}}\times\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{D}})^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times{\omega}^{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{C}}\times{\eus{D}})^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times(\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}})^{n}\times\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\theta(n)\times\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}}\times\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{C}}\times{\eus{D}})}

Proposition 3.25
.

The axioms on

∎

For ease of notation we will denote this morphism by

.

Lemma 3.26
.

\omega

Proof.

Since the intrinsic pairing

is associative, the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}}\wedge\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{D}}\wedge\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

commutes; the compatibility of the

follows from the conditions on

.
∎

Thus, given
\mathcal{V}
-categories
{\eus{C}}_{1},\dots,{\eus{C}}_{k}
, we have a corresponding
k

\omega_{k}\colon(\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}}_{k})\longrightarrow\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{C}}_{1}\times\dots\times{\eus{C}}_{k})

in

, the

for

are obtained from those for

using the pasting

. We take
\omega_{1}=\mathrm{id}
and take

to be the choice of object
(\mathds{1},\ast)\in{\mathcal{O}}(1)\times\ast\subset\mathbb{O}_{+}(\ast)
.

Theorem 3.28
.

The functor
\mathbb{O}_{+}
from
\mathcal{V}
-categories to
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras extends to a multifunctor

\mathbb{O}_{+}\colon\mathbf{Mult}(\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}))\longrightarrow\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}}).

We do not claim that the multifunctor we construct is symmetric, and we
shall show that it is not in
Remark 3.29
.

Proof.

In
{\bf{Mult}}(\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}))
, a
k
-ary morphism is just a
\mathcal{V}
-functor

F\colon{\eus{C}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\eus{C}}_{k}\longrightarrow{\eus{D}}
. Its image under the multifunctor is the composite

\textstyle{(\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}}_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{k}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{C}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\eus{C}}_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}F}
\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{D}}.}

It is clear that this assignment sends the identity of
{\eus{C}}
to the identity of
\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{C}})
. The fact that the assignment preserves composition follows from the functoriality of
\mathbb{O}_{+}
and the naturality of
\omega
(
Lemma 3.26
).
∎

Remark 3.29
.

The multifunctor
\mathbb{O}_{+}
is not symmetric. To see this, consider
\mathrm{id}_{{\eus{C}}\times{\eus{D}}}
as a bilinear map in
\mathbf{Mult}(\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}))
. On one hand, if we first hit it with the symmetry
t
and then
\mathbb{O}_{+}
, we end up with a bilinear map whose
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-functor is given by

\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{D}}\wedge\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{D}}\times{\eus{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}(t)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{C}}\times{\eus{D}}).}

If, on the other hand, we first do
\mathbb{O}_{+}
and next
t
, we get

\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{D}}\wedge\mathbb{O}_{+}{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{C}})\times\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{\mathbb{O}_{+}({\eus{C}}\times{\eus{D}}).}

These maps do not agree since they differ by the two-cell
\alpha
.

4.
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
categories and their algebras and pseudoalgebras

This section establishes terminology and notation that will be used frequently in the coming sections.
Much of what we do in
Section 4.1
is to describe explicitly what it means to do enriched category theory over the 2-category
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
.
In
Section 4.2
, we introduce algebras and pseudoalgebras in this context, and we set ourselves up to discuss multiplicative structures on our categories of algebras by introducing monoidal structures on our enriched categories in
Section 4.3
.

4.1.
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-categories

All of our categories of operators, which we introduce in Sections
5.1
and
6.2
, are examples of
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-categories, and we explain what those are here. Returning to
Section 2
, we note that
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
and
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
are symmetric monoidal
2
-categories, with cartesian and smash product, respectively. As such, we can enrich and weakly enrich over them, in the sense of
[
10
]
. Classically, a
2
-category is a category enriched in the category
\mathbf{Cat}
of (small) categories.

Definition 4.1
.

We refer to categories, functors, and natural transformations enriched in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
as
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-categories
,
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-functors
, and
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-natural transformations
, respectively.
Similarly, we call categories, functors, and natural transformations enriched in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})

\mathcal{V}_{*}
-
2
-categories
,
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-
2
-functors
, and
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-
2
-natural transformations
, respectively.

We briefly unpack these definitions. A
\mathcal{V}
-2-category
{\eus{C}}
consists of a collection of objects (
0
-cells) and a morphism
\mathcal{V}
-category
{\eus{C}}(c,d)
for each pair of objects
(c,d)
(giving objects in
\mathcal{V}
of
1
-cells and
2
-cells). For a
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-2-category we moreover have that each
{\eus{C}}(c,d)
has a basepoint, and the composition factors through the smash product.
If
{\eus{C}}
and
{\eus{D}}
are
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-categories, a
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-functor
F\colon{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow{\eus{D}}
is given by a function
F
on objects and
\mathcal{V}
-functors
{\eus{C}}(c,d)\longrightarrow{\eus{D}}(F(c),F(d))
satisfying the evident unit and associativity conditions (strictly). For a
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-2-functor we further require that the latter are
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-functors.

Finally, if
E
and
F
are
\mathcal{V}
-2-functors
{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow{\eus{D}}
, where
{\eus{C}}
and
{\eus{D}}
are
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-categories, a

\mathcal{V}
-
2
-natural transformation
\zeta\colon E\Longrightarrow F
consists of
1-cells
8
8

8

As in
Remark 2.7
, a 1-cell here means a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
\ast\longrightarrow{\eus{D}}(E(c),F(c))
.

\zeta_{c}\colon E(c)\longrightarrow F(c)
, meaning objects of
{\eus{D}}(E(c),F(c))
, such that the naturality diagrams

\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(c,d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{{\eus{D}}(E(c),E(d))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\zeta_{d})_{*}}
\textstyle{{\eus{D}}(F(c),F(d))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\zeta_{c})^{*}}

commute for all pairs

of objects of
{\eus{C}}
. The same is true for a
\mathcal{V}_{*}

As in
[

-2-categories with a zero object and

{\eus{C}}(c,d)\cong\ast

or

.
If

\mathcal{V}
-category

is based with basepoint

(4.2)

\textstyle{0_{c,d}\colon\ast\cong{\eus{C}}(\mathbf{0},d)\times{\eus{C}}(c,\mathbf{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(c,d).}

Proposition 4.3
.

For
{\eus{C}}
a
\mathcal{V}
-2-category with zero object
\mathbf{0}
, the basepoints (

) give
{\eus{C}}
an enrichment in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
.

Proof.

We only need to check that composition in
{\eus{C}}
factors through the smash product. The associativity diagram

\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(d,e)\times{\eus{C}}(\mathbf{0},d)\times{\eus{C}}(c,\mathbf{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\circ}
\scriptstyle{\circ\times\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(d,e)\times{\eus{C}}(c,d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\ast\cong{\eus{C}}(\mathbf{0},e)\times{\eus{C}}(c,\mathbf{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(c,e)}

shows that composition sends
{\eus{C}}(d,e)\times 0_{c,d}
to
0_{c,e}
and the symmetric argument shows that composition also sends

to

.
∎

The following result, whose proof we leave to the reader, characterizes
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-functors for
\mathcal{V}
-2-categories with a zero object.

Proposition 4.4
.

If
{\eus{C}}
and
{\eus{D}}
have zero objects, then
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-functors

correspond bijectively to

\mathcal{V}
-2-functors
F\colon{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow{\eus{D}}
that are reduced, in the sense that
F(\mathbf{0}_{\eus{C}})\cong\mathbf{0}_{\eus{D}}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-2-natural transformations correspond bijectively to
\mathcal{V}
-2-natural transformations between reduced
\mathcal{V}
-2-functors (that is, there is no extra condition).

Following
