be
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctors
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\eus{D}}
, where
{\eus{C}}
and
{\eus{D}}
are
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-categories.
Then a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudotransformation
\zeta\colon E\Longrightarrow F
is a
\mathcal{V}
-pseudotransformation of the underlying
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors such that each
\zeta_{b,c}
is a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformation, meaning that its component at
0_{b,c}
is the identity.

As in
Proposition 4.4
, we have the following characterization of
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctors.

Proposition 4.11
.

If
{\eus{C}}
and
{\eus{D}}
have zero objects, then
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctors
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\eus{D}}
correspond bijectively to

\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\eus{D}}
that are reduced, in the sense that
F(\mathbf{0}_{\eus{C}})\cong\mathbf{0}_{\eus{D}}
, and are such that
\varphi
restricts to the identity on the subcategories
0_{b,c}\times{\eus{C}}(a,b)
and
{\eus{C}}(b,c)\times 0_{a,b}
.

Moreover,
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudotransformations
\zeta\colon E\Longrightarrow F
correspond bijectively to
\mathcal{V}
-pseudotransformations between the underlying
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors
(that is, there is no extra condition).

As in any enriched setting, we have the following construction.

Definition 4.12
.

If
{\eus{C}}
and
{\eus{D}}
are
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-categories, their product
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-category
{\eus{C}}\times{\eus{D}}
has objects
\mathbf{Ob}({\eus{C}})\times\mathbf{Ob}({\eus{D}})
and morphism
\mathcal{V}
-categories

({\eus{C}}\times{\eus{D}})((c,d),(c^{\prime},d^{\prime}))={\eus{C}}(c,c^{\prime})\times{\eus{D}}(d,d^{\prime}),

with the evident composition. If
{\eus{C}}
and
{\eus{D}}

have zero objects, so does

{\eus{C}}\times{\eus{D}}
, with zero object
(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0})
.

Similarly, if
{\eus{C}}
and
{\eus{D}}
are
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-categories, we have a
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-
2
-category

{\eus{C}}\wedge{\eus{D}}
with objects
\mathbf{Ob}({\eus{C}})\times\mathbf{Ob}({\eus{D}})
and with

({\eus{C}}\wedge{\eus{D}})((c,d),(c^{\prime},d^{\prime}))={\eus{C}}(c,c^{\prime})\wedge{\eus{D}}(d,d^{\prime})

as the
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-category of morphisms.

The following remark allows us to regard
2
-categories as
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-categories and establishes a convenient context.

Remark 4.13
.

The underlying set functor
\mathbb{U}\colon\mathcal{V}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
specified by
\mathbb{U}X=\mathcal{V}(\ast,X)
has left adjoint
\mathbb{V}\colon\bf{Set}\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}
specified by
\mathbb{V}S=\coprod_{s\in S}\ast
, the coproduct of copies of the terminal object
\ast
indexed on the elements of the set
S
. Thus

(4.14)

\mathbf{Set}(S,\mathbb{U}X)\cong\mathcal{V}(\mathbb{V}S,X).

When
\mathcal{V}
is strongly complete
[
16
, Definition 6.2]
, a mild condition that holds in all relevant examples,

\mathbb{V}
preserves finite limits. We can apply this with
\mathcal{V}
replaced by
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
, so that

(4.15)

\mathbf{\mathbf{Cat}}({\eus{B}},\mathbb{U}{\mathcal{X}})\cong\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})(\mathbb{V}{\eus{B}},{\mathcal{X}}).

for a category
{\eus{B}}
and a
\mathcal{V}
-category
{\mathcal{X}}
. We regard categories
{\eus{B}}
as discrete
2
-categories, meaning that they have only identity
2
-cells. Applying
\mathbb{V}
to the hom categories of
2
-categories allows us to change their enrichment from
\mathbf{Cat}
to
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
. Thus we may regard categories
{\eus{B}}
as

\mathcal{V}
-
2
-categories, and we agree to do so without change of notation. We then call
{\eus{B}}
a
discrete
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-category
. In our examples,
{\eus{B}}
has a zero object and therefore gives rise to a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-category.

4.2.
Algebras and pseudoalgebras
over
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-categories

Classically, algebras over a category
{\eus{D}}
enriched in based spaces
can be defined as enriched functors
{\mathcal{X}}\colon{\eus{D}}\to\mathcal{U}_{\ast}
.
The “action” of the category
{\eus{D}}
can be seen by translating this enriched functor into adjoint form as the data of a family of compatible continuous maps
{\eus{D}}(a,b)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(a)\to{\mathcal{X}}(b)
indexed by objects
a,b
of
{\eus{D}}
. Similarly, algebras over an operad
{\mathcal{O}}
in
\mathcal{U}
can be defined either via action maps
{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times X^{j}\to X
or equivalently via a map of operads from
{\mathcal{O}}
to the endomorphism operad
\mathrm{End}(X)
.

Remark 4.16
.

This second interpretation can always be given when the ambient category is closed,
as we have assumed.
Indeed, if
\mathcal{V}
is closed, it has an internal hom object
\underline{\mathcal{V}}(V,W)
for each pair of objects and an adjunction

\mathcal{V}(V\times W,Z)\cong\mathcal{V}(V,\underline{\mathcal{V}}(W,Z)).

Each object
V
then has an endomorphism operad
\mathrm{End}(V)
with

\mathrm{End}(V)(j)=\underline{\mathcal{V}}(V^{j},V).

An action of an operad
{\mathcal{O}}
in
\mathcal{V}
on an object
V\in\mathcal{V}
is then the same as a map of operads
{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{End}(V)
. This gives an adjoint specification of
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras.

As
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
is cartesian closed (see
Remark 2.4
),
we will write
\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V})
for the resulting
\mathcal{V}
-2-category.
Similarly,
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
is closed by
Remark 2.9
, and we will write
\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
for the resulting
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-category.

If
{\eus{C}}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-category, it is natural to define a
{\eus{C}}
-algebra
{\mathcal{X}}
to be a
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-functor

{\mathcal{X}}\colon{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V})
. If
{\eus{C}}
has a zero object,
we say that
{\mathcal{X}}
is reduced if
{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{0})=\ast
. We have the following enhancement of Propositions

4.4
and
4.11
.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
{\eus{C}}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-2-category with zero object. Then

(1)

\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-functors
{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
correspond bijectively to reduced
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-functors

{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V})
.

(2)

\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctors
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})}
correspond bijectively to reduced
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V})}
such that
\varphi
restricts to the identity on the subcategories
0_{b,c}\times{\eus{C}}(a,b)
and
{\eus{C}}(b,c)\times 0_{a,b}
.

Proof.

To prove (1), let
{\mathcal{X}}\colon{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V})
be a reduced
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-functor. Then for
c\in{\eus{C}}
, the adjoint of the map

{\eus{C}}(\mathbf{0},c)\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V})({\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{0}),{\mathcal{X}}(c))

endows
{\mathcal{X}}(c)
with a basepoint:

\ast\cong{\eus{C}}(\mathbf{0},c)\times{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{0})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}(c).

Functoriality can then be used to check that this indeed gives rise to a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-functor
{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
. For the converse we apply
Proposition 4.4
with
{\eus{D}}=\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
, and then forget the basepoints to get a map with target
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
.

The argument for (2) is similar, with the caveat that the condition on
\varphi
is necessary to ensure that we do get a map with target
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
.
∎

Definition 4.18
.

Let
{\eus{C}}
be a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-category.
A (strict)
{\eus{C}}
-algebra is a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-functor
{\mathcal{X}}\colon{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
.
Unpacking the definition in adjoint form, this consists of a function that assigns a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-category
{\mathcal{X}}(c)
to each object
c
of
{\eus{C}}
,
together with action
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functors

\theta\colon{\eus{C}}(c,d)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(c)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}(d)

such that the unit and composition diagrams of
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functors

\textstyle{(\ast\amalg\ast)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{c}\wedge\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(c,c)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}

and

\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(d,e)\wedge{\eus{C}}(c,d)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge\theta}
\scriptstyle{\circ\wedge\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(d,e)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(c,e)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}(e)}

commute.

Remark 4.19
.

The unit diagram can equally well be expressed as a diagram of
\mathcal{V}
-functors, with source
\ast\times{\mathcal{X}}(c)
.

We do not discuss general
{\eus{C}}
-pseudoalgebras here, leaving such consideration for
[
14
]
.
However, we will need a version of pseudoalgebras in the special case of
{\eus{C}}=\mathscr{F}_{G}
starting in
Section 7
. We define these now.

Definition 4.20
.

Let
{\eus{C}}
be a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-category. A
weak
{\eus{C}}
-pseudoalgebra

{\mathcal{X}}
is a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctor
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})}
. Unpacking the definition in adjoint form, this

consists of a function that assigns a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-category
{\mathcal{X}}(c)
to each
c\in{\eus{C}}
, together with action

\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functors

\theta:{\eus{C}}(c,d)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(c)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}(d)

and invertible
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations

\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(d,e)\wedge{\eus{C}}(c,d)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge\theta}
\scriptstyle{\circ\wedge\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 8.19447pt\ \varphi}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(d,e)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(c,e)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}(e)}

which are the identity when either morphism is the identity 1-cell. Moreover, the
\varphi
are required to be coherent as in
Definition 4.5
.

Remark 4.21
.

The strictness with respect to basepoints encoded in the definitions above expresses the intuition that additive zero objects should behave strictly in multiplicative structures. The strictness with respect to identity arrows expresses the intuition that identity operations should be the identity.

Remark 4.22
.

Though it is not the choice made in this article, it is also possible to consider less general
{\eus{C}}
-pseudoalgebras. As
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
is closed, these can be described as
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctors
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})}
that are strictly functorial
on a subcategory
{\eus{B}}\subset{\eus{C}}
.
Different choices of
{\eus{B}}
lead to several possible versions of pseudoalgebras. While we will only deal with the weakest possible variant of pseudoalgebras here, the different types of pseudomorphisms described in the next definition will play an important role. We will return to consider pseudoalgebras in more detail in
[
14
]
.

Definition 4.23
.

Let
{\eus{C}}
be a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-category and
{\eus{B}}\subset{\eus{C}}
a subcategory, and let

{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
be
{\eus{C}}
-algebras (or weak
{\eus{C}}
-pseudoalgebras).
A
({\eus{C}},{\eus{B}})
-pseudomorphism
,

F\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 28.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
is a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudotransformation between
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-functors (or
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctors) that is strict when restricted to
{\eus{B}}
. Unpacking this definition in adjoint form, this
consists of
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functors

F(c)\colon{\mathcal{X}}(c)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}(c)

together with invertible
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations
\delta
as in the diagram

(4.24)

\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(c,d)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge F(c)}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 4.09723pt\delta}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}(c,d)\wedge{\mathcal{Y}}(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(d)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}(d)}

that are the identity transformation on the subcategory
{\eus{B}}(c,d)\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(c)
.
The
\delta
must satisfy the relevant equalities of pasting diagrams relating to composition in
{\eus{C}}
.
If
\delta
is always the identity, then
F
is a (strict)
{\eus{C}}
-map
.
Thus the condition on
{\eus{B}}
says that
F
restricts to a strict
{\eus{B}}
-algebra map.
We refer to the extreme case, in which
{\eus{B}}
consists only of identity morphisms and basepoint morphisms, as
weak
{\eus{C}}
-pseudomorphisms
.

The following notion will also be needed later.

Definition 4.25
.

A
{\eus{C}}
-pseudomorphism
\textstyle{F\colon{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}}
of
{\eus{C}}
-pseudoalgebras is said to be a
level equivalence
if each component
F(c)\colon{\mathcal{X}}(c)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}(c)
is an (internal) equivalence of
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-categories.

Definition 4.26
.

A
{\eus{C}}
-transformation

\omega
between
{\eus{C}}
-pseudomorphisms
E
and
F

consists of
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations
\omega_{c}\colon E(c)\Longrightarrow F(c)
that
are suitably compatible with the
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations
\delta^{E}
and
\delta^{F}
, as in
[
10
, Section 3.10]
.
We do
not
require the
\omega_{c}
to be isomorphisms.

This definition is just a translation of the notion of a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-modification between
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudotransformations. We will only use the explicit description just given.

These notions assemble to form various 2-categories of interest to us.

Notation 4.27
.

For a given
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-category
{\eus{C}}
with a subcategory
{\eus{B}}\subset{\eus{C}}
, we define the following 2-categories, with
{\eus{C}}
-transformations as the 2-cells in all cases.

•

{\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{Alg}}
of
{\eus{C}}
-algebras and strict
{\eus{C}}
-maps;

•

{\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{Alg_{ps{\eus{B}}}}}
of
{\eus{C}}
-algebras and
({\eus{C}},{\eus{B}})
-pseudomorphisms;

•

{\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{PsAlg}}
of weak
{\eus{C}}
-pseudoalgebras and weak
{\eus{C}}
-pseudomorphisms.

We have inclusions

{\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{Alg}}\subset{\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{Alg_{ps{\eus{B}}}}}\subset{\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{PsAlg}}.

This article largely focuses on the 2-categories
{\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{Alg_{ps{\eus{B}}}}}

in the case that
{\eus{C}}
is a category of operators. As noted in Notations
5.14
and
6.17
, we will then fix the relevant
{\eus{B}}
and drop the
subscript
{\eus{B}}
from the notation.
However, pulling back along the section
\zeta_{G}
in
Section 7
will land in a category of type
{\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{PsAlg}}
, and the strictification theorem in
Section 8
will land in a category of type
{\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{Alg}}
.

We end this subsection with two constructions on (pseudo)algebras.

Notation 4.28
.

Given a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctor
\textstyle{\xi\colon{\eus{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\,{\eus{C}}}
and a
{\eus{C}}
-pseudoalgebra
{\mathcal{X}}
, we define the
{\eus{D}}
-pseudoalgebra
\xi^{*}{\mathcal{X}}
as the composite

\textstyle{{\eus{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{X}}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast}).}

This construction extends to
{\eus{C}}
-pseudomorphisms and
{\eus{C}}
-transformations, giving a 2-functor

\xi^{*}\colon{\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{PsAlg}}\longrightarrow{\eus{D}}\text{-}{\bf{PsAlg}}.

If
\xi
is a strict
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-functor, this construction restricts to give a 2-functor

\xi^{*}\colon{\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{Alg}}\longrightarrow{\eus{D}}\text{-}{\bf{Alg}}.

The 2-functor
\xi^{*}
preserves level equivalences of pseudoalgebras as defined in
Definition 4.25
.

Definition 4.29
.

Suppose given

\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-categories

{\eus{C}}
and
{\eus{D}}
. Given a
{\eus{C}}
-pseudoalgebra
{\mathcal{X}}
and an

{\eus{D}}
-pseudoalgebra
{\mathcal{Y}}
, we define their
external smash product

{\mathcal{X}}\,\overline{\wedge}\,{\mathcal{Y}}
to be the

{\eus{C}}\wedge{\eus{D}}
-pseudoalgebra given by the composite

\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\wedge{\eus{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\wedge{\mathcal{Y}}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})\wedge\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wedge}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast}).}

If
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
are strict algebras, so is
{\mathcal{X}}\,\overline{\wedge}\,{\mathcal{Y}}
.

This construction extends to pseudomorphisms and transformations.

4.3.
Permutative structures on
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-categories

In order to encode multiplicative structures on algebras, we use monoidal structures on
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-categories, as defined in this section. Even in the case when
\mathcal{V}=\bf{Set}
, what we present here is not the most general definition of a symmetric monoidal structure on a 2-category (see
[
3
,
17
]
). Here, we present a rather strict notion in which the monoidal product is allowed to be a pseudofunctor, but must strictly satisfy associativity and unitality; while the symmetry is allowed to be a pseudotransformation, it must satisfy the symmetry axiom strictly.

Definition 4.30
.

A
strict pseudo-monoidal
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-category
consists of a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-category
{\eus{C}}
together with an object
I
, and a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctor

\textstyle{\circledast\colon{\eus{C}}\wedge{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\eus{C}}

that is strictly associative and strictly unital with respect to
I
. A
pseudo-permutative
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-category
is a
strict pseudo-monoidal
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-category together with a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudotransformation

\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\wedge{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\wedge{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\tau}

such that the following three axioms hold.

(i)

The following pasting diagram is equal to the identity of
\circledast
:

If

.

Remark 4.31
.

Classically, a permutative category is a symmetric strict monoidal category, strict meaning that the product is strictly associative and unital.
The definition above is similar, just done in the context of the 2-category of
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-categories,
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctors, and
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudotransformations.
Thus “strict pseudo-monoidal” here means that
\circledast
is a strictly associative and unital operation given by a
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor; it respects composition only up to coherent isomorphisms.
The standard coherence theorem for permutative categories still applies in this case: for any permutation
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
, there exists a unique composite of instances of
\tau
that fits in the diagram below.

\textstyle{{\eus{C}}^{\wedge k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast^{k}}
\scriptstyle{t_{\sigma}}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}^{\wedge k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast^{k}}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\tau_{\sigma}}

Here
\circledast^{k}
denotes the
k
-ary product induced by iterating
\circledast
, and the map
t_{\sigma}
sends a
k
-tuple
(c_{1},\dots,c_{k})
to
(c_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},\dots,c_{\sigma^{-1}(k)})
. The 1-cell component of
\tau_{\sigma}
is the (unique) composite of instances of the 1-cell of
\tau
that reorders

c_{1}\circledast\cdots\circledast c_{k}\longrightarrow c_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\circledast\cdots\circledast c_{\sigma^{-1}(k)}.

.

5.
The multicategory of
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras and the multifunctor
\mathbb{R}

Nonequivariantly, categories of operators were introduced on the space level in order to mediate the passage from algebras over an
E_{\infty}
-operad in spaces to special
\mathscr{F}
-spaces when comparing the operadic and Segalic infinite loop space machines
[
37
]
. Algebras over a category of operators are a generalization of both
\mathscr{F}
-spaces (aka
\Gamma
-spaces) and algebras over an
E_{\infty}
-operad.
In this section we discuss their categorical analogues. Equivariant categories of operators were studied in
[
46
,
39
]
, and their categorical analogues will be introduced in
Section 6
.

We show that for a category of operators
{\mathscr{D}}
with pseudo-commutative structure, as defined in
Section 5.2
, there is a multicategory of algebras over
{\mathscr{D}}
. For categories of operators coming from operads, the necessary structure arises from a pseudo-commutative structure on the operad, as in
Definition 3.10
. We make all of this precise in this section.

5.1.
Categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}

The definitions in this subsection are categorical analogues of definitions in
[
37
]
. We give the definitions in the setting of
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-categories.

Definition 5.1
.

Recall that
\mathscr{F}
denotes the category of based sets
\mathbf{n}=\{0,1,\dots,n\}
with basepoint
0
and let
\Pi
denote its subcategory of morphisms
\phi\colon\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}
such that
|\phi^{-1}(j)|=0
or
1
for
1\leq j\leq n
. We often use the abbreviated notation
\phi_{j}=|\phi^{-1}(j)|
. We regard
\mathscr{F}
and
\Pi
as discrete
2
-categories, meaning that they have only identity
2
-cells. Via
Remark 4.13
, we then regard them as

\mathcal{V}_{*}
-
2
-categories.

Definition 5.2
.

A
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
-category of operators

{\mathscr{D}}
over
\mathscr{F}
, abbreviated

\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
-
\mathbf{CO}
over
\mathscr{F}
, is a
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-category whose objects are the based sets
\mathbf{n}
for
n\geq 0
together with
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-functors

\textstyle{\Pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{F}}

such that
\iota
and
\xi
are the identity on objects and
\xi\circ\iota
is the inclusion.
A morphism

\nu\colon{\mathscr{D}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}
of
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
-
\mathbf{CO}
s over
\mathscr{F}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-2-functor over
\mathscr{F}
and under
\Pi
.

Definition 5.3
.

A
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
-
\mathbf{CO}

{\mathscr{D}}
over
\mathscr{F}
is
reduced
if
\mathbf{0}
is a zero object, and we then say that
{\mathscr{D}}

is a
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-category of operators
over
\mathscr{F}
. We shall restrict attention to
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}
.

Remark 5.4
.

By Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
, if
{\mathscr{D}}
is a
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-
\mathbf{CO}
over
\mathscr{F}
, then

{\mathscr{D}}
is a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-category and
\iota
and
\xi
are
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-functors; that is,
{\mathscr{D}}
is a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-category over
\mathscr{F}
and under
\Pi
.
A morphism
{\mathscr{D}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}
of reduced
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
-
\mathbf{CO}
s over
\mathscr{F}
is necessarily reduced since it must send
\mathbf{0}
to
\mathbf{0}
; thus it is a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-functor over
\mathscr{F}
and under
\Pi
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an operad in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
. We can associate to it a category of operators
{\mathscr{D}}={\mathscr{D}}({\mathcal{O}})
over
\mathscr{F}
by letting

{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=\coprod_{\phi\in\mathscr{F}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}\ \prod_{1\leq j\leq n}{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j}).

Composition is induced from the structural maps
\gamma
of
{\mathcal{O}}
. To write formulas instead of diagrams, we use elementwise notation, writing
c_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})
for objects and morphisms in
{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})
. For
(\phi,c_{1},\dots,c_{n})\colon\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}
and
(\psi,d_{1},\dots,d_{p})\colon\mathbf{n}\longrightarrow\mathbf{p}
, define

(5.5)

(\psi,d_{1},\dots,d_{p})\circ(\phi,c_{1},\dots,c_{n})=\Big(\psi\circ\phi,\prod_{1\leq j\leq p}\gamma(d_{j};\prod_{\psi(i)=j}c_{i})\rho_{j}(\psi,\phi)\Big).

The
c_{i}
with
\psi(i)=j
are ordered by the natural order on their indices
i
,
and
\rho_{j}(\psi,\phi)
is that permutation of
|(\psi\circ\phi)^{-1}(j)|
letters which converts
the natural ordering of
(\psi\circ\phi)^{-1}(j)
as a subset of
\{1,\dots,m\}
to
its ordering obtained by regarding it as
\coprod_{\psi(i)=j}\phi^{-1}(i)
, so ordered
that elements of
\phi^{-1}(i)
precede elements of
\phi^{-1}(i^{\prime})
if
i<i^{\prime}
and
each
\phi^{-1}(i)
has its natural ordering as a subset of
\{1,\dots,m\}
. When it is clear which
\phi
and
\psi
are being composed, we abbreviate the notation for the permutation
\rho_{j}(\psi,\phi)
to
\rho_{j}
.

Proposition 5.6
.

[
37
, Construction 4.1]
The above specification makes
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathcal{O}})
into a category of operators over
\mathscr{F}
, and it is reduced if
{\mathcal{O}}
is reduced.

Proof.

The map
\xi\colon{\mathscr{D}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}

sends
(\phi,c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
to
\phi
.
Recall that any morphism
\phi
in
\Pi
satisfies
\phi_{j}\leq 1
for
j>0
.
The inclusion
\iota\colon\Pi\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}
sends

\phi\colon\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}
to
(\phi,c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
, where
c_{j}=\mathds{1}\in{\mathcal{O}}(1)
if
\phi_{j}=1
and

c_{j}=\ast\in{\mathcal{O}}(0)
if
\phi_{j}=0
.
∎

5.2.
Pseudo-commutative categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}

In analogy with our definition of pseudo-commutativity of an operad, we define a compatible notion of
pseudo-commutativity of a category of operators
{\mathscr{D}}
over
\mathscr{F}
.
The categories
\Pi
and
\mathscr{F}
are permutative
under the smash product of finite based sets, as we now recall.
On objects,
\mathbf{m}\wedge\mathbf{p}
is defined to be
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p}
. Given
\phi\in\mathscr{F}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
and
\psi\in\mathscr{F}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})

their smash product
\phi\wedge\psi\in\mathscr{F}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p},\mathbf{n}\mathbf{q})
is defined, in parallel to
(3.6)
, as the restriction of the map

\phi\times\psi\colon\mathbf{m}\times\mathbf{p}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}\times\mathbf{q}

along the (based) inclusion
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{m}\times\mathbf{p}
that is
given by
(3.5)
away from the basepoint.
The symmetry
isomorphisms
\tau
are given by
the permutations
\tau_{m,p}
of
Definition 3.8
which reorder the sets
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p}

from lexicographic to reversed lexicographic ordering.
The inclusion of
\Sigma
in
\mathscr{F}
identifies
\otimes
with
\wedge
. We will continue to use the symbol
\otimes
for emphasis when dealing with permutations.

Recall the notion of pseudo-permutative
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-category from
Definition 4.30
.

Definition 5.7
.

A
pseudo-commutative structure

on
{\mathscr{D}}
is a pseudo-permutative structure
({\mathscr{D}},\mathbf{1},\circledast,\tau)
such that

(1)

\circledast
restricts to
\wedge
on

\Pi\wedge\Pi
and projects to
\wedge
on
\mathscr{F}

(in the sense that
\xi\circ\circledast=\wedge\circ(\xi\wedge\xi)
);

(2)

\circledast
restricts to a strict
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-functor on
\Pi\wedge{\mathscr{D}}
and
{\mathscr{D}}\wedge\Pi
;

(3)

\tau
restricts to the symmetry on
\Pi
given in
Definition 3.8
.

We identify the pieces of this definition explicitly. First note that condition (
1
)
implies in particular that
\circledast=\wedge
on objects.
The fact that
\circledast
is a
\mathcal{V}_{*}
-pseudofunctor means that there is a collection of invertible
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations

(5.8)

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\wedge\circledast}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge t\wedge\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\ \vartheta}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n}\wedge\mathbf{r},\mathbf{p}\wedge\mathbf{s})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\wedge\mathbf{q},\mathbf{n}\wedge\mathbf{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ\wedge\circ}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{s})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\wedge\mathbf{q},\mathbf{p}\wedge\mathbf{s})}

relating
\circledast
to composition.

Condition (
2
), which is necessary for
Theorem 6.14
, translates to requiring

\vartheta
to be the identity (so that the diagram commutes) when either both
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})
and
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s})
are restricted to
\Pi
or both
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
and
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})
are restricted to
\Pi
.

Writing this out elementwise, on 1-cells it means that, whenever the composites are defined,

(c\circledast d)\circ(a\wedge b)=(c\circ a)\circledast(d\circ b)

and

(a\wedge b)\circ(c\circledast d)=(a\circ c)\circledast(b\circ d)

where
c
and
d
are morphisms of
{\mathscr{D}}
and
a
and
b
are morphisms of
\Pi
. We think of this as saying that the monoidal structure on
\circledast
is
strict relative to
\Pi
.
As the pseudofunctoriality constraint for
\circledast
, the
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations
\vartheta
must satisfy a condition with respect to triple composition. The condition on
\circledast
being strictly associative imposes another set of conditions on

\vartheta
.

Condition (
3
) means that
the 1-cell constraint of
\tau
at the object
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p})
is the permutation
\tau_{m,p}
thought of as a morphism in
{\mathscr{D}}
, and
the pseudonaturality constraint is an invertible
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformation

(5.9)

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\circ t}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hat{\tau}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{p}\mathbf{m},\mathbf{q}\mathbf{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\tau_{m,p})^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p},\mathbf{n}\mathbf{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\tau_{n,q})_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\mathbf{n})}

that is the identity when restricted to the subcategory
\Pi(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge\Pi(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
.
These pseudonaturality constraints must be compatible with composition in
{\mathscr{D}}
and the pseudofunctoriality constraint
\vartheta
.

Remark 5.10
.

For later use, we emphasize a particular consequence of the strictness relative to
\Pi
here.
Let
a\in\Pi(\mathbf{m}^{\prime},\mathbf{m})
and
b\in\Pi(\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\mathbf{q})
. The compatibility of
\vartheta
with triple composition together with condition (
2
) implies that

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\wedge\circledast}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge\mathrm{id}\wedge a^{*}\!\wedge b^{*}}

\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge(a\wedge b)^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\prime},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\mathbf{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\wedge\circledast}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge t\wedge\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\ \vartheta}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n}\wedge\mathbf{r},\mathbf{p}\wedge\mathbf{s})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\wedge\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}\wedge\mathbf{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\circ\wedge\circ}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\prime},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\mathbf{s})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\wedge\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}\wedge\mathbf{s})}

is equal to

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\wedge\circledast}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge t\wedge\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\ \vartheta}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n}\wedge\mathbf{r},\mathbf{p}\wedge\mathbf{s})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\wedge\mathbf{q},\mathbf{n}\wedge\mathbf{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ\wedge\circ}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{s})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\wedge\mathbf{q},\mathbf{p}\wedge\mathbf{s})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(a\wedge b)^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\prime},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\mathbf{s})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\wedge\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}\wedge\mathbf{s})}

The two unlabeled squares have instances of
\vartheta
that are the identity because of condition (
2
). The boundaries on both diagrams are equal since composition in
{\mathscr{D}}
is strict. This equality expresses a condition on
\vartheta
for when the first terms of a triple composition come from
\Pi
. There are similar conditions for when the middle and the last terms come from
\Pi
.

Definition 5.11
.

We define a map
(\Psi,\mu)\colon{\mathscr{D}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}
of pseudo-commutative categories of operators to be a symmetric monoidal 2-functor (
Definition 4.32
) such that
\Psi
is a map of
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-
\mathbf{CO}
s over
\mathscr{F}
and the restriction of
\mu
to the subcategory
\Pi\wedge\Pi
is the identity transformation.

We defer the proof of the following theorem to
Section 12
. It ensures that our definitions of pseudo-commutativity for operads and for their associated categories of operators are compatible. The verification is essentially combinatorial bookkeeping and is painstaking rather than hard.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a pseudo-commutative operad in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
. Then
{\mathscr{D}}={\mathscr{D}}({\mathcal{O}})
is a pseudo-commutative category of operators.

Remark 5.13
.

The construction is functorial. With the appropriate definition of a pseudo-commutative morphism
{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
of pseudo-commutative operads,
the map
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathcal{O}})\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}(\mathcal{P})
is pseudo-commutative. In analogy with
Lemma 3.12
, when
{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{P}
are chaotic, any morphism of operads
between them is necessarily pseudo-commutative.

5.3.
The multicategory of
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras

For a category of operators
{\mathscr{D}}
over
\mathscr{F}
, we consider
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras as defined in
Definition 4.18
.
As indicated in
Remark 4.22
, there is a more general notion of
{\mathscr{D}}
-pseudoalgebra, but
we defer discussion of that to
[
14
]
. Recall that we have the notions of
({\mathscr{D}},\Pi)
-pseudomorphism and
{\mathscr{D}}
-transformation from Definitions
4.23
and
4.26
.

Notation 5.14
.

For a category of operators
{\mathscr{D}}
over
\mathscr{F}
, we denote by

{\mathscr{D}}\text{-}\bf{Alg_{ps}}
the 2-category of strict
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras,
({\mathscr{D}},\Pi)
-pseudomorphisms, and
{\mathscr{D}}
-transformations. This 2-category was denoted by
{{\mathscr{D}}\text{-}\bf{Alg_{ps}}}_{\Pi}
in
Notation 4.27
, but we now fix
{\eus{B}}=\Pi
and drop it from the notation.

This notion, with its strictness with respect to
\Pi
, is
essential
for the construction of
\mathbb{P}
in the left column of
(1.2)
, as we explain in
Remark 6.3
.

Let
{\mathscr{D}}
be a reduced pseudo-commutative category of operators over
\mathscr{F}
.
We define the multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathscr{D}})
of
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras, which amounts to
defining the
k
-ary morphisms. As said before, we set it up to have its objects be
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras, although with only slightly more work we could equally well
have set it up to have its objects be
{\mathscr{D}}
-pseudoalgebras.

Recall from
Section 4.2
that a
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebra is given by a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-functor
{\mathcal{X}}\colon{\mathscr{D}}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
, which can be expressed in adjoint form
as in
Definition 4.18
. Thus the action of
{\mathscr{D}}
on
{\mathcal{X}}
is given by
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functors

\theta\colon{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{n}).

Let
{\mathscr{D}}^{\wedge k}
denote the
k
-fold smash power. Following
Definition 4.29
, given
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras
{\mathcal{X}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{X}}_{k}
, we have the external smash product
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\overline{\wedge}\dots\overline{\wedge}{\mathcal{X}}_{k}
. It sends an object
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{n}_{k})
of
{\mathscr{D}}^{\wedge k}
to
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}(\mathbf{n}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathcal{X}}_{k}(\mathbf{n}_{k})
, with action map
\theta^{k}
given by the composite

\textstyle{\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}_{i},\mathbf{n}_{i})\wedge\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}_{i},\mathbf{n}_{i})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bigwedge\theta}
\textstyle{\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{n}_{i}),}

where the first map is the appropriate shuffle. For a
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}
, we consider the
{\mathscr{D}}^{\wedge k}
-pseudoalgebra

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}^{\wedge k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast^{k}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast}).}

The conditions in
Definition 5.7
imply that
{\mathcal{Y}}\circ\circledast^{k}
restricts to a strict
\Pi^{\wedge k}
-algebra. Since
\Pi^{\wedge k}
is discrete, this is a
functor from
\Pi^{\wedge k}
to the underlying
1
-category of
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
.

Definition 5.15
.

Let
{\mathscr{D}}
be a reduced pseudo-commutative category of operators over
\mathscr{F}
.
We define a (symmetric) multicategory

Unpacking the action of

The top and bottom trapezoids commute by the definition of
F{\sigma}
. The left two trapezoids commute trivially. The bottom right trapezoid commutes
since

is a (strict)

-algebra.
The top right trapezoid is filled by an invertible

-transformation
