given by the pseudonaturality constraint of the appropriate (unique) composition of instances of the pseudocommutativity of
{\mathscr{D}}
.

Recall
Notation 4.28
.

Theorem 5.20
.

Let
(\Psi,\mu)\colon{\mathscr{D}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}
be a map of pseudo-commutative categories of operators (
Definition 5.11
). Then pulling back along
\Psi
induces a (symmetric) multifunctor

\Psi^{*}\colon\mathbf{Mult}(\mathscr{E})\longrightarrow\mathbf{Mult}({\mathscr{D}}).

Proof.

Given a
k
-ary morphism
F\colon({\mathcal{X}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{X}}_{k})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}
in
\mathbf{Mult}(\mathscr{E})
, the multimorphism
\Psi^{*}(F)
is defined as the pasting

\textstyle{\mathscr{E}^{\wedge k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi^{\wedge k}}
\scriptstyle{\circledast^{k}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{k}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}^{\wedge k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathcal{X}}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\circledast^{k}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 8.19447ptF}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})^{\wedge k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wedge^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast}),}

where the 2-cell
\mu_{k}
denotes an appropriate composite of instances of
\mu
, which is unique by the associativity of
\mu
. Compatibility with the identity, composition, and the symmetric group action follows from the axioms in
Definition 5.11
.
∎

5.4.
Definition of the functor
\mathbb{R}

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a reduced operad in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
with associated
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-
\mathbf{CO}

{\mathscr{D}}
over
\mathscr{F}
. We define a
2
-functor
\mathbb{R}\colon{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}\text{-}\bf{Alg_{ps}}
with the property that for an
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
, the resulting
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebra is defined on objects by
\mathbf{n}\mapsto{\mathcal{A}}^{n}
, and we show in
Section 5.5
that
\mathbb{R}
extends to a multifunctor.

We have the
2
-category
\Pi\text{-}{\bf Alg}
of

\Pi
-algebras,
\Pi
-morphisms, and
\Pi
-transformations, and we have the evident
2
-functor
\mathbb{R}\colon\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})\longrightarrow\Pi\text{-}{\bf Alg}
that sends a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-category
{\mathcal{A}}
to the
\Pi
-algebra
\Pi\longrightarrow\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
whose value at
\mathbf{n}
is
{\mathcal{A}}^{n}
. The injections, projections, and permutations of
\Pi
are sent to basepoint inclusions, projections, and permutations of the
{\mathcal{A}}^{n}
.
For a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functor
F\colon{\mathcal{A}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
,
\mathbb{R}F
has component
F^{n}\colon{\mathcal{A}}^{n}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}^{n}
at
\mathbf{n}
.

The notation
\mathbb{R}
records that
\mathbb{R}
is right adjoint to the
2
-functor
\mathbb{L}
that sends a
\Pi
-algebra to its first
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-category,
\mathbb{L}({\mathcal{X}})={\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{1})

[
34
, §1]
.
We claim that
\mathbb{R}
extends to a
2
-functor
\mathbb{R}\colon{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}\text{-}\bf{Alg_{ps}}
. When starting operadically, it is convenient to use
\times
instead of
\wedge
.
For an
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
, we give
\mathbb{R}{\mathcal{A}}
a
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebra structure via a
\mathcal{V}
-functor

\theta\colon{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\times{\mathcal{A}}^{m}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}^{n}

that is compatible with basepoints and therefore descends to a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functor on the smash product.
Writing
\phi_{j}=|\phi^{-1}(j)|
again, this
\mathcal{V}
-functor can be expressed as a composition

\textstyle{\coprod\limits_{\phi\colon\mathbf{m}\to\mathbf{n}}\Big(\prod\limits_{1\leq j\leq n}{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\Big)\times{\mathcal{A}}^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coprod\limits_{\phi\colon\mathbf{m}\to\mathbf{n}}\,\prod\limits_{1\leq j\leq n}\big({\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\times{\mathcal{A}}^{\phi_{j}}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}^{n}.}

On each component
\phi\colon\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}
,
the first map reorders
{\mathcal{A}}^{m}\cong{\mathcal{A}}^{\phi_{0}}\times{\mathcal{A}}^{\phi_{1}}\times\dots\times{\mathcal{A}}^{\phi_{n}}
and projects away
{\mathcal{A}}^{\phi_{0}}
, while the second map
is the product of
n
algebra structure maps
\theta(\phi_{j})\colon{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\times{\mathcal{A}}^{\phi_{j}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
.

We next define
\mathbb{R}
on morphisms. Thus let
\textstyle{(F,\partial_{*})\colon{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}}
be a pseudomorphism
of
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras.
We define a
({\mathscr{D}},\Pi)
-pseudomorphism

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 51.34195pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-51.34195pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{R}(F,\partial_{*})=(\mathbb{R}F,\delta)\colon\mathbb{R}{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.34195pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 75.34195pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{R}{\mathcal{B}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

The required
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformation

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathcal{A}}^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge F^{m}}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 16.38895pt\delta_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathcal{B}}^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}}

is obtained by passage to smash products from a coproduct of whiskerings of

\textstyle{\prod\limits_{1\leq j\leq n}\big({\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\times{\mathcal{A}}^{\phi_{j}}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod\limits\mathrm{id}\times F^{\phi_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\prod\theta}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 12.2917pt\prod\partial}
\textstyle{\prod\limits_{1\leq j\leq n}\big({\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\times{\mathcal{B}}^{\phi_{j}}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod\theta}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}}

along the reordering morphisms
\Big(\prod\limits_{1\leq j\leq n}{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\Big)\times{\mathcal{A}}^{m}\longrightarrow\prod\limits_{1\leq j\leq n}\big({\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\times{\mathcal{A}}^{\phi_{j}}\big)
.

For an
{\mathcal{O}}
-transformation
\omega\colon E\Longrightarrow F
, we define the component of the
{\mathscr{D}}
-transformation
\mathbb{R}\omega
at
\mathbf{n}
as
\omega^{n}
.
We leave it to the reader to fill in the details of the proof of the following result.

Proposition 5.21
.

The above data specifies a
2
-functor
\mathbb{R}\colon{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}\text{-}\bf{Alg_{ps}}
.

5.5.
The proof that
\mathbb{R}
is a symmetric multifunctor

Now let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a reduced pseudo-commutative operad in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})

with associated pseudo-commutative category of operators
{\mathscr{D}}
.

Theorem 5.22
.

The
2
-functor
\mathbb{R}\colon{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}\text{-}\bf{Alg_{ps}}

extends to a symmetric multifunctor
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})\longrightarrow\mathbf{Mult}({\mathscr{D}})
.

Proof.

Let

(F,\delta_{i})\colon({\mathcal{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{A}}_{k})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
,
be a
k
-ary morphism in
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})
. Here
F
is a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functor
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
and
\delta_{i}
is given by
\mathcal{V}
-transformations
\delta_{i}(n)
as in
(3.15)
.
We must construct a
k
-ary morphism

\mathbb{R}(F,\delta_{i})=(\mathbb{R}F,\mathbb{R}\delta)\colon(\mathbb{R}{\mathcal{A}}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{R}{\mathcal{A}}_{k})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}{\mathcal{B}}
as in
Definition 5.15
.

Writing
n=n_{1}\cdots n_{k}
as before, the component

\mathbb{R}{\mathcal{A}}_{1}(\mathbf{n}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbb{R}{\mathcal{A}}_{k}(\mathbf{n}_{k})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{n})

of
\mathbb{R}F
is

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{n_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathcal{A}}_{k}^{n_{k}}\xrightarrow{\ell}({\mathcal{A}}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathcal{A}}_{k})^{n}\xrightarrow{F^{n}}{\mathcal{B}}^{n},

where
\ell
is a based version of the map defined in
Definition 3.20
, using lexicographic ordering. Next, we specify the
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations
\mathbb{R}\delta
in the following specialization of diagram
(5.17)
.

(5.23)

\textstyle{\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}_{i},\mathbf{n}_{i})\wedge\bigwedge\limits_{i}{{\mathcal{A}}_{i}}^{m_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge\mathbb{R}{F}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 8.19447pt\ \mathbb{R}\delta}
\textstyle{\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}_{i},\mathbf{n}_{i})\wedge{\mathcal{B}}^{m}}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\wedge\mathrm{id}}
\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}_{i},\mathbf{n}_{i})\wedge{\mathcal{A}}_{i}^{m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\bigwedge\limits_{i}\theta}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathcal{B}}^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathcal{A}}_{i}^{n_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}F}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}.}

Before passage to smash products, these transformations are constructed as disjoint unions of products of compositions of the
\delta_{i}(n)
’s.
To see this, consider, for instance, the case in which
k=2
and
\mathbf{n}_{1}=\mathbf{n}_{2}=\mathbf{1}
. We restrict further to the components
{\mathcal{O}}(m_{1})\subseteq{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{1})
and
{\mathcal{O}}(m_{2})\subseteq{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}_{2},\mathbf{1})
corresponding to the maps
\phi\colon\mathbf{m}_{1}\longrightarrow{\bf 1}
and
\psi\colon\mathbf{m}_{2}\longrightarrow{\bf 1}
that send all non-basepoint elements to 1. Then the
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformation
\mathbb{R}\delta
is obtained by passage to smash products from the 2-cell

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(m_{1})\times{\mathcal{O}}(m_{2})\times{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{m_{1}}\times{\mathcal{A}}_{2}^{m_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\mathbb{R}F}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(m_{1})\times{\mathcal{O}}(m_{2})\times{\mathcal{B}}^{m_{1}m_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\times\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(m_{1})\times{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{m_{1}}\times{\mathcal{O}}(m_{2})\times{\mathcal{A}}_{2}^{m_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta\times\theta}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(m_{1}m_{2})\times{\mathcal{B}}^{m_{1}m_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}}

of (
v
), the axiom for commutation of cells
\delta_{i}
and
\delta_{j}
, in
Section 11.2
, where
Definition 3.14
is completed.
Now consider the general case of
k=2
, with arbitrary
\mathbf{n}_{1}
and
\mathbf{n}_{2}
. For the component of
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{1})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{2})
indexed by maps
\phi\colon\mathbf{m}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}_{1}
and
\psi\colon\mathbf{m}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}_{2}
in
\mathscr{F}
, the required 2-cell is of the form

\textstyle{\prod\limits_{1\leq j\leq n_{1}}{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\times\prod\limits_{1\leq k\leq n_{2}}{\mathcal{O}}(\psi_{k})\times{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{m_{1}}\times{\mathcal{A}}_{2}^{m_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\Pi\theta\times\Pi\theta)\circ t}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\times\mathbb{R}F}
\textstyle{\prod\limits_{j,k}{\mathcal{O}}((\phi\wedge\psi)_{(j,k)})\times{\mathcal{B}}^{m_{1}m_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi\theta}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{n_{1}}\times{\mathcal{A}}_{2}^{n_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}F}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{n_{1}n_{2}}}

and is a product of 2-cells of the previous type. We note that
\mathbb{R}\delta
is the identity when
{\mathscr{D}}^{k}
is restricted to
\Pi^{k}
, by axioms (
i
) and (
iii
) in
Definition 3.14
. When
k=1
, the construction above recovers that of
Section 5.4
.
Axiom (
v
) of
Definition 3.14
implies that
\mathbb{R}
preserves composition.

We prove that
\mathbb{R}
is symmetric by a comparison of the definitions here with those of
Section 3.3
.
Remembering the lexicographic reordering, it is straightforward to check by comparison
of
(3.16)
with
(5.18)
that
\mathbb{R}(F\sigma)=(\mathbb{R}F)\sigma
for a
k
-ary morphism
(F,\delta_{i})
of
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})
.
The equality of pasting diagrams required to ensure that the
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations in
(\mathbb{R}(F,\delta_{i}))\sigma
and
\mathbb{R}((F,\delta_{i})\sigma)
are equal follows from
axiom (
v
) of
Definition 3.14
.
∎

6.
The multicategory of
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras and the multifunctor
\mathbb{P}

We introduced
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
-categories of operators in
Section 5
, as well as the multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathscr{D}})
associated to any pseudo-commutative category of operators
{\mathscr{D}}
.
In this section, we finally bring in equivariance, starting in
Section 6.1
, where we specialize the content of the previous sections to the category
G\mathcal{V}
of
G
-objects in
\mathcal{V}
. In
Section 6.2
we introduce
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V})
-categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
, the category of finite based
G
-sets.
A key idea here is
Construction 6.7
, which allows us to prolong from equivariant categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}
to equivariant categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
.
We introduce the multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathscr{D}}_{G})
in
Section 6.5
and extend the prolongation functor to a symmetric multifunctor in
Section 6.6
.
Much of this section is precisely parallel to the previous one.

6.1.
G\mathcal{V}
-categories and
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-categories

So far, equivariance has not entered into the picture and yet everything we have done applies equally well equivariantly, as we now explain. Start again with a category
\mathcal{V}
satisfying Assumptions
2.2
and
2.3
, such as the category
\mathcal{U}
of spaces, and let
G
be a finite group. An action of
G
on an object
X
of
\mathcal{V}
can be specified in several equivalent ways. One is to regard
G
as a group in
\mathcal{V}
via
Remark 4.13
and to require a map
G\times X\longrightarrow X
in
\mathcal{V}
that satisfies the evident unit and associativity properties, expressed diagrammatically. Another is to regard
G
as a category with one object and to require a functor
G\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}
that sends the one object to
X
. We have the evident notion of a
G
-map
X\longrightarrow Y
.

Let
G\mathcal{V}
denote the category of
G
-objects in
\mathcal{V}
and
G
-maps between them. Then
G\mathcal{V}
is bicomplete, with limits and colimits created in
\mathcal{V}
and given the induced actions by
G
. With the second description, this is a standard fact about functor categories. Therefore
G\mathcal{V}
satisfies
Assumption 2.2
. Similarly, we have the
2
-category
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V})
of categories internal to
G\mathcal{V}
, which can be identified with the 2-category of
G
-objects in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
. Hence
G\mathcal{V}
satisfies
Assumption 2.3
as well. Thus we can replace
\mathcal{V}
by
G\mathcal{V}
and everything we have said so far applies verbatim.

Remark 6.1
.

As just noted, we can think of a
G\mathcal{V}
-category as a
\mathcal{V}
-category
{\eus{C}}
together with
\mathcal{V}
-functors
g\colon{\eus{C}}\longrightarrow{\eus{C}}
for
g\in G
. Then a
G\mathcal{V}
-functor

F\colon{\eus{B}}\longrightarrow{\eus{C}}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
F
such that the diagrams

\textstyle{{\eus{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{{\eus{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\eus{C}}

commute. A
G\mathcal{V}
-transformation
\nu\colon E\Longrightarrow F
is a
\mathcal{V}
-transformation such that

\nu\colon\mathbf{Ob}{\eus{B}}\longrightarrow\textbf{Mor}{{\eus{C}}}
is
G
-equivariant. This is equivalent to having the following equality of pasting diagrams.

\textstyle{{\eus{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle E}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle F}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\nu}
\textstyle{{\eus{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\eus{C}}

=

\textstyle{{\eus{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{{\eus{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle E}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle F}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\nu}
\textstyle{{\eus{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\eus{C}}

The terminal object of
\mathcal{V}
, with trivial
G
-action, is terminal in
G\mathcal{V}
, and we have the category
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
of based objects in
G\mathcal{V}
, which can be identified with the category of
G
-objects in
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
. We also have the
2
-category
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
of categories internal to
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
, which can be identified with the 2-category of
G
-objects in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
; an analogue of
Remark 6.1
applies in this case as well.

We also have
G\mathcal{V}
-
2
-categories, which are defined to be categories enriched in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V})
, using cartesian products,
and
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-categories, which are categories enriched in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
, defined using smash products.
We emphasize that
G
does not act on the collection of objects of a
G\mathcal{V}
-2-category. It acts on the
\mathcal{V}
-categories of morphisms.

What changes is that we now build finite
G
-sets into the picture. We work with categories of operators
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
, our chosen permutative model of the category of based finite
G
-sets. We define these in
Section 6.2
, we define pseudo-commutativity for them in
Section 6.3
, and we define algebras and pseudoalgebras over them in
Section 6.4
. When
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
is pseudo-commutative, we define a multicategory with underlying category
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}_{ps}
in
Section 6.5
. We define the prolongation functor
\mathbb{P}
from
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras to
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras and show that it extends to a symmetric multifunctor
\mathbb{P}\colon\mathbf{Mult}({\mathscr{D}})\longrightarrow\mathbf{Mult}({\mathscr{D}}_{G})
in
Section 6.6
.

6.2.
Categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}

The definition of a category of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
in this section is parallel to that of a category of operators over
\mathscr{F}
in
Section 5.1
, and it is the categorical analogue of the definition given in
[
39
]
on the space level. After giving the relevant definitions, we show how we can go back and forth between categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}
and categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
\mathscr{F}_{G}
be the following model of the
G
-category of based finite
G
-sets. An object in
\mathscr{F}_{G}
consists of a based set
\mathbf{n}
together with a
G
-action prescribed by a homomorphism
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow\Sigma_{n}
. We denote this object by
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
.The morphisms are defined to be all based functions, not just the equivariant ones, and we let
G
act by conjugation on the set of morphisms. Thus
\mathscr{F}_{G}
can be viewed as a
G
-category where
G
acts trivially on objects.

Let
\Pi_{G}\subset\mathscr{F}_{G}
be the sub
G
-category of morphisms

\phi\colon\mathbf{m}^{\alpha}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}^{\beta}
such that
\phi_{j}=0
or
1
for
1\leq j\leq n
. Write
\mathbf{n}
for
\mathbf{n}^{\varepsilon}
, where
\varepsilon
is the trivial homomorphism.
That fixes compatible embeddings of
\Pi
in
\Pi_{G}
and
\mathscr{F}
in
\mathscr{F}_{G}
.
Note that
\mathbf{0}
is a zero object in
\Pi_{G}
and
\mathscr{F}_{G}
.

Remark 6.3
.

We think of
\Sigma_{n}
as the subset of isomorphisms in
\Pi(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n})
. For a based
G
-set
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
, the homomorphism
\alpha
thus maps
G
to
\Pi(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n})
, which is contained in all categories of operators of either type. We have built strictness with respect to
\Pi
into all of our structures, and the strictness with respect to permutations is crucial in dealing with equivariance, in particular in constructing the prolongation functor
\mathbb{P}
.

Definition 6.4
.

A
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V})
-category of operators

{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
, which we abbreviate to

\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V})
-
\mathbf{CO}
over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
, is a

G\mathcal{V}
-
2
-category whose objects are the based
G
-sets
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
for
n\geq 0
, together with
G\mathcal{V}
-
2
-functors

\textstyle{\Pi_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{G}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{G}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{F}_{G}}

such that
\iota_{G}
and
\xi_{G}
are the identity on objects and
\xi_{G}\circ\iota_{G}
is the inclusion.
A morphism
\nu_{G}\colon{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}_{G}
of
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V})
-
\mathbf{CO}
s over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
is a
G\mathcal{V}
-
2
-functor over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
and under
\Pi_{G}
.

For a
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V})
-
\mathbf{CO}

{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
, let
{\mathscr{D}}
denote the full subcategory on the objects
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{n}^{\varepsilon}
with trivial
G
-action. This is the underlying
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V})
-
\mathbf{CO}
over
\mathscr{F}
of
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
.

Definition 6.5
.

A
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V})
-category of operators

{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
is
reduced
if
\mathbf{0}
is a zero object, and we then say that
{\mathscr{D}}

is a
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-category of operators
over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
. We shall restrict attention to
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
.

Remark 6.6
.

As in
Remark 5.4
, a
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-
\mathbf{CO}

{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
is a
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-category, with
\iota_{G}
and
\xi_{G}

G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-functors, and a morphism of reduced
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V})
-
\mathbf{CO}
s over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
is reduced and is thus a
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-functor over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
and under
\Pi_{G}
.

Construction 6.7
.

We construct a
prolongation functor

\mathbb{P}
from the category of
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-
\mathbf{CO}
s over
\mathscr{F}
to the category of
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-
\mathbf{CO}
s over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
. Let
{\mathscr{D}}
be a
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-
\mathbf{CO}
over
\mathscr{F}
.
Define the morphism
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-category
\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})
to be a copy of
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
, but with
G
-action induced by conjugation and the original given
G
-action on
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
. Explicitly, the action of
g\in G
on
\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})
, which we shall call
\mathbb{P}g
when
\alpha
and
\beta
are understood, is the composite

(6.8)

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}g:={\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha(g^{-1})^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta(g)_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}).}

Here
\alpha(g^{-1})^{*}
and
\beta(g)_{*}
are defined by precomposition with
\alpha(g^{-1})
and postcomposition with
\beta(g)
; we think of them as prewhiskerings and postwhiskerings. Composition is inherited from
{\mathscr{D}}
and is equivariant.
Observing that
\Pi_{G}
and
\mathscr{F}_{G}
are the prolongations of
\Pi
and
\mathscr{F}
, the inclusion
\iota_{G}
and projection
\xi_{G}
are inherited from
{\mathscr{D}}
as
\mathbb{P}\iota
and
\mathbb{P}\varepsilon
, This uses the functoriality of
\mathbb{P}
, which we now explain. For a map of
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-
\mathbf{CO}
s
\nu\colon{\mathscr{D}}\to\mathscr{E}
, we define

\mathbb{P}\nu\colon\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}\mathscr{E}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})

to just be
\nu
; it is equivariant with respect to the new action because
\nu
is a
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-functor and thus is compatible with the
G
-action and with precomposition and postcomposition with maps in
\Pi
.

Proposition 6.9
.

If
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
is a
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-
\mathbf{CO}
over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
, then
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\cong\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}
, where
{\mathscr{D}}
is the underlying
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-
\mathbf{CO}
over
\mathscr{F}
of
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
be a
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-
\mathbf{CO}
over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
. Let
\mathrm{id}^{\alpha}\in\Pi_{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})
and
\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}\in\Pi_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
be the morphisms given by the identity map on the set
\mathbf{m}
. They are not identity morphisms but rather are mutual inverses in
\Pi_{G}
, and hence in
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
. Since
G
acts by conjugation on
\Pi_{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})
and
\Pi_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
, the action of
g
sends
\mathrm{id}^{\alpha}
and
\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}
to the maps on
\mathbf{m}
given by
\alpha(g)
and
\alpha(g)^{-1}
, respectively.

Precomposition with
\mathrm{id}^{\alpha}
and postcomposition with
\mathrm{id}_{\beta}
induce an isomorphism of
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-categories

(6.10)

{\mathscr{D}}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})={\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}).

The above observations and the fact that composition in
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
is
G
-equivariant imply that this map becomes
G
-equivariant when we endow the target with the action defined on
\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})
, giving the desired isomorphism
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\cong\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}
.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

The map of
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-categories in
(6.10)
induces a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-functor

{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}.

It is an inverse up to invertible
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-natural transformation to the inclusion
{\mathscr{D}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
. Thus
{\mathscr{D}}
and
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
are equivalent as
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-categories, but not as
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-categories.

Definition 6.12
.

For a reduced operad
{\mathcal{O}}
in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V})
, define the associated category of operators
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}({\mathcal{O}})
over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
to be the prolongation
\mathbb{P}({\mathscr{D}}({\mathcal{O}}))
.

A more explicit description is given on the space level in
[
39
, Definition 5.1]
.
13
13

13

Numbered references to
[
39
]
refer to the first version posted on ArXiv; they will be changed when the published version appears.

6.3.
Pseudo-commutative categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}

Observe that
\Pi_{G}
and
\mathscr{F}_{G}
are permutative under the smash product of finite based
G
-sets. On underlying sets, the smash product and its symmetry isomorphism are defined just as for
\Pi
and
\mathscr{F}
. Recall that when we restrict to
\Sigma
, we denote the smash product
\otimes
. This can be thought of as a collection of maps
\Sigma_{m}\times\Sigma_{n}\longrightarrow\Sigma_{mn}
.
Then homomorphisms
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow\Sigma_{m}
and
\beta\colon G\longrightarrow\Sigma_{n}
have the product homomorphism
\alpha\otimes\beta
given by applying
\otimes
elementwise; that is,
(\alpha\otimes\beta)(g)=\alpha(g)\otimes\beta(g)
.

Then
\mathbf{m}^{\alpha}\wedge\mathbf{n}^{\beta}=\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}^{\alpha\otimes\beta}
, and the smash product

\mathscr{F}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{p}^{\gamma})\wedge\mathscr{F}_{G}(\mathbf{n}^{\beta},\mathbf{q}^{\delta})\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}_{G}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}^{\alpha\otimes\beta},\mathbf{p}\mathbf{q}^{\gamma\otimes\delta})

is
G
-equivariant.

The following definition is precisely analogous to
Definition 5.7
.

Definition 6.13
.

A
pseudo-commutative structure

on a category of operators
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
is a pseudo-permutative structure
({\mathscr{D}}_{G},\mathbf{1},\circledast,\tau)
such that

(1)

\circledast
restricts to
\wedge
on

\Pi_{G}\wedge\Pi_{G}
and projects to
\wedge
on
\mathscr{F}_{G}

(in the sense that
\xi_{G}\circ\circledast=\wedge\circ(\xi_{G}\wedge\xi_{G})
);

(2)

\circledast
restricts to a strict
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-functor on
\Pi_{G}\wedge{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
and
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\wedge\Pi_{G}
;

(3)

\tau
restricts to the symmetry on
\Pi_{G}
.

Define a map of pseudo-commutative categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
as in
Definition 5.11
.

The following theorem states that the prolongation of a pseudo-commutative category of operators is again pseudo-commutative.

Theorem 6.14
.

Let
{\mathscr{D}}
be a pseudo-commutative
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-category of operators over
\mathscr{F}
. Then
\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}
is a
pseudo-commutative
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-category of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
and the inclusion
({\mathscr{D}},\Pi)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}},\Pi_{G})
preserves the pseudo-commutative structure.

Proof.

We define the
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctor
\textstyle{\circledast\colon\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}}
as follows. On objects,
\circledast
is just
\wedge
; that is,

\mathbf{m}^{\alpha}\circledast\mathbf{n}^{\beta}=\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}^{\alpha\otimes\beta}
. On
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-categories of morphisms,

\circledast\colon\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{p}^{\gamma})\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n}^{\beta},\mathbf{q}^{\delta})\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}^{\alpha\otimes\beta},\mathbf{p}\mathbf{q}^{\gamma\otimes\delta})

is just

\circledast\colon{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{q})\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p}\mathbf{q}).

We need to show that
\circledast
is equivariant with respect to the action of
G
on
\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}
(see
Remark 6.1
). The equivariance is encoded in the following commutative diagram.

(6.15)

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\scriptstyle{\ \ \alpha(g^{-1})^{*}\wedge\beta(g^{-1})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}g\wedge\mathbb{P}g}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\scriptstyle{g\wedge g}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\scriptstyle{\gamma(g)_{*}\wedge\delta(g)_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p}\mathbf{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p}\mathbf{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\gamma\otimes\delta)(g)_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p}\mathbf{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\alpha\otimes\beta)(g^{-1})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}g}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p}\mathbf{q}))}

The central square commutes
since
{\mathscr{D}}
is a
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-category of operators, making the displayed functors
\circledast
internal to
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
and therefore equivariant.
The left and right trapezoids commute because
\circledast
is strict when
composing with morphisms in
\Pi
according to condition (
2
) of
Definition 5.7
.
The upper and lower trapezoids commute by definition, as in
(6.8)
.

The pseudofunctoriality constraint

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n}^{\beta},\mathbf{p}^{\gamma})\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{r}^{\zeta},\mathbf{s}^{\eta})\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q}^{\delta},\mathbf{r}^{\zeta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\wedge\circledast}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge t\wedge\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\ \vartheta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n}\mathbf{r}^{\beta\otimes\zeta},\mathbf{p}\mathbf{s}^{\gamma\otimes\eta})\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{q}^{\alpha\otimes\delta},\mathbf{n}\mathbf{r}^{\beta\otimes\zeta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n}^{\beta},\mathbf{p}^{\gamma})\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{r}^{\zeta},\mathbf{s}^{\eta})\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q}^{\delta},\mathbf{r}^{\zeta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ\wedge\circ}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{p}^{\gamma})\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q}^{\delta},\mathbf{s}^{\eta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{q}^{\alpha\otimes\delta},\mathbf{p}\mathbf{s}^{\gamma\otimes\eta})}

is just that of
{\mathscr{D}}
at the corresponding objects with trivial action (see
(5.8)
). The equivariance of
\vartheta
with respect to the original action on
{\mathscr{D}}
and the conditions on
\vartheta
from
Remark 5.10
combine to show that
\vartheta
prewhiskered with
\mathbb{P}g
is equal to
\vartheta
postwhiskered with
\mathbb{P}g
, as needed.

The symmetry
\tau
is prolonged similarly. The 1-cell at the object
(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{p}^{\beta})
is the permutation
\tau_{m,p}
considered as a 1-cell in
\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}
. The pseudonaturality constraint
\hat{\tau}
(see
(5.9)
)
is given by that on
{\mathscr{D}}
. One needs to check that this
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformation is
G
-equivariant with respect to the prolonged action. This follows from the equivariance of
\hat{\tau}
with respect to the original action on
{\mathscr{D}}
, the compatibility of
\hat{\tau}
with
\vartheta
, condition (
2
) of
Definition 5.7
, and the fact that
\hat{\tau}
restricted to
\Pi
is the identity.
∎

The construction is functorial with respect to pseudo-commutative morphisms of pseudo-commutative categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}
. Theorems
5.12
and
6.14
have the following corollary.

Corollary 6.16
.

If
{\mathcal{O}}
is a pseudo-commutative operad, then
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}({\mathcal{O}})
is pseudo-commutative category of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
.

6.4.
Algebras and pseudoalgebras over categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}

Just as for categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}
, Definitions
4.18
,

4.23
, and
4.26
specialize to
define a 2-category of algebras, pseudomorphisms, and transformations for categories of operators
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
.

Notation 6.17
.

Let
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
be a
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-
\mathbf{CO}
over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
. We denote by

{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}_{ps}
the 2-category
of strict
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras,
({\mathscr{D}}_{G},\Pi_{G})
-pseudomorphisms, and
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-transformations. This 2-category was denoted by
{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}_{ps}}_{\Pi_{G}}
in
Notation 4.27
. Just as in
Notation 5.14
we fix
{\eus{B}}=\Pi_{G}
and drop the subscript from the notation.

Again, we do not discuss general
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-pseudoalgebras here, leaving such consideration for
[
14
]
. However, we will need pseudoalgebras in the special case of
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}=\mathscr{F}_{G}
starting in
Section 7
. Recall from
Notation 4.27
that we have the 2-category
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf PsAlg}
of weak
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-pseudoalgebras, weak
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-pseudomorphisms, and
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-transformations.

Remark 6.18
.

We comment on the choice of weak pseudoalgebras.
This choice is already essential nonequivariantly. Look back at
(1.2)
, but take
G=e
.
With more effort, we could have started with
{\mathcal{O}}
-pseudoalgebras, as defined in
[
16
]
; the functor
\mathbb{R}

would then land in
{\mathscr{D}}
-pseudoalgebras that are strict over
\Pi
.
However, the section
\zeta\colon\mathscr{F}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}
(see
Proposition 7.4
) loses strictness with respect to
\Pi
, as explained in
Remark 7.5
, so that whether the
domain of
\zeta^{*}
is taken to be
{\mathscr{D}}\text{-}\bf{Alg_{ps}}
or some 2-category of
{\mathscr{D}}
-pseudoalgebras, its target must still be
\mathscr{F}\text{-}{\bf PsAlg}
. It takes strict
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras only to weak
\mathscr{F}
-pseudoalgebras.

6.5.
The symmetric multicategory of
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras

Let
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
be a pseudo-commutative category of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
.
We define the multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathscr{D}}_{G})
of
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras, which amounts to defining the
k
-ary morphisms.
The definition is exactly like
Definition 5.15
, hence we refer the reader there for details. Again, we set it up to have its objects be
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras, although with only slightly more work we could equally well have set it up to have its objects be
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-pseudoalgebras. Remember that we write

\theta\colon{\mathscr{D}}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{n}^{\beta})

for the action of
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
on a
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebra
{\mathcal{X}}
. For a
k
-tuple of finite
G
-sets
\mathbf{m}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
,
we write
\mathbf{m}^{\alpha}
for the finite
G
-set with
m=m_{1}\cdots m_{k}
and
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_{k}
.

Recall from
Definition 4.29
that given
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras
{\mathcal{X}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{X}}_{k}
, we have the external smash product
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\overline{\wedge}\dots\overline{\wedge}{\mathcal{X}}_{k}
, which is a
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{\wedge k}
-algebra. For a
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}
, we have the
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{\wedge k}
-pseudoalgebra
\mathscr{Y}\circ\circledast^{k}
, which is strict over

\Pi^{\wedge k}_{G}
.

Definition 6.19
.

We define a symmetric multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathscr{D}}_{G})
of
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras. The objects are the
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras.
For
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq k
, and
{\mathcal{Y}}
, a
k
-ary morphism

\underline{{\mathcal{X}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}
consists of a
({\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{\wedge k},\Pi_{G}^{\wedge k})
-pseudomorphism

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 28.64066pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-28.64066pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\overline{\wedge}\dots\overline{\wedge}{\mathcal{X}}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 52.64066pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 52.64066pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}\circ\circledast^{k}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Composition and the symmetric action are specified as in
Definition 5.15
.

Unpacking the definition, a
k
-ary morphism
F=(F,\delta)
consists of
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functors

F\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{1}(\mathbf{m}_{1}^{\alpha_{1}})\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathcal{X}}_{k}(\mathbf{m}_{k}^{\alpha_{k}})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha}),

together with invertible
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations
\delta
as in the following diagram, in which
1\leq i\leq k
.

(6.20)

\textstyle{\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathscr{D}}_{G}(\mathbf{m}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}},\mathbf{n}_{i}^{\beta_{i}})\wedge\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(\mathbf{m}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}})}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge F}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 8.19447pt\ \delta}
\textstyle{\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathscr{D}}_{G}(\mathbf{m}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}},\mathbf{n}_{i}^{\beta_{i}})\wedge{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\wedge\mathrm{id}}
\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathscr{D}}_{G}(\mathbf{m}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}},\mathbf{n}_{i}^{\beta_{i}})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(\mathbf{m}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}})
\scriptstyle{\bigwedge\limits_{i}\theta}
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\wedge{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{\bigwedge\limits_{i}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(\mathbf{n}_{i}^{\beta_{i}})}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{n}^{\beta}).}

The strictness over
\Pi_{G}^{\wedge k}
is encoded by requiring
\delta
to be the identity if
all factors
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
are restricted to
\Pi_{G}
.
These transformations must satisfy coherence with respect to composition in
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{\wedge k}
, details of which can be found in
Section 11.3
.

We have the following analogue of
Theorem 5.20
; its proof is essentially the same.

Theorem 6.21
.

Let
(\Psi,\mu)\colon{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}_{G}
be a map of pseudo-commutative categories of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
. Then pulling back along
\Psi
induces a (symmetric) multifunctor

\Psi^{*}\colon\mathbf{Mult}(\mathscr{E}_{G})\longrightarrow\mathbf{Mult}({\mathscr{D}}_{G}).

6.6.
The symmetric multifunctor
\mathbb{P}

Let
{\mathscr{D}}
be a
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
-category of operators over
\mathscr{F}
and

{\mathscr{D}}_{G}=\mathbb{P}{\mathscr{D}}
be its prolonged category of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
, as defined in
Construction 6.7
.
Define

\mathbb{U}\colon{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}_{ps}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}\text{-}\bf{Alg_{ps}}

by restricting along the inclusion
{\mathscr{D}}\subset{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
. Then
\mathbb{U}
has a left adjoint
prolongation functor on the level of algebras,

\mathbb{P}:{\mathscr{D}}\text{-}\bf{Alg_{ps}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}_{ps}.

For the subcategory of
strict
maps, this is the categorical analogue of the prolongation functor from

[
39
, §4.3]
or
[
48
]
, and it gives an equivalence of categories.
We will discuss the extension of
\mathbb{P}
to
({\mathscr{D}},\Pi)
-pseudomorphisms
in
Theorem 6.23
below.

On objects,

\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{X}})(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})\in\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
is defined by letting
\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{X}})(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})
be a copy of
{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})
, but with the action of

g\in G
, denoted
\mathbb{P}g
when
\alpha
is understood, defined to be the composite

\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha(g)_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m}).}

Here
\alpha(g)_{*}\colon{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})
is given by the action of
\Pi
on
{\mathcal{X}}
.
The enriched functor
{\mathcal{X}}
takes the morphisms of
\Pi
, which are
G
-fixed, to
G
-equivariant functors. It follows that
we can equivalently write
\mathbb{P}g
as the composite

(6.22)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha(g)_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X(\mathbf{m}).}

The action
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
–functor

\mathbb{P}\theta\colon{\mathscr{D}}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{n}^{\beta})

is defined to be

\theta\colon{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{n}).

The following diagram shows that

\mathbb{P}\theta
is equivariant because
\theta
is equivariant, as displayed in the middle square.

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\alpha(g^{-1})^{*}\wedge\alpha(g)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}g\wedge\mathbb{P}g}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{g\wedge g}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\beta(g)_{*}\wedge\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta(g)_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}g}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{n})}

Since the action
\theta
is compatible with composition in
{\mathscr{D}}
, the left and right trapezoids commute, the left one using that
\alpha(g^{-1})_{*}\circ\alpha(g)_{*}=\mathrm{id}
.

With these preliminaries, we have the following result.

Theorem 6.23
.

The functor
\mathbb{P}\colon{\mathscr{D}}\text{-}\bf{Alg_{ps}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}_{ps}
extends to a (symmetric) multifunctor

\mathbb{P}\colon\mathbf{Mult}({\mathscr{D}})\longrightarrow\mathbf{Mult}({\mathscr{D}}_{G}).

Proof.

Since the values of
\mathbb{P}{\mathcal{X}}
at objects in
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
are the values of
{\mathcal{X}}
but with a new
G
-action, the idea of the proof is to show that the data of a map of
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras remains
G
-equivariant with respect to the new action.

Thus let
(F,\delta)\colon({\mathcal{X}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{X}}_{k})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}
be a
k
-ary morphism of
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras. This means that we are given

G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functors

F\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{1}(\mathbf{m}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathcal{X}}_{k}(\mathbf{m}_{k})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{m})

and invertible
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations
\delta
is as in
(5.17)
. We define
\mathbb{P}(F,\delta)=(\mathbb{P}F,\mathbb{P}\delta)
as follows. For
\mathbf{m}_{1}^{\alpha_{1}},\dots,\mathbf{m}_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}
, with
m=m_{1}\cdots m_{k}
and
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_{k}
, the map

\mathbb{P}F\colon\mathbb{P}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}(\mathbf{m}_{1}^{\alpha_{1}})\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathcal{X}}_{k}(\mathbf{m}_{k}^{\alpha_{k}})\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha}),

is given by
F
. Similarly, we define
\mathbb{P}\delta
in the diagram
(6.20)
to be
\delta
in the underlying
diagram
(5.17)
.

We must check that these give
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functors and
G\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations, respectively, that is, that they are equivariant with respect to the prolonged action.
When
k=1
, this gives the promised definition of
\mathbb{P}
on pseudomorphisms of
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras.
We first check that
\mathbb{P}F
is equivariant. For ease of notation, we consider the case
k=2
.
Recall the
G
-action on
\mathbb{P}{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})
given in
(6.22)
.
Then to check that

\mathbb{P}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}(\mathbf{m}_{1}^{\alpha_{1}})\wedge\mathbb{P}{\mathcal{X}}_{2}(\mathbf{m}_{2}^{\alpha_{2}})\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}F}\mathbb{P}{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})

is
G
-equivariant, it suffices to show that the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}(\mathbf{m}_{1})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}_{2}(\mathbf{m}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}(g)_{*}\wedge\alpha_{2}(g)_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha(g)_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}(\mathbf{m}_{1})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}_{2}(\mathbf{m}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{g\wedge g}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}(\mathbf{m}_{1})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}_{2}(\mathbf{m}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{m})}

commutes.
The top square commutes since
F
restricts to a strict transformation of
\Pi
-functors (see
Definition 4.23
). The bottom square commutes since
F
is equivariant with respect to the original action.

It remains to check that
\mathbb{P}\delta
is equivariant with respect to the prolonged action. By
Remark 6.1
, this is done by proving that prewhiskering
\delta
with
\mathbb{P}g
is equal to postwhiskering it.
We illustrate by taking
k=1
.
Thus we return to
Definition 4.23
.
Let
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
be
{\mathscr{D}}
-algebras and let
F\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 28.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

and

and

-action.

Definition 6.24
.

We define
\mathbb{R}_{G}
to be the composite

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
