\textstyle{(\xi^{*}\mathrm{St}_{\eus{C}}{\mathcal{X}})\,\overline{\wedge}\,(\zeta^{*}\mathrm{St}_{\eus{D}}{\mathcal{Y}})}

8.2.
The extension of strictification to a multifunctor

We now assume that
({\eus{C}},I,\circledast,\tau)
is a
permutative
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-category (
Definition 4.30
). We extend
\mathrm{St}
to a multifunctor from the multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{PsAlg}})
of (weak)
{\eus{C}}
-pseudoalgebras to the multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{Alg}})
of strict
{\eus{C}}
-algebras and strict multilinear maps. The former is defined by replacing
\mathscr{F}_{G}
by
{\eus{C}}
in
Definition 7.11
, and we now define the latter.

Definition 8.11
.

The multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{Alg}})
is constructed by using the symmetric monoidal structure on
{\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{Alg}}
given by Day convolution along iterations of the monoidal product
\circledast
on
{\eus{C}}
. Reinterpreting this externally, via the universal property of Day convolution, we see that for strict

{\eus{C}}
-algebras
{\mathcal{X}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{X}}_{k}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
, a
k
-ary morphism is given by a strict
{\eus{C}}^{\wedge k}
-morphism

F\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\,\overline{\wedge}\,\cdots\,\overline{\wedge}\,{\mathcal{X}}_{k}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}\circ\circledast^{k}.

We remark that the definition above differs from Definitions
5.15
and
6.19
in that the multimorphisms are pseudotransformations in those cases, and strict here.

Theorem 8.12
.

The strictification functor
\mathrm{St}
induces a (non-symmetric) multifunctor

\mathrm{St}\colon\mathbf{Mult}({\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{PsAlg}})\longrightarrow\mathbf{Mult}({\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{Alg}}).

Proof.

For a
{\eus{C}}
-pseudoalgebra
{\mathcal{X}}
,
we set

\mathrm{St}{\mathcal{X}}=\mathrm{St}_{\eus{C}}{\mathcal{X}}
. For multimorphisms, we use the 2-functor
\mathrm{St}_{{\eus{C}}^{\wedge k}}
, which for brevity we denote by
\mathrm{St}_{k}
, to strictify
{\eus{C}}^{\wedge k}
-pseudomorphisms. More precisely, recall that a
k
-ary morphism in
\mathbf{Mult}({\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{PsAlg}})
is given by a
{\eus{C}}^{\wedge k}
-pseudomorphism

\textstyle{F\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\,\overline{\wedge}\,\cdots\,\overline{\wedge}\,{\mathcal{X}}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\,{\mathcal{Y}}\circ\circledast^{k}.}

We define the
k
-ary morphism
\mathrm{St}(F)
in
\mathbf{Mult}({\eus{C}})
as the composite

\textstyle{\mathrm{St}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\,\overline{\wedge}\,\cdots\,\overline{\wedge}\,\mathrm{St}{\mathcal{X}}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathrm{St}_{k}({\mathcal{X}}_{1}\,\overline{\wedge}\,\cdots\,\overline{\wedge}\,{\mathcal{X}}_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{St}_{k}(F)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{St}_{k}({\mathcal{Y}}\circ\circledast^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{(\mathrm{St}{\mathcal{Y}})\circ\circledast^{k},}

where the unnamed isomorphism is that of
Lemma 8.9
, and the map
\psi
is the one defined in
Lemma 8.6
.

Note that on 1-ary morphisms,
\mathrm{St}
is just the 2-functor
\mathrm{St}_{\eus{C}}
, so in particular this assignment sends the identity to itself.

It remains to prove that
\mathrm{St}
preserves multicomposition. Let

F\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 25.28642pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-25.28642pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\,\overline{\wedge}\,\cdots\,\overline{\wedge}\,{\mathcal{Y}}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.28642pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 49.28642pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\,{\mathcal{Z}}\circ\circledast^{k}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

be a
{\eus{C}}^{\wedge k}
-pseudomorphism
and, for
1\leq r\leq k
, let

E_{r}\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 32.7098pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-32.7098pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{r,1}\,\overline{\wedge}\,\cdots\,\overline{\wedge}\,{\mathcal{X}}_{r,j_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.7098pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 56.7098pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\,{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}\circ\circledast^{j_{r}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

be a
{\eus{C}}^{\wedge j_{r}}
-pseudomorphism.
The composite in
\mathbf{Mult}({\eus{C}}\text{-}{\bf{PsAlg}})
is given by the pasting diagram in
(5.16)
. In terms of the external smash product of
Definition 4.29
and the pullback of
Notation 4.28
, this
{\eus{C}}^{\wedge j}
-pseudomorphism can be expressed as the composite of
{\eus{C}}^{j}
-pseudomorphisms

\textstyle{\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r,i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{r,i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}E_{r}}
\textstyle{\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}(\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}=(\bigwedge\limits_{r}\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}(\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}{\mathcal{Y}}_{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\bigwedge\limits_{r}\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}F}
\textstyle{(\bigwedge\limits_{r}\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}(\circledast^{k})^{*}{\mathcal{Z}}=(\circledast^{j})^{*}{\mathcal{Z}}.}

Consider the following diagram of
{\eus{C}}^{\wedge j}
-morphisms.

\textstyle{\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r,i}}\mathrm{St}{\mathcal{X}}_{r,i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}\mathrm{St}_{j_{r}}\Big(\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{r,i}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}\mathrm{St}_{j_{r}}(E_{r})}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}\mathrm{St}_{j_{r}}\Big((\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}\psi_{\circledast^{j_{r}}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathrm{St}_{j}\Big(\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r,i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{r,i}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{St}_{j}\Big(\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}E_{r}\Big)}
\textstyle{\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}(\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}\mathrm{St}{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{St}_{j}\Big(\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}(\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\bigwedge\limits_{r}\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}\Big(\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}\mathrm{St}{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathrm{St}_{j}\Big((\bigwedge\limits_{r}\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}(\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}{\mathcal{Y}}_{r})\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{St}_{j}\big((\bigwedge\limits_{r}\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}F\big)}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{(\bigwedge\circledast^{j_{r}})}}
\textstyle{(\bigwedge\limits_{r}\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}\mathrm{St}_{k}\Big(\overline{\bigwedge\limits_{r}}{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\bigwedge\limits_{r}\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}\mathrm{St}_{k}F}
\textstyle{\mathrm{St}_{j}\Big((\bigwedge\limits_{r}\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}(\circledast^{k})^{*}{\mathcal{Z}}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{(\bigwedge\circledast^{j_{r}})}}
\textstyle{(\bigwedge\limits_{r}\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}\mathrm{St}_{k}\Big((\circledast^{k})^{*}{\mathcal{Z}}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\bigwedge\limits_{r}\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}\psi_{\circledast^{k}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{St}_{j}\Big((\circledast^{j})^{*}{\mathcal{Z}}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{\circledast^{j}}}
\textstyle{(\circledast^{j})^{*}\mathrm{St}{\mathcal{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\bigwedge\limits_{r}\circledast^{j_{r}})^{*}(\circledast^{k})^{*}\mathrm{St}{\mathcal{Z}}}

Strictifying
E_{1},\dots,E_{r}
and
F
and then composing is equal to going around clockwise. Using that
\mathrm{St}_{j}
is a 2-functor, we get that going around counter-clockwise is equal to strictifying the composite. The diagram commutes; indeed, going from top to bottom, the regions commute by associativity of
\wedge
in
\underline{\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathcal{V}_{\ast})
,
Lemma 8.9
,
Lemma 8.10
, naturality of
\psi
, and
Lemma 8.8
, respectively.
∎

Remark 8.13
.

This proof depends crucially on the fact that the monoidal product on
{\eus{C}}
is a strict
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-2-functor and not just a pseudofunctor. It does not work for our pseudo-commutative categories of operators
{\mathscr{D}}
or
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
. This is the crux of why the route in this paper is less categorically intensive than the monadic route of
[
28
]
, which simultaneously strictifies and transfers structure from
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
to
\mathscr{F}_{G}
.

8.3.
\mathrm{St}
is not a symmetric multifunctor

As stated in
Theorem 8.12
,
\mathrm{St}
is not a
symmetric
multifunctor. We explain why in this parenthetical subsection.
We consider the case when
k=2
, with
\sigma
the non-trivial element of
\Sigma_{2}
. Since the problem already appears nonequivariantly, we take
G=e
and specialize to
{\eus{C}}=\mathscr{F}
. Thus let

F\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.31949pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.31949pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\wedge{\mathcal{X}}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.31949pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 40.31949pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\,{\mathcal{Y}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
be a (weak)
\mathscr{F}
-pseudomorphism between (weak)
\mathscr{F}
-pseudoalgebras. Following
(5.18)
, for an object
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p})
of
\mathscr{F}\wedge\mathscr{F}
, the 1-cell component of
F\sigma
is the composite

\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{2}(\mathbf{m})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}_{1}(\mathbf{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}(\mathbf{p})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}_{2}(\mathbf{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{p}\mathbf{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}(\tau_{p,m})}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p}).}

We claim that the 1-cell components of
\mathrm{St}(F\sigma)
and
(\mathrm{St}F)\sigma
do not agree. For an object
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p})
of
\mathscr{F}\wedge\mathscr{F}
, these are
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functors

\mathrm{St}{\mathcal{X}}_{2}(\mathbf{m})\wedge\mathrm{St}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}(\mathbf{p})\longrightarrow\mathrm{St}{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p}).

We compare them at the level of objects, writing elementwise.
An object of the source has the form

\Big((f_{2},x_{2}),(f_{1},x_{1})\Big)

where
f_{1}\colon\mathbf{q}\longrightarrow\mathbf{p}
and
f_{2}\colon\mathbf{n}\longrightarrow\mathbf{m}
are morphisms of
\mathscr{F}
and
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are objects of

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}(\mathbf{q})
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{2}(\mathbf{n})
, respectively.

Then
\mathrm{St}(F)\sigma
sends
\Big((f_{2},x_{2}),(f_{1},x_{1})\Big)
to
\Big((\tau_{p,m}\circ(f_{1}\wedge f_{2}),F(x_{1},x_{2})\Big)
.
We can rewrite
the output as

\Big((f_{2}\wedge f_{1})\circ\tau_{q,n},F(x_{1},x_{2})\Big).

On the other hand,
\mathrm{St}(F\sigma)
sends
\Big((f_{2},x_{2}),(f_{1},x_{1})\Big)
to

\Big((f_{2}\wedge f_{1}),\theta(\tau_{q,n},F(x_{1},x_{2}))\Big).

We conclude that the multifunctor
\mathrm{St}
is not symmetric.

Remark 8.14
.

This failure of symmetry is forced by our need to use weak pseudostructure in the target of
\zeta_{G}^{*}
. If we instead use pseudofunctors which are strict relative to
\Pi
when we strictify,
for example using the generalized strictification theorem given in
[
14
]
,
then we do have symmetry.
Symmetry is also studied in the
2
-monadic context in
[
28
]
, where the problem is entirely different.

9.
From
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-algebras in Cat to
G
-spectra

In this section, we describe how we pass from categorical to topological
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-algebras and
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras, and then to
G
-spectra, keeping track of multiplicative structure. Nonequivariantly,
\mathscr{F}
-spaces (aka
\Gamma
-spaces) were introduced by Segal in his treatment of infinite loop space theory. These generalize to
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-
G
-spaces, which are the input of the equivariant version of the Segal infinite loop space machine. For a detailed treatment of
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-
G
-spaces, we refer the reader to
[
39
]
, and for a treatment of its symmetric monoidal structure to
[
15
]
. Topological categories of operators
{\mathscr{D}}
and
{\mathscr{D}}
-spaces were introduced in
[
37
]
as an intermediary between
\mathscr{F}
-spaces and operadic algebras in the proof of the uniqueness of infinite loop space machines. The topological equivariant analogues,
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-spaces, are treated extensively in
[
39
]
in the comparison of equivariant infinite loop space machines.

In
Section 9.1
, we discuss the classifying space functor multiplicatively. We recall the equivariant Segal machine in
Section 9.2
. Using that the classifying space functor and the Segal machine are both lax monoidal, we restate and prove
Theorem A
as
Theorem 9.13
. In effect, it gives a multiplicative equivariant infinite loop space machine starting from operadic categorical input.

Some technicalities ensuring that our passage from categorical to space-level input is homotopically well-behaved are postponed to
Section 9.3
. The point is just to give conditions on the categorical input that ensure that the output
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-
G
-spaces have nondegenerate basepoints. We briefly discuss a related open question about Day convolution in
Section 9.4
. The brief
Section 9.5
shows how to obtain homotopies between maps of
G
-spectra from operadic categorical input.

9.1.
The multifunctor
B

In order to construct equivariant spectra from
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras in a multiplicative way, we need to understand the multiplicative properties of the classifying space functor
B
.

The classifying space functor
B
does not commute with smash products in general. However, we have the following result, which allows us to use
B
to change enrichment. Recall
Definition 2.6
.

Proposition 9.1
.

The classifying space functor
B\colon\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U}_{\ast})\longrightarrow G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}_{\ast}
is lax symmetric monoidal.

Proof.

The map

B{\eus{C}}\times B{\eus{D}}\cong B({\eus{C}}\times{\eus{D}})\longrightarrow B({\eus{C}}\wedge{\eus{D}})

sends the subspace
B{\eus{C}}\vee B{\eus{D}}
to the basepoint and therefore induces a based map

B{\eus{C}}\wedge B{\eus{D}}\longrightarrow B({\eus{C}}\wedge{\eus{D}}).\qed

Definition 9.2
.

Let
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
be a
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U}_{\ast})
-category of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
, as defined in
Definition 6.5
. Let
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}
denote the category enriched in
G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}_{*}
obtained by applying
B
to morphism based categories to change the enrichment. When
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}=\mathscr{F}_{G}
, the morphism categories are discrete (identity morphisms only). Since the classifying space of a discrete category is isomorphic to itself, we can identify
\mathscr{F}_{G}^{top}
with
\mathscr{F}_{G}
. It follows that
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}
is a category of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
in the sense of
[
39
, §4.2]
.

Notation 9.3
.

Since the category
G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}_{*}
is closed monoidal, it is enriched over itself. We denote
this enriched category by
\underline{G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}}_{*}
. Its based
G
-spaces of morphisms are given by the spaces of all nonequivariant based maps, based at the constant maps at the basepoint, with
G
acting by conjugation. We denote by
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}\text{-}G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}_{\ast}}
the category of
G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}_{*}
-enriched functors
{\mathcal{X}}\colon{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}\longrightarrow\underline{G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}}_{*}
. The enrichment over based
G
-spaces implies that
{\mathcal{X}}(0)=\ast

[
39
, Lemma 1.13]
.
To emphasize that these are just (enriched) functors to
G
-spaces, we call them
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}
-
G
-spaces. In particular,
an
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-
G
-space
will mean an object of
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}_{\ast}}
.

Recall that we write
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-
\mathbf{Alg}
as shorthand for the category of
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras and strict maps in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U}_{\ast})
.

Proposition 9.4
.

Applying the classifying space functor levelwise induces a functor

B\colon{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\mathbf{Alg}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}\text{-}G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}_{\ast}}.

Proof.

By
Proposition 9.1
the classifying space functor
B
is lax symmetric monoidal. It follows formally that it induces a map on functor categories.
Explicitly, if
{\mathcal{X}}
is a
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebra in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U}_{\ast})
, we obtain the
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}
-
G
-space
B{\mathcal{X}}
by applying
B
levelwise, with action maps given by the composites

B{\mathscr{D}}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\wedge B{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})\longrightarrow B({\mathscr{D}}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\wedge{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha}))\xrightarrow{B\theta}B{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{n}^{\beta}),

where the first map is the monoidal constraint for
B
. The commutativity of the composition and unit diagrams follows from their analogs for
{\mathcal{X}}
(see
Definition 4.18
) and the axioms for a lax monoidal functor. The functoriality of
B
on strict algebra maps is obtained by applying
B
levelwise.
∎

We now concentrate on the case of
\mathscr{F}_{G}
. The categories
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}_{\ast}}
and
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
are symmetric monoidal via Day convolution.

Proposition 9.5
.

The functor

B\colon\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}_{\ast}}

of
Proposition 9.4
is lax symmetric monoidal.

Proof.

This follows formally from
Proposition 9.1
, but we sketch the argument. The induced functor
B
on our categories of algebras preserves the monoidal unit, which in both the source and the target is given by the representable functor
\mathscr{F}_{G}(\bf{1},-)
. Given
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
in
\mathscr{F}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
, we construct a map

B{\mathcal{X}}\wedge B{\mathcal{Y}}\longrightarrow B({\mathcal{X}}\wedge{\mathcal{Y}})

in
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}_{\ast}}
by applying the universal property of Day convolution to the map of
(\mathscr{F}_{G}\wedge\mathscr{F}_{G})
-
G
-spaces with components given by the composites

B{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})\wedge B{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\longrightarrow B({\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})\wedge{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{n}^{\beta}))\longrightarrow B({\mathcal{X}}\wedge{\mathcal{Y}})(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}^{\alpha\otimes\beta}).

Here the first map is the lax monoidal constraint for
B
and the second map is obtained by applying
B
to the components of the unit of the Day convolution adjunction. It is routine to check that this map satisfies the required compatibilities with the unit, associativity and symmetry isomorphisms.
∎

Remark 9.6
.

Note that if
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
is a
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U}_{\ast})
-category of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
equipped with a pseudo-commutative structure (
Definition 6.13
), this does
not
give rise to a symmetric monoidal structure on
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}
. As a result, we do not have a monoidal structure on the category of
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}
-algebras, and so we cannot expect an analogue of
Proposition 9.5
for
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras.

9.2.
From
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-
G
-spaces to
G
-spectra

In this section, we first recall the properties of the equivariant Segal machine, whose construction is given in detail in
[
39
]
. A treatment that deals with multiplicative properties can be found in
[
15
]
. In this paper, we treat the Segal machine as a black box, and we refer the reader to those sources for details.

All homotopical versions of the Segal machine come in the form of bar constructions, which are only homotopically well-behaved when the input functors

{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathscr{F}_{G}\longrightarrow\underline{G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}}_{*}
take values in nondegenerately based
G
-spaces. However, in the previous subsection, we concentrated on formal properties of our constructions. Write
G\mathscr{T}
and
\mathscr{T}_{G}
for the full subcategories of nondegenerately based
G
-spaces in
G\mathcal{U}_{\ast}
and in
\underline{G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}}_{*}
. Since these categories are not bicomplete, they are less useful for formal purposes. We introduce notations and definitions to help deal with the resulting dichotomy.

Notation 9.7
.

Let
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}
be a
G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}_{*}
-category of operators over
\mathscr{F}_{G}
, such as the one in
Definition 9.2
. A
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}
-
G
-space is
levelwise nondegenerately based
if each
{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
is nondegenerately based. We write
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}\text{-}G\mathscr{T}
for the full subcategory of
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}\text{-}G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}_{\ast}}
whose objects are levelwise nondegenerately based. In particular, starting with the commutativity operad, whose terms are one-point
G
-spaces, this defines the full subcategory
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathscr{T}
of
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}_{\ast}}
.

Definition 9.8
.

Define
\mathbb{T}
to be the composite functor

\mathbb{T}=\mathrm{St}_{\mathscr{F}_{G}}\circ\zeta_{G}^{*}\circ\mathbb{R}_{G}:{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf Alg}.

Then define
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}}
to be the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}
consisting of those
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras
{\eus{A}}
such that
B\mathbb{T}{\eus{A}}
is in
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathscr{T}
.
Thus, by definition, the composite
B\mathbb{T}
restricts to a functor
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathscr{T}
.

The functor
\mathbb{T}
collates the categorical functors studied in previous sections,
B
passes from categorical data to space level data, and the Segal machine passes from there to spectra.
That machine will be well-behaved when we restrict it to
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathscr{T}
, and
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}}
specifies those
{\mathcal{O}}
algebras that feed into
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathscr{T}
.
We will show in the next subsection that most
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras of interest are in
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}}
.

To define the notion of a Segal machine
\mathbb{S}_{G}
, we need the key notion of a
special

\mathscr{F}_{G}
-
G
-space
. To give a conceptual setting for this notion, observe first that, just as we had on categories, we have a composite functor
\mathbb{R}_{G}^{top}=\mathbb{P}^{top}\mathbb{R}^{top}
from
{\mathcal{O}}^{top}
-algebras in
G\mathscr{T}

to
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}
-
G
-spaces, where
{\mathcal{O}}^{top}
is a operad in
G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}
with associated category of operators
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}
.
We specialize this to the initial operad
{\mathcal{O}}^{top}
, which has
{\mathcal{O}}^{top}(0)={\mathcal{O}}^{top}(1)=\ast
and all other
{\mathcal{O}}^{top}(j)=\emptyset
. Its associated category of operators is
\Pi_{G}
. Applying
\mathbb{R}_{G}^{top}
to this operad, we obtain a functor
\mathbb{R}_{G}^{top}\colon G\mathscr{T}\longrightarrow\Pi_{G}\text{-}G\mathscr{T}
.

For a based
G
-space
X
,
\mathbb{R}_{G}^{top}(X)
sends
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
to the
G
-space
X^{\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}}=G\mathscr{T}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha},X)
. More explicitly, this is

X^{n}
with
G
-action given by

(9.9)

g(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=(ga_{\alpha(g^{-1})(1)},\dots,ga_{\alpha(g^{-1})(n)}).

The functor
\mathbb{R}_{G}^{top}
is right adjoint to the functor
\mathbb{L}_{G}^{top}
that evaluates
a
\Pi_{G}
-
G
-space at
\bf{1}
. For a
\Pi_{G}
-
G
-space
{\mathcal{Y}}
,
the unit

\delta\colon{\mathcal{Y}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{G}^{top}({\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{1}))

of the adjunction is a map of
\Pi_{G}
-
G
-spaces
called the
Segal map
. At level
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
, it is induced
by the
n
projections
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}\to\mathbf{1}

[
39
, Definition 2.28]
.

Definition 9.10
.

We say that a
\Pi_{G}
-
G
-space
{\mathcal{Y}}
is
special
if
\delta
is a levelwise weak
G
-homotopy equivalence.
We say that
a
{\mathscr{D}}^{top}_{G}
-
G
-space, and in particular an
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-
G
-space,
is special if
its underlying
\Pi_{G}
-
G
-space
is special.

Recall that an orthogonal
G
-spectrum
E
is a positive
\Omega
-
G
-spectrum if its adjoint structure maps

E_{V}\longrightarrow\Omega^{W}E_{V\oplus W}

are weak
G
-equivalences when
V^{G}\neq 0
and is connective if the negative homotopy groups of its fixed point spectra are all zero.

Definition 9.11
.

A
Segal machine
is a functor
\mathbb{S}_{G}\colon\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}_{\ast}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Sp}_{G}
together with a natural map of
G
-spaces

\nu\colon{\mathcal{X}}({\bf 1})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{S}_{G}{\mathcal{X}})_{0}

such that the following properties hold when
{\mathcal{X}}
is a special
\mathscr{F}
-
G
-space in
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathscr{T}
.

(i)

\mathbb{S}_{G}{\mathcal{X}}
is a connective positive
\Omega
-
G
-spectrum.

(ii)

The composite of
\nu
with the adjoint structure map

(\mathbb{S}_{G}{\mathcal{X}})_{0}\longrightarrow\Omega^{V}(\mathbb{S}_{G}{\mathcal{X}})_{V}

is a group completion for all
V
such that
V^{G}\neq 0
.

Remark 9.12
.

It is equivalent to replace general
V
by
V=\mathbb{R}
in (ii).

The notion of a group completion of a Hopf
G
-space is defined as a group completion on all fixed point maps (see
[
13
, Definition 1.9]
). The nonequivariant construction of the Segal machine was introduced in
[
47
]
. The equivariant construction is due to Shimakawa
[
48
]
, who started from an unpublished version that is also due to Segal. It is given a self-contained modernized treatment in
[
39
]
. A multiplicative version is given in
[
15
]
. We refer the reader to those sources for details.

From now on, we set
\mathbb{S}_{G}
to be the Segal machine from
[
39
]
, which is lax monoidal by
[
15
]
.
We could just as well use the equivalent symmetric monoidal version from
[
15
]
, but that would not be of any benefit since we lost symmetry with the multifunctor

\mathrm{St}_{\mathscr{F}_{G}}
. Moreover, using the machine from
[
39
]
will be convenient in
Section 10
, where we will use several results from
[
39
]
.
We repeat that we mostly treat the Segal machine
\mathbb{S}_{G}
as a black box. The only detail from
[
39
]
that we will need to make explicit is a partial description of the construction that allows us to define the natural map
\nu\colon{\mathcal{X}}(1)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{S}_{G}{\mathcal{X}})_{0}
required in
Definition 9.11
. That will be given where it is used in
Section 10
.

We now restate and prove
Theorem A
.

Theorem 9.13
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a chaotic
E_{\infty}

G
-operad in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U})
. The functor

(9.14)

\mathbb{K}_{G}=\mathbb{S}_{G}\circ B\circ\mathrm{St}_{\mathscr{F}_{G}}\circ\zeta_{G}^{*}\circ\mathbb{R}_{G}\colon{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Sp}_{G}.

from
(1.2)
extends to a multifunctor

\mathbb{K}_{G}\colon\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Mult}}(\mathbf{Sp}_{G}).

For an
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
{\eus{A}}\in{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}}
,
\mathbb{K}_{G}{\eus{A}}
is a connective positive
\Omega
-
G
-spectrum
with a group completion map

B{\eus{A}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{V}(\mathbb{K}_{G}{\eus{A}})_{V}

for all
V
such that
V^{G}\neq 0
.

Proof.

By
Corollary 6.25
,
Theorem 7.13
,
Theorem 8.12
,
Proposition 9.5
, and
[
15
, Section 5.2]
,
\mathbb{K}_{G}
is a composition of multifunctors and is thus a multifunctor.
When
{\eus{A}}
is in
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}}
,
B\mathbb{T}{\eus{A}}
is in
\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}G\mathscr{T}
, and we claim that it is special. That will imply the second statement.
Since
\mathbb{T}{\eus{A}}
is level
G
-equivalent to
\mathbb{R}_{G}{\eus{A}}
, by
Theorem 8.1
, the claim follows from the fact that
B
takes equivalences of
G
-categories to
homotopy equivalences of
G
-spaces and commutes with
\mathbb{R}_{G}
, in the sense that
B\mathbb{R}_{G}\cong\mathbb{R}_{G}^{top}B
.
∎

9.3.
The identification of objects in
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}}

When the operad
{\mathcal{O}}
and an
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
{\eus{A}}
are topologically discrete, in the sense that they are categories internal to
G\text{Set}
,
{\eus{A}}
is in
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}}
since all of our categorical constructions retain discreteness and the geometric realization of a based simplicial set is nondegenerately based. We show here that many topologically non-trivial examples, such as those that appear in
Section 10
, are also in
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}}
.

We require the following definition. Nonequivariantly, its use goes back at least to Milnor’s classical paper
[
40
]
, and it was studied in more detail by Dyer and Eilenberg
[
7
]
and later Lewis
[
26
]
. Details of equivariant cofibrations are in
[
2
, Section A.2]
.

Definition 9.15
.

A
G
-space
X
is
G
-locally equiconnected
(
G
-LEC for short) if the diagonal map
\Delta\colon X\longrightarrow X\times X
is a
G
-cofibration.

Examples of
G
-LEC
G
-spaces include
G
-CW-complexes
[
7
,
26
]
. Every basepoint of a
G
-LEC
G
-space is nondegenerate
[
7
, Corollary II.8]
.
The following lemma gives sufficient conditions for the classifying space of a chaotic category to be nondegenerately based.

Lemma 9.16
.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U}_{\ast})
is chaotic and that
\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{C}}
is
G
-LEC. Then
B{\mathcal{C}}
has a nondegenerate basepoint.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{C}}
is
G
-LEC and
{\mathcal{C}}
is chaotic, the nerve of
{\mathcal{C}}
is levelwise
G
-LEC.
Then
B{\mathcal{C}}
is
G
-LEC by
[
26
, Corollary 2.4(b)]
, and in particular it has a nondegenerate basepoint.
∎

We also need the following two general results about
G
-LEC
G
-spaces.

Lemma 9.17
.

Let
X
be a
G
-LEC based
G
-space and

\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
be a finite based
G
-set. Then
X^{\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}}=\underline{G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}}_{*}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha},X)
is
G
-LEC.

Proof.

The
G
-space
X^{\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}}
can be viewed as the restriction along the homomorphism
G\longrightarrow G\wr\Sigma_{n}
of the
G\wr\Sigma_{n}
-space
X^{n}
.
It then follows from
[
2
, Proposition A.2.6]
that
X^{\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}}
is
G
-LEC.
∎

Lemma 9.18
.

Let
H
be a subgroup of
G
, and let
Y
be an
H
-LEC space. Then
G\times_{H}Y
is
G
-LEC.

Proof.

The diagonal on
G\times_{H}Y
factors as

\textstyle{G\times_{H}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\Delta}
\textstyle{(G\times_{H}Y)\times(G\times_{H}Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(G\times G)\times_{H\times H}(Y\times Y)}
\textstyle{G\times_{H}(Y\times Y).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta\times\mathrm{id}}

The map
\mathrm{id}\times\Delta
is the induction from
H
to
G
of the
H
-cofibration
\Delta_{Y}
, and it follows that it is a
G
-cofibration. On the other hand, we claim that the map
\Delta\times\mathrm{id}
is the inclusion of a coproduct summand and is therefore a
G
-cofibration. To see this, note that the subset
\{(g,gh)\mid g\in G,h\in H\}\subset G\times G
is a
(G,H\times H)
-invariant subset, and it is precisely the image under the right
(H\times H)
-action of
\Delta(G)\subset G\times G
. Since
G
is discrete, it follows that we may decompose
G\times G
as a
(G,H\times H)
-equivariant disjoint union of this subset and its complement. Crossing with
Y\times Y
and passing to
(H\times H)
-orbits gives a
G
-equivariant decomposition of
(G\times G)\times_{H\times H}(Y\times Y)
into the image of
\Delta\times\mathrm{id}
and its complement.
∎

In the remainder of this section, we let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an operad in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}})
, and
{\eus{A}}
be an
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra.

Proposition 9.19
.

If
\mathbf{Ob}{\eus{A}}
is
G
-LEC and has a disjoint basepoint, then
{\eus{A}}
is in
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}}
.

Proof.

We first prove that each object
G
-space
\mathbb{T}{\eus{A}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
of
\mathbb{T}{\eus{A}}
is
G
-LEC. Write
{\mathcal{W}}
for
\zeta_{G}^{*}\mathbb{R}_{G}{\eus{A}}
, so that
\mathbb{T}{\eus{A}}=\mathrm{St}_{\mathscr{F}_{G}}{\mathcal{W}}
. The
G
-space
\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{W}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
can be identified with
(\mathbf{Ob}{\eus{A}})^{\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}}
, and is thus
G
-LEC by
Lemma 9.17
. Moreover, it has a disjoint basepoint. Recall from
Theorem 8.1
that

\mathbf{Ob}\mathrm{St}_{\mathscr{F}_{G}}{\mathcal{W}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})=\bigvee_{\mathbf{k}^{\beta}}\mathscr{F}_{G}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta},\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})\wedge\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{W}}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta}).

Since
\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{W}}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta})
is
G
-LEC, it follows that
\mathscr{F}_{G}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta},\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})\wedge\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{W}}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta})
is
G
-LEC.
Since the basepoint of
\mathscr{F}_{G}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta},\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})\wedge\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{W}}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta})
is disjoint, the infinite wedge is in fact an infinite disjoint union, with an adjoined disjoint basepoint.
Since an arbitrary coproduct of
G
-LEC
G
-spaces is again
G
-LEC,
\mathbf{Ob}\mathbb{T}{\eus{A}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
is
G
-LEC.

In the proof of
Theorem 8.1
, we defined a pseudomorphism

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 35.01895pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-35.01895pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{m\colon\mathbb{T}{\eus{A}}=\mathrm{St}_{\mathscr{F}_{G}}{\mathcal{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.01895pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 59.01895pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{W}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

each of whose components is an equivalence of categories.
Let
{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})\subset\mathbb{T}{\eus{A}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
be the subcategory
{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})=m_{\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}}^{-1}(\ast)
. Since
m_{\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}}
is an equivalence of categories,
{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
is equivalent to the trivial category and is therefore chaotic.
The fact that the basepoint splits off of
\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{W}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
implies that
\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
splits off from
\mathbf{Ob}\mathbb{T}{\eus{A}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
and is therefore
G
-LEC since
\mathbf{Ob}\mathbb{T}{\eus{A}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
is
G
-LEC.
Since the basepoint of
B\mathbb{T}{\eus{A}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
lies in
B{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
and
B{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
has a nondegenerate basepoint by
Lemma 9.16
, this gives the conclusion.
∎

The following example will be used in
Theorem 10.1
.

Remark 9.20
.

We embed
G\mathcal{U}
in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U})
by regarding an unbased
G
-space
X
as an object of
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U})
with
X
as both the object and the morphism
G
-space and with the source, target, identity and composition maps all the identity. Similarly, we regard
X_{+}
as an object of
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U}_{\ast})
. The free

{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra generated by
X_{+}
is the disjoint union of the categories
{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times_{\Sigma_{j}}X^{j}
with base object
\ast
the
0
th term.

Proposition 9.21
.

If
X\in G\mathcal{U}
is
G
-LEC and
\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{O}}(j)
is a (discrete) free
\Sigma_{j}
-set for each
j
, then
\mathbb{O}_{+}(X)
is in
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}}
.

Proof.

By
Proposition 9.19
, it suffices to show that
\mathbf{Ob}\mathbb{O}_{+}(X)
is
G
-LEC. This holds if each
G
-space

\mathbf{Ob}\Big({\mathcal{O}}(j)\times_{\Sigma_{j}}X^{j}\Big)\cong\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times_{\Sigma_{j}}X^{j}

is
G
-LEC. Since
\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{O}}(j)
is discrete with free
\Sigma_{j}
-action, we can write it as a disjoint union of
(G\times\Sigma_{j})
-sets
(G\times\Sigma_{j})/\Lambda
, where
\Lambda\subset G\times\Sigma_{j}
is a subgroup such that
\Lambda\cap e\times\Sigma_{j}=e
. In other words, the subgroup
\Lambda
is the graph of a homomorphism
\alpha\colon H\longrightarrow\Sigma_{j}
for some subgroup
H\leq G
. For each such
\Lambda
, we have an isomorphism of
G
-spaces

\big((G\times\Sigma_{j})/\Lambda\big)\times_{\Sigma_{j}}X^{j}\cong G\times_{H}X^{j^{\alpha}}.

Since
X
is
G
-LEC,
Lemma 9.17
implies that
X^{j^{\alpha}}
is
H
-LEC. Then by
Lemma 9.18
, we have that
G\times_{H}X^{j^{\alpha}}
is
G
-LEC as wanted.
∎

Consider the category of operators
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}={\mathscr{D}}_{G}({\mathcal{O}})
. The following analogue of
Proposition 9.19
, with
\mathscr{F}_{G}
replaced by
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
, will be needed
in
Section 10.2
.
There we use comparisons between infinite loop space machines
\mathbb{S}_{G}
defined on
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-
G
-spaces and
\mathbb{S}_{G}^{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}}
defined on
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}
-
G
-spaces, where
{\mathscr{D}}^{top}_{G}
is the topological version of
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
, as specified in
Definition 9.2
. The machine
\mathbb{S}_{G}^{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}}
has good properties when its domain is restricted to
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}\text{-}G\mathscr{T}
. Just as for
\mathscr{F}_{G}
, we have categories
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}_{ps}
of strict
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras and pseudomorphisms and a subcategory
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}
of strict
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebras and strict morphisms.
Section 8

specializes to give a strictification functor

\mathrm{St}_{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}}\colon{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}_{ps}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}.

The composite

\mathrm{St}_{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}}\mathbb{R}_{G}\colon{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{G}\text{-}\bf{Alg}

plays a role analogous to that of
\mathbb{T}
in the earlier results of this subsection, and we let
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}\!({\mathscr{D}}_{G})}
be the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}
consisting of those
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras

{\eus{A}}
such that
B\mathrm{St}_{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}}\mathbb{R}_{G}{\eus{A}}
is in
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}\text{-}G\mathscr{T}
.
Thus, by definition,
B\mathrm{St}_{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}}\mathbb{R}_{G}
restricts to a functor
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}\!({\mathscr{D}}_{G})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{G}^{top}\text{-}G\mathscr{T}
.

Proposition 9.22
.

If
\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{O}}(j)
for each
j
and
\mathbf{Ob}{\eus{A}}
are
G
-LEC, and
\mathbf{Ob}{\eus{A}}
has a disjoint basepoint, then
{\eus{A}}
is in
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}\!({\mathscr{D}}_{G})}
.

Proof.

We need a modification of the first step of the proof of
Proposition 9.19
to account for strictification over
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
rather than
\mathscr{F}_{G}
. Writing
{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathbb{R}_{G}{\eus{A}}
, we have

\mathbf{Ob}\mathrm{St}_{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}}{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})=\bigvee_{\mathbf{k}^{\beta}}\mathbf{Ob}{\mathscr{D}}_{G}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta},\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})\wedge\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta}).

But
\mathbf{Ob}{\mathscr{D}}_{G}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta},\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
is a finite coproduct of finite products of
G
-spaces
\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{O}}(j)
, each of which has a disjoint basepoint and is assumed to be
G
-LEC. Therefore
\mathbf{Ob}\mathrm{St}_{{\mathscr{D}}_{G}}{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
is
G
-LEC. The rest of the proof of
Proposition 9.19
goes through unchanged.
∎

The proof of
Proposition 9.21
applies directly to give the following analog.

Proposition 9.23
.

If
X\in G\mathcal{U}
is
G
-LEC and
\mathbf{Ob}{\mathcal{O}}(j)
is a (discrete) free
\Sigma_{j}
-set for each
j
, then
\mathbb{O}_{+}(X)
is in
{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{{\bf Alg}_{\bf ps}^{G\mathscr{T}}\!({\mathscr{D}}_{G})}
.

9.4.
Nondegenerate basepoints and Day convolution

This brief parenthetical section highlights a question that seems to have been overlooked in all previous papers dealing with the use of Day convolution in topology, even nonequivariantly, whether for spectra or for categories of operators. We concentrate on the latter and restrict attention to
\mathscr{F}
, thinking nonequivariantly for simplicity.

Of course, smash products are constructed as quotient spaces
X\times Y/X\vee Y
in
\mathcal{U}_{\ast}
. It is essential to be working in compactly generated spaces since otherwise the smash product is not even associative
[
36
, Theorem 1.7.1]
. It follows from Lillig’s union theorem
[
27
]
that
X\wedge Y
is nondegenerately based if
X
and
Y
are. Therefore both
\mathcal{U}_{\ast}
and its full subcategory
\mathscr{T}
are symmetric monoidal under the smash product. By
Remark 2.15
, we have associated multicategories
\mathbf{Mult}(\mathcal{U}_{*})
and
\mathbf{Mult}(\mathscr{T})
.

An
\mathscr{F}
-space is an (enriched) functor
\mathscr{F}\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{\ast}
and the category
\mathscr{F}
-
\mathcal{U}_{\ast}

of
\mathscr{F}
-spaces is symmetric monoidal under the internal smash product given by Day convolution. By
Remark 2.15
, it also has an associated multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}(\mathscr{F}\text{-}\mathcal{U}_{\ast})
. That can be defined using either the internal smash product as in
Remark 2.15
or using the external smash product as in
Definition 5.15
. These definitions give isomorphic multicategories by the universal property of Day convolution.

Now consider the category
\mathscr{F}
-
\mathscr{T}
of (enriched) functors
\mathscr{F}\longrightarrow\mathscr{T}
. It has been asserted in many places, including our own
[
15
]
, that
\mathscr{F}
-
\mathscr{T}
is symmetric monoidal under the internal smash product. We do not know whether or not that is true, and we believe that it is not. The external smash product

X\barwedge Y\colon\mathscr{F}\wedge\mathscr{F}\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{*}
of functors
X,Y\colon\mathscr{F}\longrightarrow\mathscr{T}
clearly takes values in
\mathscr{T}
, but it does not follow that the internal smash product

X\wedge Y\colon\mathscr{F}\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{*}
takes values in
\mathscr{T}
. That is, we do not believe that Day convolution preserves levelwise nondegeneracy of basepoints. We cannot use the universal property to prove that it does, and we have not succeeded in proving that it does by direct inspection of the construction.

That problem does not affect applications since,
using the external smash product
as in
Definition 5.15
, we have the full submulticategory
\mathbf{Mult}(\mathscr{F}\text{-}\mathscr{T})
of

\mathbf{Mult}(\mathscr{F}\text{-}\mathcal{U}_{\ast})
, whose objects are
levelwise nondegenerately based functors. When we reinterpret
internally
, using Day convolution, we may leave that world. The same holds for the functors from
\mathscr{F}
or, equivariantly,
\mathscr{F}_{G}
to topological
G
-categories that are the focus of this paper.

9.5.
From
{\mathcal{O}}
-transformations to homotopies of maps of
G
-spectra

It is classical that the classifying space functor takes
G
-categories,
G
-functors and
G
-natural transformations to
G
-spaces,
G
-maps, and
G
-homotopies. For the last,
G
-natural transformations are functors
{\eus{C}}\times\mathscr{I}\longrightarrow{\eus{D}}
, where
\mathscr{I}
is the category with two objects
[0]
and
[1]
and one non-identity morphism
[0]\longrightarrow[1]
. For based
G
-categories, based
G
-transformations are given by based
G
-functors
{\eus{C}}\wedge\mathscr{I}_{+}\longrightarrow{\eus{D}}
.

Recall the definition of
{\mathcal{O}}
-transformations from
Definition 2.12
.

Proposition 9.24
.

The functor
\mathbb{K}_{G}
takes
{\mathcal{O}}
-transformations to homotopies of maps of
G
-spectra.

Proof.

We showed in
[
15
, Proposition 6.16]
that the topological Segal machine
\mathbb{S}_{G}
preserves homotopies. If we start with

\mathscr{F}_{G}
-algebras in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U}_{\ast})
, which of course are themselves
G
-functors, then maps between them are
G
-natural transformations and maps between those are
G
-modifications. These are given levelwise by
G
-categories,
G
-functors, and
G
-natural transformations.
Since
B
commutes with products, it takes
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-
G
-algebras in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U}_{\ast})
,
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-functors, and
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-transformations between them to

G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}_{*}
-enriched functors
\mathscr{F}_{G}\longrightarrow\underline{G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}}_{*}
, enriched natural transformations, and homotopies between those.
As
\mathbb{R}_{G}
,
\zeta_{G}^{*}
, and
\mathrm{St}_{\mathscr{F}_{G}}
are all
2
-functors, with
\mathrm{St}_{\mathscr{F}_{G}}
converting pseudostructure to strict structure, their composite takes
{\mathcal{O}}
-transformations to
\mathscr{F}_{G}
-transformations, which are levelwise
G
-natural transformations.
∎

The result above is used in
[
12
, Remark 2.9]
, but it will surely find other uses.

10.
The multiplicative Barratt-Priddy-Quillen Theorem

In this section, we prove
Theorem B
. We begin by producing the transformation
\alpha
in
Section 10.1
. We show that
\alpha_{X}
is a stable equivalence of orthogonal
G
-spectra for all
G
-LEC
G
-spaces
X
in
Section 10.2
and we finish by showing that
\alpha
is monoidal in
Section 10.3
.

10.1.
The construction of
\alpha

We restate and begin the proof of
Theorem B
.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a topologically discrete chaotic
E_{\infty}

G
-operad in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U})
.
Then there is a lax monoidal natural transformation

\alpha\colon\Sigma^{\infty}_{G+}\longrightarrow\mathbb{K}_{G}\mathbb{O}_{+}

of functors
G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Sp}_{G}

such that
\alpha_{X}
is a stable equivalence of orthogonal
G
-spectra for all

G
-LEC
G
-spaces
X
.

Recall that we write
\mathbb{T}
for the composite

\mathbb{T}=\mathrm{St}_{\mathscr{F}_{G}}\circ\zeta_{G}^{*}\circ\mathbb{R}_{G}:{\mathcal{O}}\text{-}{\bf Alg_{ps}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}_{G}\text{-}{\bf Alg},

so that
\mathbb{K}_{G}
is given by
\mathbb{K}_{G}=\mathbb{S}_{G}\circ B\circ\mathbb{T}
. We shall exploit the fact that
\Sigma^{\infty}_{G+}\colon G\mathbf{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Sp}_{G}
is left adjoint to the zeroth

G
-space functor
(-)_{0}
, with basepoint forgotten, to construct
\alpha
.
Therefore,
to define
\alpha:\Sigma^{\infty}_{G+}\longrightarrow\mathbb{K}_{G}\mathbb{O}_{+}
in
Theorem 10.1
, it suffices
to define a map of unbased
G
-spaces

\tilde{\alpha}_{X}:X\longrightarrow{\mathbb{S}}_{G}\big(B\mathbb{T}\mathbb{O}_{+}X\big)_{0}

for each unbased
G
-space
X
.
We define
\tilde{\alpha}_{X}
to be the composite displayed in the diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\alpha}_{X}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{S}}_{G}\big(B\mathbb{T}\mathbb{O}_{+}X\big)_{0}}
\textstyle{BX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B\eta}
\textstyle{B\mathbb{O}_{+}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\big(B\mathbb{R}_{G}\,\mathbb{O}_{+}X\big)(\mathbf{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Bi}
\textstyle{\big(B\mathbb{T}\mathbb{O}_{+}X\big)(\mathbf{1}).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}

Regarding
X
as an object of
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U})
as in
Remark 9.20
, the top left isomorphism is immediate;

\eta
on the left is the unit of the monad
\mathbb{O}_{+}
.
For the bottom left equality, it is true by definition that
{\eus{A}}=(\mathbb{R}_{G}{\eus{A}})(\mathbf{1})
for any
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
{\eus{A}}
in
\mathbf{Cat}(G\mathcal{U})
, such as
{\eus{A}}=\mathbb{O}_{+}X
.
Next, for any strict
{\mathscr{D}}_{G}
-algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}
, such as
{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbb{R}_{G}\mathbb{O}_{+}X
, the map

i\colon{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{1})\longrightarrow\mathrm{St}_{\mathscr{F}_{G}}\zeta_{G}^{*}{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{1})

is given by

{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{1})=\zeta_{G}^{*}{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{1})\xrightarrow{i}\mathrm{St}_{\mathscr{F}_{G}}\zeta_{G}^{*}{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{1}),

where
i
is a component of the unit of the adjunction of
Theorem 8.1
and is an equivalence. Finally,
the map
\nu
is the natural map required by
Definition 9.11
; it will be specified in
Section 10.2
.

The adjoints of the maps
\tilde{\alpha}_{X}
define the natural transformation
\alpha
, and we must verify that
\alpha
is monoidal
and homotopical, the latter meaning that
\alpha
is a stable
G
-equivalence.

10.2.
The proof that
\alpha
is a stable equivalence

We will show that
\alpha_{X}
is an equivalence by comparing it with the equivariant Barratt-Priddy-Quillen equivalence for the equivariant operadic machine proven in
[

]
and the equivalence between the equivariant operadic and Segal machines proven in

. Consider the following diagram of

,

. The dotted arrows signify zig-zags of maps.

(10.2)

\textstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}_{G}X_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(1)

to be equal to the 2-cell

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(j)\times\prod\limits_{i=1}^{j}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{i})\times{\mathcal{O}}(\ell)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(\ell)\times{\mathcal{O}}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(k\ell).}

Here, in the first
2
-cell above,
D_{\ell,k_{*}}
is the distributivity isomorphism specified in the following definition.

Definition 11.1
.

Given
\ell
,
j
, and
k_{1},\dots,k_{j}
, let
k=\sum k_{i}
and define

D_{\ell,k_{*}}\colon\underline{\ell}\otimes(\underline{k_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus\underline{k_{j}})\longrightarrow(\underline{\ell}\otimes\underline{k_{1}})\oplus\dots\oplus(\underline{\ell}\otimes\underline{k_{j}})

in the bipermutative category
\Sigma
. It is given explicitly as the permutation

D_{\ell,k_{*}}=\left(\tau_{k_{1},\ell}\oplus\dots\oplus\tau_{k_{j},\ell}\right)\tau_{\ell,k}.

Alternatively, using the operad structure on
\mathbf{Assoc}
, we can identify
D_{\ell,k_{*}}
as

D_{\ell,k_{*}}=\gamma(\tau_{\ell,j};\Delta^{\ell}(e_{k_{1}},\dots,e_{k_{j}})).

It is the permutation in
\Sigma_{\ell k}
that permutes blocks of sizes
k_{1},\dots,k_{j},\dots,k_{1},\dots,k_{j}
according to
\tau_{\ell,j}
.

Remark 11.2
.

In the language of Corner and Gurski
[
5
, Theorem 4.6]
, axiom (
ii
)
states that we require pseudo-commutativity structures to be symmetric. Axiom (
iii
) is an equivariance axiom that is necessary in order for
\alpha
to induce a map at the monad level (
Proposition 3.22
), but which was unfortunately omitted in
[
5
]
.

Axiom (
iv
) encodes the compatibility of
\alpha
with operadic composition, and is given in
[
5
, Theorem 4.4]
.
Unpacking axiom (
iv
), it states that
given
x\in{\mathcal{O}}(j)
,
y_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}(k_{i})
, and
z\in{\mathcal{O}}(\ell)
, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\gamma(x;y_{1}\circledast z,\dots,y_{j}\circledast z){\big(\tau_{\ell,k_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus\tau_{\ell,k_{j}}\big)D_{\ell,k_{*}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\gamma(x;y_{1}\circledast z,\dots,y_{j}\circledast z){\tau_{\ell,k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\gamma\Big(x;(y_{1}\circledast z)\tau_{\ell,k_{1}},\dots,(y_{j}\circledast z)\tau_{\ell,k_{j}}\Big){D_{\ell,k_{*}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma(\mathrm{id};\alpha,\dots,\alpha){D_{\ell,k_{*}}}}
\textstyle{\gamma(x;z\circledast y_{1},\dots,z\circledast y_{j}){D_{\ell,k_{*}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Big(\gamma(x;y_{1},\dots,y_{j})\circledast z\Big){\tau_{\ell,k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\gamma(x\circledast z;\Delta^{\ell}(y_{1}),\dots,\Delta^{\ell}(y_{j})){D_{\ell,k_{*}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\gamma((x\circledast z)\tau_{\ell,j};\Delta^{\ell}(y_{1},\dots,y_{j}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma(\alpha;\mathrm{id},\dots,\mathrm{id})}
\textstyle{\gamma(z\circledast x;\Delta^{\ell}(y_{1},\dots,y_{j}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{z\circledast\gamma(x;y_{1},\dots,y_{j}).}

This formulation is closer to what is stated in
[
5
]
.
We can summarize it by the equation

(\alpha_{x,z}\circledast\mathrm{id}_{y})\circ[(\mathrm{id}_{x}\circledast\alpha_{y,z})D_{\ell,k_{*}}]=\alpha_{xy,z}.

This axiom plays the role of a hexagon axiom in our context. (There is a second axiom
relating
\alpha
to operadic composition in
[
5
, Theorem 4.4]
, but the two axioms are equivalent in the presence of the symmetry axiom (
ii
).)

11.2.
Coherence axioms for
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{O}})

Here we return to the diagram
(3.15)
, which defines the invertible
\mathcal{V}
-transformations
\delta_{i}
central to
Definition 3.14
, and complete that definition.
In axioms (
ii
) and (
iv
), we will use
the shorthand notation
{\mathcal{A}}_{i,j}
for the product
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i+1}\times\dots\times{\mathcal{A}}_{j}.

The map
\mu
appearing in (
iv
) and (
v
) is defined
immediately following the axioms.
The axioms require certain pasting diagrams to be equal, and in some cases, it will not be immediately apparent that the boundaries are equal; we address that issue after the axioms as well.
We use
\times
rather than
\wedge
throughout since the
{\mathcal{O}}(n)
are unbased and their algebras are defined using powers rather than smash powers.

(i)

(Unit Object) The
\mathcal{V}
-transformation
\delta_{i}(0)
is the identity.

\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{\rule{0.0pt}{6.02777pt}\mathrm{id}\times\theta(m)^{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{B}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta(n)}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathrm{id}\times\delta_{i}(m)^{n}\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\delta_{j}(n)\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(nm)\times_{\Sigma_{nm}}({\mathcal{A}}_{i}\times{\mathcal{A}}_{j})^{nm}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times F^{nm}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(nm)\times_{\Sigma_{nm}}{\mathcal{B}}^{nm}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta(nm)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}.}

These axioms require explanation. They encode the idea that whenever the
\delta_{i}

combine to give two transformations with the same source functor and the same target functor, both with target
category
{\mathcal{B}}
, then they are equal. There is an implicit coherence theorem saying that the diagrams we display generate
all others. In all of our diagrams, the interior subdiagrams unoccupied by a
2
-cell commute either by
the definition of an operad or by a naturality diagram.

Axioms (
i
) and (
ii
) give the compatibilities with basepoints and equivariance necessary for these multimorphisms to give rise to multimorphisms of
\mathbb{O}
-algebras, as defined by Hyland and Power
[
18
]
.
In (
ii
), we must check that the source and target functors of the two diagrams displayed are equal. The target
functors agree trivially. The source functors agree by the Equivariance Axiom for
\theta
, the naturality of
s_{i}
,
and the fact that
F^{n}\circ\rho=\rho\circ F^{n}
.
Axiom (
iii
) corresponds to the Operadic Identity Axiom and
requires no explanation.

In (
iv
), we define the map
\mu

to be the map that shuffles the operad variables to the left and applies the structure map of the operad in those variables.
The source and target functors of the two diagrams agree by the compatibility axioms for
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras.

In (
v
),
we abuse notation and again write
\mu
for the effect of passing to orbits from the
\mu
used above.
Here the target functors of the first and third diagrams agree trivially but their source functors do not; their left
vertical composites differ. After pasting the second diagram to the third, the source
functors of the first diagram and the composite agree.
We note that passage to
\Sigma_{mn}
-orbits in the second diagram is essential, as in
Proposition 3.22
;
without that, the pseudo-commutativity isomorphism
\alpha_{m,n}
would not mediate between
its source and target functors.

Remark 11.3
.

These axioms imply further compatibilities of the
\delta_{i}
with the unit object 0. In particular, it follows that the component of
\delta_{i}(n)
at an object

(a_{1},\dots,(x;a_{i,1},\dots,a_{i,n}),\dots,a_{k})

is
\mathrm{id}_{0}
if either
a_{j}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
is
0
for some
j\neq i
or all coordinates
a_{i,r}
of the
i
th object
a_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{i}^{n}
are
0
.
Moreover, for
1\leq r\leq n
, the

-cell

\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{1,i-1}\times{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i}^{n-1}\times{\mathcal{A}}_{i+1,k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times(\sigma_{r}\times\mathrm{id})\times\mathrm{id}}

\scriptstyle{s_{1}}

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 16.38895pt\delta_{i}(n)}

is equal to the

\scriptstyle{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{3.01389pt}}\mathrm{id}\times\theta(n-1)\times\mathrm{id}}

12.
The pseudo-commutativity of
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathcal{O}})

We prove
Theorem 5.12
here. Thus let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a pseudo-commutative
operad in
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})

and
{\mathscr{D}}={\mathscr{D}}({\mathcal{O}})
the associated category of operators, as in
Proposition 5.6
.
We must construct a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctor
\textstyle{\circledast\colon{\mathscr{D}}\wedge{\mathscr{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}}
and prove that it gives a pseudo-commutative structure. For the sake of clarity, we work with
\times
rather than
\wedge
in this section; the statements about
\Pi
build in basepoint conditions that imply that all the constructions descend to the smash product. We break the proof into several parts. Recall that for a morphism
\phi\colon\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}
of
\mathscr{F}
and
1\leq j\leq n
, we write
\phi_{j}=|\phi^{-1}(j)|
.

When restricted to
\Pi
,
\circledast
must be
\wedge
. Thus, on objects,
\mathbf{m}\circledast\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p}
. At the level of Hom categories, the map

\circledast\colon{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p},\mathbf{n}\mathbf{q})

sends the summand in the source labeled by
\phi\colon\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}
and
\psi\colon\mathbf{p}\longrightarrow\mathbf{q}
to the one labeled by
\phi\wedge\psi\colon\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}\mathbf{q}
in the target. Therein, the
\mathcal{V}
-functor

\prod\limits_{1\leq j\leq n}{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\times\prod\limits_{1\leq k\leq q}{\mathcal{O}}(\psi_{k})\longrightarrow\prod\limits_{1\leq\ell\leq nq}{\mathcal{O}}((\phi\wedge\psi)_{\ell}),

is such that its projection onto the
\ell
th factor is given by first projecting onto

{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\times{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{k})
, where
\ell
maps to the pair
(j,k)
under the lexicographic ordering of
\mathbf{n}\wedge\mathbf{q}
, and then applying the pairing
\circledast
of
{\mathcal{O}}
.
The definition makes sense since

(\phi\wedge\psi)_{\ell}=|(\phi\wedge\psi)^{-1}(\ell)|=|\phi^{-1}(j)||\psi^{-1}(k)|=\phi_{j}\psi_{k}.

It is immediate from the definition that
\circledast
restricts to
\wedge
on
\Pi
(along
\iota
) and projects to
\wedge
on
\mathscr{F}
(via
\xi
), as required.

To complete the construction of the
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctor
\circledast
, we must prove the following result, which is the
heart of the proof that
{\mathscr{D}}
is pseudo-commutative.

Proposition 12.1
.

The following diagram of
\mathcal{V}
-functors relating
\circledast
to composition
commutes up to an invertible
\mathcal{V}
-transformation
\vartheta
.

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\times\circledast}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times t\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\ \vartheta}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{nr},\mathbf{ps})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{mq},\mathbf{nr})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ\times\circ}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{s})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{mq},\mathbf{ps})}

The collection of such
\mathcal{V}
-transformations descends to the smash product and makes
\textstyle{\circledast\colon{\mathscr{D}}\wedge{\mathscr{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}}
into a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctor.

Proof.

The essential combinatorial claim is that the pseudo-commutativity isomorphisms

\alpha
of
{\mathcal{O}}
from
Definition 3.10
assemble to give the required invertible
\mathcal{V}
-transformations
\vartheta
.
This is not obvious since the
\alpha
give maps that are not obviously relevant to the diagram.
The strategy is to express the results of the source and target of the diagram
in such a way that the invertible
\mathcal{V}
-transformation
between them becomes obvious. In the following equations, we will use the associativity and equivariance formulas from the definition of an operad to massage the two composites into comparable form.

Since
\Pi
and
\mathscr{F}
are permutative categories regarded as
\mathcal{V}
-
2
-categories,
the diagram clearly commutes when
{\mathscr{D}}=\Pi
or
{\mathscr{D}}=\mathscr{F}
. That is, fixing morphisms

\psi\colon\mathbf{n}\longrightarrow\mathbf{p},\ \ \nu\colon\mathbf{r}\longrightarrow\mathbf{s},\ \ \phi\colon\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n},\ \text{and}\ \mu\colon\mathbf{q}\longrightarrow\mathbf{r}

in
\mathscr{F}
, we have

(12.2)

(\psi\wedge\nu)\circ(\phi\wedge\mu)=(\psi\circ\phi)\wedge(\nu\circ\mu).

Thus, for the summand labeled by our fixed morphisms
\psi
,
\nu
,

\phi
, and
\mu
in
\mathscr{F}
, the clockwise and counterclockwise directions land in the same summand of the target. It follows that it suffices to restrict the diagram to these summands.
Let

1\leq j\leq n,\ \ 1\leq k\leq p,\ \ 1\leq h\leq r,\ \text{and}\ 1\leq i\leq s.

Looking at the definitions of the composition
\circ
of
{\mathscr{D}}
in
(5.5)

and of
\circledast
, we see that to chase the diagram
starting at the top left with

\prod_{k}{\mathcal{O}}(\psi_{k})\times\prod_{i}{\mathcal{O}}(\nu_{i})\times\prod_{j}{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\times\prod_{h}{\mathcal{O}}(\mu_{h}),

it suffices to consider its projection onto each term of the product in the target. Thus, we fix
k
and
i
,
project to

{\mathcal{O}}(\psi_{k})\times{\mathcal{O}}(\nu_{i})\times\prod_{j\in\psi^{-1}(k)}{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\times\prod_{h\in\nu^{-1}(i)}{\mathcal{O}}(\mu_{h}),

and then chase. Going around both ways, we land in the term
{\mathcal{O}}\Big(\big((\psi\wedge\nu)\circ(\phi\wedge\mu)\big)_{\ell}\Big)
of the target, where
(k,i)\in\mathbf{p}\wedge\mathbf{s}
corresponds to
\ell\in\mathbf{p}\mathbf{s}
under lexicographical ordering.

Writing in terms of elements to better
apply formulas rather than chase large diagrams, let

c\in{\mathcal{O}}(\psi_{k}),\ \ a\in{\mathcal{O}}(\nu_{i}),\ \ d_{j}\in{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j}),\ \text{and}\ b_{h}\in{\mathcal{O}}(\mu_{h}),

and recall the permutations
\rho
defined in
(5.5)
.
Going clockwise, the tuple
(c,a,\prod_{j}d_{j},\prod_{h}b_{h})
gets sent to

(12.3)

\textstyle\gamma\Big(c\circledast a;\prod\limits_{(\psi\wedge\nu)(j,h)=(k,i)}d_{j}\circledast b_{h}\Big)\,\rho_{(k,i)}(\psi\wedge\nu,\phi\wedge\mu),

and going counterclockwise, it gets sent to

(12.4)

{\textstyle\Big(\gamma(c;\prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}d_{j})\rho_{k}(\psi,\phi)\Big)\circledast\Big(\gamma(a;\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}b_{h})\rho_{i}(\nu,\mu)\Big).}

In what follows, we use the notation
\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\ell}\!(x)
to denote the
\ell
-tuple
(x,\dots,x)
. Using the definition of
\circledast
, the associativity from the definition of an operad twice and abbreviating

\rho_{(k,i)}(\psi\wedge\nu,\phi\wedge\mu)=\rho_{(k,i)}
, we identify the expression
(12.3)
as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip-10.00002pt\textstyle\gamma\Big(c\circledast a;\prod\limits_{(\psi\wedge\nu)(j,h)=(k,i)}d_{j}\circledast b_{h}\Big)\,\rho_{(k,i)}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\gamma\Big(\gamma(c;\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\psi_{k}}\!(a));\prod\limits_{(\psi\wedge\nu)(j,h)=(k,i)}\gamma(d_{j};\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(b_{h}))\Big)\,\rho_{(k,i)}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\gamma\Big(c;\ \prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}\gamma\big(a;\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}\gamma(d_{j};\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(b_{h}))\big)\Big)\rho_{(k,i)}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\gamma\Big(c;\ \prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}\gamma\big(\gamma(a;\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\nu_{i}}\!(d_{j}));\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(b_{h})\big)\Big)\rho_{(k,i)}

(12.5)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\gamma\Big(c;\ \prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}\gamma\big(a\circledast d_{j};\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(b_{h})\big)\Big)\rho_{(k,i)}.

We abbreviate

\rho_{k}(\psi,\phi)=\rho_{k}
and
\rho_{i}(\nu,\mu)=\rho_{i}
.
Using
Remark 3.7
and the associativity axiom of the operad, we identify the expression
(12.4)
as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip-10.00002pt\textstyle\Big(\gamma(c;\prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}d_{j})\rho_{k}\Big)\circledast\Big(\gamma(a;\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}b_{h})\rho_{i}\Big)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\Big(\gamma(c;\prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}d_{j})\circledast\gamma(a;\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}b_{h})\Big)(\rho_{k}\otimes\rho_{i})

\displaystyle\textstyle=\gamma\Big(\gamma(c;\prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}d_{j});\,\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle(\psi\circ\phi)_{k}}\!(\gamma(a;\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}b_{h}))\Big)(\rho_{k}\otimes\rho_{i})

\displaystyle\textstyle=\gamma\Big(c;\ \prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}\gamma(d_{j};\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(\gamma(a;\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}b_{h})))\Big)(\rho_{k}\otimes\rho_{i})

\displaystyle\textstyle=\gamma\Big(c;\ \prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}\gamma\big(\gamma(d_{j};\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(a));\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}b_{h})\big)\Big)(\rho_{k}\otimes\rho_{i})

(12.6)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\gamma\Big(c;\ \prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}\gamma\big(d_{j}\circledast a;\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}b_{h})\big)\Big)(\rho_{k}\otimes\rho_{i})

The similarity between the reinterpretations
(12.5)
and
(12.6)
of
(12.3)
and
(12.4)
is clear.
We use the
\alpha
’s from
Definition 3.10
to build an invertible
\mathcal{V}
-transformation from the expression
(12.5)

to the expression
(12.6)
. This will specify
\vartheta
. For legibility, we omit
the indices on the
\alpha
’s. From the pseudo-commutativity of
{\mathcal{O}}
, for fixed
i
and
j
we have an isomorphism

\textstyle{\alpha\colon(a\circledast d_{j})\tau_{\phi_{j},\nu_{i}}\longrightarrow d_{j}\circledast a,}

where
\tau_{\phi_{j},\nu_{i}}
is as in
Definition 3.8
. It induces the second isomorphism in the composite

\textstyle{\gamma\Big(a\circledast d_{j};\,\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(b_{h})\Big)D_{\phi_{j},\mu_{*}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\gamma\Big((a\circledast d_{j})\tau_{\phi_{j},\nu_{i}};\,{\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}b_{h})}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma(\alpha;\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(\mathrm{id}))}
\textstyle{\gamma\Big(d_{j}\circledast a;\,{\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}b_{h})}\Big).}

The first equality is given by the equivariance formula for
\gamma
; the permutation

D_{\phi_{j},\mu_{*}}
is as in
Definition 11.1
, with
\ast
running through the set
\nu^{-1}(i)
. This is precisely the permutation of

\phi_{j}\cdot\sum_{\nu(h)=i}\mu_{h}=\phi_{j}\cdot(\nu\circ\mu)_{i}

elements which permutes according to
\tau_{\phi_{j},\nu_{i}}
the
\phi_{j}\cdot\nu_{i}
blocks of lengths given by the tuple
\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(\prod_{\nu(h)=i}\mu_{h})
.

By applying
\gamma(c;-)
to the product over
j\in\psi^{-1}(k)
of the composite above, we obtain the second isomorphism in the composite

(12.7)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 114.74994pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\cr\cr\cr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-114.74994pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\gamma\Big(c;\,\prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}\gamma\big(a\circledast d_{j};\,\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(b_{h})\big)\Big)\Big(\bigoplus\limits_{\psi(j)=k}D_{\phi_{j},\mu_{*}}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern-94.7872pt\raise-36.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\gamma\Big(c;\prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}\gamma\big(a\circledast d_{j};\,\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(b_{h})\big)D_{\phi_{j},\mu_{*}}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern
