a;\,{\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}b_{h})}\big)\Big).}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

The equality is again given by the equivariance of
\gamma
.

A straightforward computation, which we omit, gives that

\rho_{(k,i)}=\Big(\bigoplus_{\psi(j)=k}D_{\phi_{j},\mu_{*}}\Big)\cdot(\rho_{k}\otimes\rho_{i}).

Therefore, multiplying the isomorphism
(12.7)
by
\rho_{k}\otimes\rho_{i}
yields the
desired isomorphism

\textstyle{\gamma\Big(c;\,\prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}\gamma\big(a\circledast d_{j};\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(b_{h})\big)\Big)\rho_{(k,i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma\left(\mathrm{id};\prod\limits\gamma(\alpha;\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(\mathrm{id}))\right)(\rho_{k}\otimes\rho_{i})}
\textstyle{\gamma\Big(c;\ \prod\limits_{\psi(j)=k}\gamma\big(d_{j}\circledast a;\,{\Delta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\phi_{j}}\!(\prod\limits_{\nu(h)=i}b_{h})}\big)\Big)(\rho_{k}\otimes\rho_{i})}

from
(12.3)
to
(12.4)
. Interpreting the proof diagrammatically shows that we have an invertible
\mathcal{V}
-transformation
\vartheta
as needed. Compatibility of
\vartheta
with identity morphisms and the fact that it descends to the smash product follow from
Lemma 12.10
below. Compatibility with composition in
{\mathscr{D}}\wedge{\mathscr{D}}
is tedious to check, but boils down to repeated use of Axiom (
iv
) of
Definition 3.10
. This completes the proof that
\textstyle{\circledast\colon{\mathscr{D}}\wedge{\mathscr{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}}
is a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctor.
∎

Condition (
2
) of
Definition 5.7
holds as a result of the following more general lemma.

Lemma 12.8
.

After restricting the domain of the functors in the diagram of
Proposition 12.1
to

{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s})\times\Pi(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{mq},\mathbf{ns})

or

{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})\times\Pi(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\times{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{mq},\mathbf{ns}),

the transformation
\vartheta
is the identity.

Proof.

We use the notation of the construction of
\vartheta
in
Proposition 12.1
. The first restriction is the case when the

d_{j}

are all
\ast
or
\mathds{1}
, and the second restriction is the case when
e
is
\ast
or
\mathds{1}
.
The key is that if either
e
or
d_{j}
is
\ast
, then

\textstyle{\alpha\colon(e\circledast d_{j})\tau_{\phi_{j},\nu_{i}}\longrightarrow d_{j}\circledast e}

is the identity map of
\ast
. Similarly, by
Definition 3.10
(
i
), if
e=\mathds{1}
, then
\alpha
is the identity map
of
d_{j}
, whereas if
d_{j}=\mathds{1}
, then
\alpha
is the identity map of
e
.
∎

Note that this result in particular implies that as a
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctor,
\circledast
restricts to
\wedge
on
\Pi
.

Lemma 12.9
.

The
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctor
\circledast
is strictly associative in the sense that the following diagram of
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctors commutes.

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}^{\wedge 3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\wedge\circledast}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\wedge\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}^{\wedge 2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}^{\wedge 2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}}

Proof.

Since this is an equality of
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctors, we need to check equality of the level of assignments on objects,
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-functors on morphisms, and pseudofunctoriality constraints. The equality of assignments on objects follows from the strict associativity of
\wedge
in
\Pi
. The equality at the level of morphisms follows from the strict associativity of the pairing of
{\mathcal{O}}
(
Proposition 3.3
). For each composite, the pseudofunctoriality constraint is given by a pasting of two instances of the
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformation
\vartheta
of
Proposition 12.1
. As such they are each constructed using instances of
\alpha
and the operadic structure. After some standard simplifications, the equality of these constraints reduces to axiom (
iv
) of
Definition 3.10
.
∎

Lemma 12.10
.

The
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctor
\textstyle{\circledast\colon{\mathscr{D}}\wedge{\mathscr{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}}
has a symmetry
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudotransformation

\tau
such that the strict monoidal
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-
2
-functors
\iota\colon\Pi\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}
and

\xi\colon{\mathscr{D}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}
preserve the symmetry.

Proof.

The
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-pseudofunctors
\circledast
and
\textstyle{\circledast\circ t\colon{\mathscr{D}}\wedge{\mathscr{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}}

we are comparing have the same object functions. Given objects
\mathbf{m}
and
\mathbf{p}
, the

1
-cell component of
\tau
is given by the permutation
\tau_{m,p}\colon\mathbf{mp}\longrightarrow\mathbf{pm}
of
Definition 3.8
,
thought of as a morphism in
\Pi\subset{\mathscr{D}}
.
We need invertible
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformations

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\wedge{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circledast\circ t}
\scriptstyle{\circledast}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hat{\tau}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{p}\mathbf{m},\mathbf{q}\mathbf{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\tau_{m,p})^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p},\mathbf{n}\mathbf{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\tau_{n,q})_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m}\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\mathbf{n}).}

As in the previous proofs, we can restrict to the components of
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
and

{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
, which are indexed on morphisms
\phi\colon\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}
and
\psi\colon\mathbf{p}\longrightarrow\mathbf{q}

of
\mathscr{F}
.
Note that both maps send the component of
(\phi,\psi)
in the source to that of

(\psi\wedge\phi)\circ\tau_{m,p}=\tau_{n,q}\circ(\phi\wedge\psi)

in the target (see
(3.9)
). We thus fix such
\phi
and
\psi
and start with

\prod_{j}{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})\times\prod_{k}{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{k})
,
where
1\leq j\leq n
and
1\leq k\leq q
. Again for simplicity we work with elements

c_{j}\in{\mathcal{O}}(\phi_{j})
and
d_{k}\in{\mathcal{O}}(\psi_{k})
.

Considering permutations as morphisms of
\Pi\subset{\mathscr{D}}
and using the definition of composition in

{\mathscr{D}}
, we find that
the clockwise composite sends
\big((c_{1},\dots,c_{n}),(d_{1},\dots,d_{q})\big)
to

{\textstyle\prod\limits_{j,k}}(c_{j}\circledast d_{k})\tau_{\psi_{j},\phi_{k}},

and the counterclockwise composite sends it to

{\textstyle\prod\limits_{j,k}}(d_{k}\circledast c_{j}),

with both products ordered in reverse lexicographical order.

Applying a product of maps
\alpha
gives the invertible
\mathcal{V}_{\ast}
-transformation
\hat{\tau}
indicated in the diagram. Note that, similar to
Lemma 12.8
, we have that the 2-cell
\hat{\tau}
is the identity when either copy of
{\mathscr{D}}
is restricted to
\Pi
.
We leave to the reader the verification of compatibility with composition, and axioms (
i
) and (
ii
) of
Definition 4.30
.
∎
